## G: G truck–go home

### G truck
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SARGESON</b> ? A horse float; lorry or railway wagon for carrying (race-) horses (?f. GG-truck). <i>(1940,Man & His Wife, A,1944 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> We'd go in a G truck with the horses. <i>(1946,That Summer,32</i>) [NOTES: `Pair of Socks']

>  <b>SARGESON</b> G truck: railway truck for transporting horses. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,300</i>) [NOTES: Pearson Glossary]

<b>1</b> <i>exclam.</i> (Rural) a command to a sheep-dog to set out on a run.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> G'way: initial command to set a dog on its run. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,38</i>)

 <i>abbr.</i> The General Assembly Library, now part of the National Library.

### GABA
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Gay Auckland Business Association [ORIGIN: acronym, GABA started 1984]

>  Welcome to Gaba The Gay Auckland Business Association was started in 1984 as an organisation of gay men and lesbian women in business and the professions. <i>(Dec 15, 2005,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.gaba.org.nz/]

>  <b>GABA</b> The Gay Auckland Business Association was started in 1984 as an organisation of gay men and lesbian women in business and the professions. It's [sic] basic aims are: to promote and strengthen the business and professional interests of our members; to build a gay and lesbian business community; to provide opportunities for interaction amongst gay men and women in the professions and in business; and to provide funding and assistance to the gay community through requests [sic] to the GABA Charitable Trust. For further information visit www.gaba.co.nz <i>(2008,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.heroicgardens.org.nz/sponsors.php]

>  One of the main events of the GABA and the Auckland Gay and Lesbian community year is set to go as we prepare for the Fantastic, Fabulous and Full of Fun Xmas Auction. <i>(Nov 16, 2005,Express (Gay & Lesbian Express),24</i>)

>  Don't forget that we are on the hunt for old gaba photos that we can put on the new website (which is just weeks away). <i>(May 21, 2008,Express (Gay & Lesbian Express),9</i>)

### gaff
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  In some parts the eels were "bobbed" or "gaffed" out by the hundredweight. <i>(Sep 11, 1890,Otago Witness,28</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to land fish, esp. eels, using a stick with an iron hook

>  In some parts the eels were "bobbed" or "gaffed" out by the hundredweight. <i>(Sep 11, 1890,Otago Witness,28</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Severe criticism.

>  Hasty Mr Hastie, a new `Tabernacle' parson, of Dunedin, has been saying things about Dunedin's notorious drunkenness and some Dunedinites wince under the gaff. <i>(Jul 21, 1906,NZ Truth,57/1/7</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a stick with an iron hook for landing fish, esp. eels

>  Now, I do remember, as kids, some brazen Hawke's Bay brats at the creek armed with such an instrument and a gaff - a nasty-looking stick with a big fish-hook bound to it. <i>(Jun 21, 2003,Dominion Post,F6</i>)

### gafu

>  <b>WILSON</b> `I remember that as fast as I got a squad on its feet to move, it was shot down again.' ... `What a gafu!' exclaims Corry. <i>Ibid.</i> 146 A bloody gafu again; silent night advance ... hell! <i>(1951,Brave Company,48</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> GAFU God Almighty fuck-up; the WWII NZ Second Expeditionary Force variation of the RAF's SNAFU (Situation Normal All Fucked Up), usually a reference to decisions that landed fighting men in impossible situations. [Jim Henderson] <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,48</i>) [NOTES: WWII]

### gahnia
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Gahnia (?)sieberiana</i>, saw edge. A tall sharp-leaved adventive grass associated with kauri forests in Northland. >  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Gahnia procera</i> GAHNIA ... Large green tussocks up to 1 m tall. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,368</i>)

>  I entered a world of.. gahnia, that most despised of razor-sharp weeds. <i>(1996,Hunting for DOC,16</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Gahnia xanthocarpa. </i>

### gain the bottom wire of the fence
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to marry the daughter of the boss; to become a member of a farmer's family.

>  <b>SUTHERLAND & TAYLOR</b> 'You ought to grab her. That way you'd gain the bottom wire of the fence.''How do you mean? 'She's the boss's daughter and he's taken quite a shine to himself ...' <i>(1981,Sunrise on Hikurangi,58</i>)

>  If a whare boy (single shepherd) is lucky, he might gain the bottom wire of the fence (marry the farmer's daughter). <i>(Nov 3, 2002,Dominion,C6</i>) [NOTES: ??Dominion Nov 33 C6]

### gaiting
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [used earlier elsewhere, but sp. in New Zealand for flax drying] the forming of gaits, stacks or wigwams of flax fibres to enhance drying in drying paddocks.

>  <b>KERR</b> Out of the tanks, a machine feeds the retted straw...on to the drying paddocks. There it half-dries, when it is ready for 'gaiting,' the wet underside becoming the outside of the 'gait.' They are these small wigwams one sees in rows in the drying fields, and are another stage of drying. <i>(1976,From the Beginning: chronicles of a county,292</i>)

### Gal Blacks
 
 <i>n.</i> a short-lived proposed name for the women's rugby union team that was given the name Black Ferns

>  She plays on the wing for the Black Ferns, as the New Zealand women's rugby team is now called after rejecting labels like Gal Blacks, Elle Blacks and She Bs. <i>(Aug 1998,Mana,28</i>)

>  The New Zealand Women's Team, or "the Gal Blacks" as the media referred to them, were indeed very scary in Amsterdam. <i>(Jun 1999,Metro,89</i>)

>  What may have been the shortest-lived New Zealand usage on record hit the headlines in May last year at the time of the women's rugbv union World Cup played in the Netherlands. As the Kiwi women rucked and ran their way with stunning ease to the title of world champions, they began to be described, by journalists initially, as the Gal Blacks, a name rather cleverly punning on (loosely rhyming with) that of their hitherto more illustrious male counterparts the All Blacks, whose classic black uniform they too wear.No sooner was the women's triumph complete, however, than it was made clear to all and sundry that the name bestowed on them was unacceptable both to the team itself and the New Zealand Rugby Football Union which administers both male and female rugby. NZRFU chief executive David Moffatt was reported as rejecting the name as inappropriate on the grounds that 'gal is an American slang term and certainly not what we want to call our team'. A replacement was promised, and in the meantime most journalists and others accepted the name's red-carding. It was sighted however on the cover of the July 1998 issue of New Zealand Sport Monthly ('Where now for the Gal Blacks?'), whose editorial lauding the team's success said the name would have to do until something better came along... What is striking about all these current brand names is that without exception they derive directly or indirectly from the prototypical All Black name. Even Gal Blacks, admittedly Americanised, was paying homage to its mana. <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,2(1) : 5</i>)

### Gala
 
 <i>n.</i> Variety of apple

>  If the inhabitants of Ryde, Australiam, can openm a Granny Smith Memorial Park to celbrate their wodl-famous applle variety, then surely Greytown hcan do the same with the Gala, which was developed there in the 1920s by J H Kidd, who cossed Golden Delicious with his own Kidd's Orange Red, itself a cross of Cox's Orange and Red Delicious. He first releaed it in 1934, and it was introduced to the US in 1981. <i>(Mar 27, 2004,Dominion Post,E6</i>)

>  A new hybrid apple, Gala,.. has been approved by the Apple and Pear Board. <i>(May 1966,Orchardist NZ,106</i>)

>  Gala - this is a New Zealand apple which is a cross between a Golden Delicious and a Kidds Orange Red. <i>(Apr 1969,Orchardist NZ,99</i>)

>  Gala is proving to be a very profitable apple variety for the early export period. <i>(Jun 1974,Orchardist NZ,135</i>)

>  Gala is uniquely a New Zealand apple and at present is grown commercially only in New Zealand. <i>(Nov 1974,Orchardist NZ,350</i>)

>  Gala, New Zealand's own apple, certainly continues to excite other producers and consumers round the world. <i>(Mar 1978,Orchardist NZ,74</i>)

>  <b>BATEMAN</b> Gala... A sweet dessert apple with thin skin and crisp flesh. <i>(1986,Illustrated Encycl. of NZ,45</i>)

>  GALA. An apple with a pink blush on a yellow background, it was discovered by Mr Kidd of Greytown in the 1930's. A cross between Kidd's Orange Red and Golden Delicious, it is a very sweet, crisp eating apple. <i>(1991,New Zealand's Best Apple Recipes,12</i>)

>  Let's put a green belt round the fertile valleys where the Galas, Granny Smiths and Albany Surprise grow so well. <i>(Jan 1992,Metro,53</i>)

### galley-side
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a piece of canvas used to form the wall of a tent.

>  <b>CURTIS</b> While the field officer was with us he got us to lay out galley-sides (long narrow pieces of canvas) set up on sticks about a metre off the ground at each end of the clearing. <i>(1997,Murphy's Law and the Hunter,54</i>)

>  <b>BLAKE</b> Some distance up the Dane River I came across the framework of an old tent camp, and as it was far enough upstream to work from, I decided to lash my gally-side [sic] to it and make use of it for the next few days. <i>(1999,Trappers, Dogs 'n' Deer,93</i>)

>  <b>BLAKE</b> Glossary galley-side - piece of canvas used for wall of tent <i>(1999,Trappers, Dogs 'n' Deer,261</i>)

### Gallipoli
 
 <i>n.</i> As a name symbolic of the whole of the Gallipoli peninsula and operation.

>  It is a very nice feeling to be leaving Gallipoli behind ... <i>(Aug 22, 1915,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1915 Aug 27 G. Harper in 1978 Letters from Gunner 7/516 27]

>  <b>DUFF</b> Not to feel the importance of the occasion is like walking unmoved through a full cemetery or hearing nothing but the sea on Gallipoli beach. <i>(Oct 31, 1953,Shepherd's Calendar,149,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> [cp ANZAC] We thought [Cape Helles] was going to be something of a holiday from the Anzac sector. By then, well into May, I was no longer elated about war, especially Gallipolli. <i>Ibid.</i> 97 ... a sickening sideshow of World War I which is remembered, where recalled elsewhere at all, under the name of Gallipoli. For many New Zealanders, though, Gallipoli has remained as potent a placename as any in the outside world, a name as familiar as Taupo or Timaru, but one which chills the heart. <i>(1988,Voices of Gallipoli,37</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> Attrib. or in combin., esp. Gallipoli Day, an early ephemeral name for Anzac day.

>  GALLIPOLI DAY [title] The keeping of Gallipoli Day, originally called Anzac Day, is now an institution on Cairo. It was instituted by the Anzacs, but there were other heroes besides the Australian and New Zealand troops on Gallipoli; and it is in memory of all who fell on the spot, or who were later carried, after terrible sufferings, to their last resting place in Old Cairo, that we now keep the Day. <i>(May 15, 1918,Kia-ora Coo-ee,3/8</i>)

>  Dawn Parades on Anzac day, Hart always felt uneasy bout the R.S.A. As a Gallipoli veteran he found it difficult to relate to the majority of the R.S.A., who had joined up after 1915. <i>(1988,Great Adventure,1</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  So popular with the troops were these one-woman brothels on Cambridge Terrace that the area had become known as Gallipoli. <i>(Apr 1996,NZ Journal of History,15</i>) [NOTES: See also Truth, 19 Feb., & 20 May 1916]

### Gallipoli rose
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Soldiers who survived the battle at Gallipoli in World War I came home with seeds of a little bush growing wild there that covered itself in rose-like flowers. They called it the Gallipoli rose... The plant's real name is <i>cistus</i>, covering about 20 evergreen species native to dry, stony soils throughout the Mediterranean. Common names include rock rose and sun rose. Another common name is gum cistus because the leaves of some species are sticky to the touch. <i>(Jun 5, 1999,Dominion,19</i>)

### gallows
 
 <i>n.</i> A wooden frame, usu. of two uprights and a crosspiece, on which carcases are hung to be dressed. See also Spanish gallows. [(farmers') also as meat gallows]

>  Fisher and Loon returned bringing gallows posts. <i>(Feb 28, 1855,St Leonard's Journal</i>)

>  On Oct 17 1859 we read `Erected small gallows.' <i>(Oct 17, 1859,Puketoi Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1859 Oct 17 Puketoi Station Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 79]

>  putting up gallows at stockyard <i>(Oct 3, 1860,Puketoi Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: [Hocken TS]]

>  We put three loads of furse and two of straw under the gallows ... <i>(Aug 5, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,51,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> I am glad we have chosen to build our house here instead of at the homestead two miles off; for I like to be removed from the immediate neighbourhood of all the work of the station, especially from that of the `gallows,' - a high wooden frame from which the carcases of the butchered sheep dangle; ... <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,64,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ELWELL</b> The gallows [was] a pair of poles set upright with a third pole across the top, to which in the centre was fastened a hook. From this hook ... there was suspended the carcase of a sheep. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,82</i>)

>  <b>D'AVIGDOR</b> Soon the Keas would watch for a sheep to be killed, and then at once fly to the wooden gallows to which the carcase is hung. <i>(1888,Antipodean Notes,75</i>)

>  <b>KNOX</b> ... Investigation showed that ... the parrots had come at night to the gallows, where the herdsmen hung the carcases of slaughtered sheep. ... It seems that the attacks on the sheep began ... after the parrots had first tasted mutton at the meat-gallows. <i>(1888,Boy Travellers,276</i>)

>  <b>POTTS</b> [The kea] has discovered the out-stations of some of the back-country settlers. Of course, every station has a meat-gallows. The gallows ... <i>(1882,Out in the Open,84</i>) [NOTES: 1895 NZ Reader 84 quoting 1882 T.H. Potts Out in the Open [Central Otago]]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Gallows, n. Explained in quotation. Common at all stations, where of course the butchering is done on the premises. <i>(1898,Austral English,158</i>) [NOTES: sole quot. 1866 Lady B SL 64]

>  [The bird] was attracted by a carcase, newly killed, suspended from a gallows ... <i>(1907,TrNZI,39/82</i>)

>  gallows n. a framework on which animals are cut up when killed on a station for food purposes. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,5</i>)

>  Gallows. Two right posts, perhaps 10 feet high, with a strong rail across the top. When a sheep is half skinned it is pulled half way up until the skinning and dressing are finished. It is then pulled up to the top to hang out of the way of dogs. ([1874 Lady Barker Station Life] p.64; [1898 Morris Austral-English]) <i>(Oct 21, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Glossary]

>  <b>McNEISH</b> After a long time he appeared to notice the carcases rigged to the gallows and he mumbled `Scab, eh? Well who'd a-knowed it for scab?' <i>(1970,Mackenzie,128</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> ... the drover became a butcher overnight, with a `gallows' between two silver beech trees. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,118</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> [caption to a 4-pole, roofed structure] The gallows. [Lake Haupiri Station, Canterbury] <i>(1982,Historic Sheep Stations NI,46</i>) [NOTES: (OED various fig. uses: in this sense quotes Lady B 1866; AC1847)]

>  Repairing stockyard with old gallows poles. <i>(Oct 1, 1864,Puketoi Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: [Hocken TS]]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>REED</b> [the gum-buyer's] steel-yard is suspended from a rough gallows [to weigh the gum]. <i>(1948,Gumdigger, The,93</i>)

### gally
 
 <i>v.</i> To frighten; to daze. Often as a pa ppl aj gallied (spg galeyed, gallid), occas. transf. to humans. [Whaling Hist.]

>  Saw a large Whale was in chase best part of the day, but she was so Galeyed we had no chance of fastening. <i>(May 21, 1838,Piraki Log,73,ed. Anson 1911 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ED JOLLIE</b> He [Captain Toms] was a very old whaler ... he had had most of the principal bones of his body broken by whales flukes but when I met him he was what the whalers called `gallid' or afraid even of the sight of a whale. I expect his nerves were hurt when his bones were broken. <i>(1873,Reminiscences,25</i>) [NOTES: MS]

>  <b>GRADY</b> [glossary] Gallied - An alarmed or frightened whale. These often take off at high speed, or sound for extended lengths of time. Often gallied whales will adapt zig-zag swimming patterns under water, No Perano chasers were equipped with sonar, which meant that the erratic movements of a whale underwater were much more difficult for them to follow. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,227</i>)

>  <b>MacNAB</b> They so gallied the poor brutes [of whales] that those which survived forsook their long established rendezvous to seek new grounds for food, and to bring forth their young in peace. <i>(1908,Old Whaling Days,288</i>)

### gamble
 
 <i>n.</i> A `spreader' (phs the more usual term of butchers and farmers) for keeping the hocks of hung carcases apart.

>  Gamble. When a sheep is pulled up on the gallows by a rope, the rope is tied to a g[amble], which is a piece of wood or iron, the ends of which are stuck through the sheep's hocks. The gamble also serves to keep the sheep's hind legs stretched. (C.) <i>(Oct 21, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss.]

>  <b>MORRIESON</b> ... he strode briskly, a quarter-fiiled sack of skids and gambols [94] over his shoulder, along the siding, stepping over the bright blotches of blood with the hovering blow-flie's outside the `glory hole'. <i>(1976,Pallet on the Floor,93</i>)

>  <b>C. CORNE</b> gamble, gambol, gambel - gambrel, for hanging carcasses on: microcarpa (= macrocarpa) is a similar deformation <i>(Oct 14, 1983,Letter</i>) [NOTES: Auckland]

>  My father already had the gallows ready, complete with ropes, pulleys and a 'gamble'. <i>(Jan 26, 1956,Weekly News,39</i>)

### gambrel
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [gamble](g-go.md#gamble))

### game as Ned Kelly
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Mrs Archer, game as Ned Kelly, dancing about wearing nothing but three pot lids. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,175</i>)

>  <b>BRUCE</b> They told him the position and the danger involved but he was as game as Ned Kelly. <i>(1977,Life in the Hinterland of the South Island,35</i>)

### Game Industry Board
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a promotion and marketing board for deer velvet and venison. (See [GIB](g-go.md#gib))

>  Early indications from deer farmers show that the Game Industry Board should at least remain or at best have more powers. <i>(Oct 8, 1998,NZ Farmer,7</i>)

### game of two halves
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  What constitutes a "worthy" rugby supporter? Knowing who played fullback in the 1953 Test series against Albania? The ability to say "it's a game of two halves" with a straight face? <i>(May 28, 2003,Capital Times,3</i>)

>  At the end of the day I think they will be going home to play a game of two halves. <i>(Jun 8, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),15</i>)

### game-recovery
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The commercial game-recovery operators are unconventional. <i>(Nov 2002,Forest & Bird,306/2</i>)

### gamebreaker
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Upper Hutt desperately needs somebody with a bit of a game-breaker to fit the image of the town. <i>(Oct 1, 2001,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  It could add weight to the argument the Warriors will chase Australians who can fill key positions around the New Zealand gamebreakers. <i>(May 9, 1999,Sunday Star Times,B9</i>)

>  Though they have a good pack, a lack of game-breakers in the backs will be their downfall. <i>(Feb 20, 2006,Dominion Post,D3</i>)

>  Although the paucity of genuine stars and game-breakers in British union would seem in theory to give league most of the aces in the northern hemisphere, Lindsay knows that many of his league stars already have a union or southern hemisphere background. <i>(Jun 22, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35</i>)

>  The big Auckland pack laid on a wealth of possession for the first five-eighth Carlos Spencer, who impressed with the skill and cheek that have made him one of rugby's best gamebreakers. <i>(Aug 27, 2000,Sunday Star Times,B4</i>)

>  He makes mistakes, but does brilliant things and is a gamebreaker. <i>(Oct 13, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35</i>)

### gang
 
 <i>n.</i> In NZ, as elsewhere (OED 1627), the main word for a `team of workmen, a working team' associated here mainly with shearers, watersiders, road-workers, railway-workers; in children's use for a close-knit group with a leader and usu. a meeting-place; and more recently with bikie and other groups: hence, in attrib. use or combin. gang-bang (OED2 1953), gang-bash, gangie, gang-shag (OED2 1927), gang-slash, gang-splash, gangster. [Also shearing gang, contract gang etc.]

>  <b>PHARAZYN</b> Harbuca, Wyroa and the whole gang called <i>(1846,Journal,34</i>)

>  As children we looked forward from one shearing's end to the beginning of the next, when the gang of twenty or so Maori men, women, and children, with the inevitable kuri ... would come stringing into the station. [fn kuri] Dog. <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,51,1988 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> I stole a box. Soon I had a trolley. I stole boards from the timber yard and built a wooden hut for my gang in the backyard. <i>(1934,Children of the Poor,146,1949 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> In towns the same sort of group fighting was usually known as gang-fighting. Different gangs took different sides in the Paper Chase. (1900; Riwaka) <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,78</i>)

>  I tell myself that it is amazing that I did not end up in a gang, and, thus, in prison. Yet ... and yet, with the gang would come a sense of belonging... <i>(2003,Huia 5: Contemp. Maori Fiction,192</i>)

### gang grubbing
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the using of gangs of grubbers to eradicate nassella tussock.

>  <b>LOVELL-SMITH</b> Because of the board's success in reducing tussock numbers, the gang-grubbing hours required to control the weed dropped steadily. The peak year for the board's gang-grubbing work was in 1968 when 121,000 man-hours of actual grubbing covered 80,000 hectares .. it charged farmers 50 to 55 per cent of the labour cost of gang grubbing ... [gang-grubbing] <i>(2000,Hurunui Heritage,60</i>)

>  The decree of ranger-grubbing was to be left to the discretion of the ranger, this discretion to be consistent with the present policy of gang grubbing. <i>(Jan 1968,Straight Furrow,8</i>)

### gang patch
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He believed the attack was linked to a "puerile" fight over possession of a gang patch stolen a few months ago. <i>(Dec 10, 1999,Dominion,1</i>)

>  Edwards turned up to court yesterday with friends, some wearing Mongrel mob gang patches. <i>(Nov 18, 2005,Dominion Post,A6</i>)

>  "I didn't allow any gang patches in my house." <i>(Nov 9, 2005,Horowhenua-Kapiti Chronicle,4</i>) [NOTES: Horowhenua-Kapiti Chronicle]

### gang shearing
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the shearing of sheep by organised teams of shearers and shedhands, usually under contract.

>  <b>BUCKINGHAM</b> Gang shearing becomes more usual all the time. <i>(1969,From Hethersett to Quarry Hills,41</i>)

### gang splash
 
 <i>n.</i> = gang rape

>  She ... heard a boy ... say: `How about a gang splash?' She knew that this expression meant a number of boys having intercourse with a girl. <i>(May 8, 1968,Dominion,4/5</i>)

>  [see citation at GANGIE n] <i>(Mar 30, 1971,NZ Truth,7</i>)

### gang-bashing
 
 <i>n.</i> gang-rape

>  ... I started pashing her up ... Hutana who said he had been a Black Power boy for a month, said another gang member came up and told him there would be no gang-bashing in his garden. <i>(Feb 14, 1978,NZ Truth,3</i>)

### gang-slash
 
 <i>n.</i> var. of gang-splash [see also have a slash at (something), `have a go at, an attempt at'. ca1969 Used in Wellington.]

### ganger
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sp. use] a person in charge of a shearing gang.

>  <b>HALL</b> And when Alan, the ganger at the other end of the board, saw me walking away from Paul and caught my eye, I knew very well what he must have been suspecting. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,9</i>)

>  <b>BARNES</b> The Ganger is the boss and the Ringer is the fastest shearer. <i>(1987,Country Calendar (TVNZ),118</i>)

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> Mr Williams was already talking to the ganger, or shearing boss. <i>(1994,I Was So Lucky,25</i>)

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> Dick and his brother-in-law Tubby Dewer, who was the ganger, showed me several tally books which indicated without a doubt that it was normal not to shear all day as they inevitably ran out of sheep. <i>(1994,I Was So Lucky,235</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> .. several of the family joined the gang as shearers or fleecos and Bill took over the role as 'ganger.' <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,46</i>)

>  He says convenience is probably a factor, as the ganger or farmer doesn't have to walk down and count the sheep themselves. <i>(2002,Wool Grower,Winter 18</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a supervisor of station-hands, stockmen, shepherds or ploughmen.

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> There were also anything from 6 to 12 contract teams working in addition to our own 20 ploughmen, with a head ploughman (ganger) ... <i>(1940,Te Waimate,93</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Ganger (in charge, above ) fairly permanent station hands. <i>(1993,Station Country I,124</i>)

>  a person in charge of a team of rural workers - a ganger. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### gangie
 
 <i>n.</i> A gang-rape.

>  He explained he had heard the word `gangy' called out as he returned to the clubhouse. ... He understood the word meant that `a gang of boys was going out with one or two sheilas to have a group intercourse.' <i>(May 9, 1968,Dominion,5/3</i>)

>  What is a `gangie' or `gangbang'? - A gang of jokers on to one girl. It could be rape, but might not be. I took it as rape. <i>(May 5, 1970,NZ Truth,25</i>)

>  Gary (Ngatai) said they wanted to have a `gangie' on us [girls]. ... Sergeant: What is a `gangie'? - It's a line-up. It's a group of males having intercourse with a girl or girls one after the other. Isn't that what happened? - Yes = but I wouldn't call that a gang splash. <i>(Mar 30, 1971,NZ Truth,7</i>)

### gangmower
 
 <i>n.</i> a set of mowers (usu. towed by a tractor) that work as a unit [cf.OED gangplough, gangsaw, gangloom]

>  Gangmower Operator / Groundsperson... This position will involve gangmowing, assisting with preparation and development, and litter control at sportsgrounds in the Napier area... Essential requirements are... Experience with tractors and gangmowers. <i>(Aug 1, 1998,Napier Daily Telegraph,6</i>) [NOTES: check page]

### gangsta lean
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  The "gangsta lean" of Tau Henare and his fellow warriors sends cold shivers down the spine of both the "old" and the "new" National Party. <i>(Aug 1, 1997,Independent (Auckland),13</i>)

### gangster
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  An 11-man and one woman jury had found Jones guilty of the rape of a girl picked up from a city bus terminal on the night of Friday, April 23, and taken to a house on the Ellerslie Main Highway where she was forced to `pull a train' of Hell's Angels and Storm Troopers gangsters. <i>(Jul 20, 1971,NZ Truth,14</i>)

>  It surprises me when she tells me how she met her gangster husband at a party when she was still in the 4th form ... <i>(May 1986,More Magazine,26</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

### gannet
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Sula bassana serrator</i> (Gray), a white goose-sized seabird with a yellow crown and black-tipped wings, which plunges on fish from a height; =takapu.

>  ... myself in a boat ... killing chiefly Gannets or Solan Geese so like the European ones that [449] they are hardly distinguishable from them. <i>(Dec 24, 1769,Banks Journal,1/448,1962 ed.</i>)

>  ... myself in a boat ... killing chiefly Gannets or Solan Geese so like the European ones that [449] they are hardly distinguishable from them. <i>(Dec 24, 1769,Banks Journal,1/448,1962 ed.</i>)

>  Sea and water fowles of all sorts are however in no great plenty, those known in Europe are Ducks, Shags, Gannets and gulls all of which were eat by us and found exceeding good, ... <i>(Mar 31, 1770,Cook's Journals,1/276,1955 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> We shot a Gannet [[ed. fn] Australian gannet <i>Sula bassana serrator</i> Gray, 1843.] & an Albatross. <i>(Apr 6, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/247</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> We found ... oyster-catchers or sea-pies, ... gannets, gulls, pinguins, and others of the aquatic kind. <i>(1770,Voyage Round the World,1/158</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> Flocks of gannets and petrels flying round the ship in all directions. Hope to see New Zealand tomorrow. <i>(Dec 14, 1814,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/76,1817 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),69</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>POLACK</b> Masses of sandstone ... gave this part of the coast [near Waipo[u]a] a barren ... appearance; large detached masses of black rocks lined the shore, on which the gannet, curlew, pelican, and gulls ... sat perched ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/72</i>)

>  <b>COLENSO</b> ... several fine Gannets ( Sula, sp.) ... [the natives] call it Takupu. <i>(1844,Excursion in the Northern Island of NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Colenso Excursion Northern Island in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 54]

>  DYSPORUS SERRATOR, Banks. Gannet. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/78</i>) [NOTES: Potts Birds]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 146. DYSPORUS SERRATOR. Banks. Gannet. Takapu. <i>Sula capensis</i>. LICHT. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,49</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> DYSPORUS SERRATOR. - Gannet (<i>Takapu</i>). <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/99</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Gannet</i>, ... the English name for the Solan Goose and its tribe. The Australian species are- The spices in New Zealand is <i>Dysporus serrator</i>, Grey; Maori name, <i>Takapu. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,158</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Takapu, ... [Morus] serrator, gannet. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,438</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> GANNET (<i>Morus serrator</i>), Takapu of the Maoris, is common around the coasts of the North Island. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,79</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Gannet - takapu, <i>Sula bassana serrator </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  He found the whole of the nesting ground thickly covered with birds, showing that practically most of the gannets had returned. <i>(Aug 31, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,17</i>)

>  Ornithological science tells us not to call our large ocean birds "albatross," but "mollymauks," and our Tarakoekoea albatross a "gannet". <i>(Sep 14, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,18</i>)

>  [The Moriori] called our gannet "hopo-tchar," which the Maori changed to "toroa-a-tara". <i>(Sep 14, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,18</i>)

>  On being placed before me, this voluptuous creation was pounced upon by the gannets at my table. <i>(Sep 1, 1922,Evening Post,29</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  We passed dive-bombing Australasian gannets, king shags and fur seals loitering around a salmon farm. <i>(Jul 11, 2004,Sunday  Star Times: Escape,10</i>)

>  76. <i>Sula bassana serrator</i> Gray, 1843 <i>Austral[as]ian Gannet (Takapu) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,31,1980 ed.</i>)

>  Australasian Gannet <i>Sula bassana serrator</i> GRAY, 1843 ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Takapu, Australian gannet, solan goose. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,110</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> SULA [DYSPORUS] SERRATOR. (AUSTRALIAN GANNET.) ... <i>Native name[s].-</i> Takapu, [, Takupu, and Toroa-haoika]. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,325,1888 2nd edn 2/177 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> AUSTRALIAN GANNET <i>Sula serrator</i> Local name: <i>Takapu. </i> <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,60</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> But the only really familiar member of this order [Pelecaniformes] ... is the gannet [258] ..., which seems to be the same as the Australian <i>Sula serrator</i> ... <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,257</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> selfish or greedy person; one who takes without asking [f. nautical use]

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> GANNET (n) Selfish or greedy person. One who takes without asking. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,248</i>)

>  Even the stoical Bill English fell upon Clark's mashed credibility like a gannet on a gut truck. <i>(Apr 27, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

### gaol ports
 
 <i>n.</i> ????????

>  Mary Collins is a hard-faced, Christchurch batter, who serves as a sort of human football for the local Harvey Duffs, who shoot her between the gaol ports [?pun on goal posts] of the booby hatch on the slightest provocation. <i>(Mar 13, 1909,NZ Truth,195/1/7</i>)

### gap
 
 <i>n.</i> Esp. across the gap, the Tasman Sea. See also other side.

>  [Maggie Barry after reading the NZ stock exchange reports moves to the Australian stock exchange report with] Meanwhile, across the gap, BHP shares are ... <i>(May 8, 1987,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: Good Morning NZ]



 
 <i>n.</i> To make a gap or notch in the edge of a cutting implement.

>  Woolshed terms. ... To gap shears is to make (accidentally) a g. in the edge. <i>(Jan 27, 1934,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  ... and from down in the shed vibrates Dad's angry voice `Who's gapped my axe?' <i>(Jan 6, 1958,Straight Furrow,17(6):34</i>)

### gap one's axe
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to annoy one [as in having one's axe stuck in a log.]

>  <b>GORDON</b> As foretelling the future of a young pup is impossible, one of those you put in someone else's hands could easily go on to win a major title and wouldn't that gap your axe! <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,56</i>)

### GAQ
 
<b>1</b> <i>abbr.</i> (Rural) a grade of beef, good average quality.

>  <b>BARTON</b> GAQ ox, heifer or cow = Good average quality Carcasses of this grade are usually of the beef breeds and their crosses although a small proportion of carcasses of dairy-bred cattle can be graded GAQ. Carcasses in the GAQ grade are adequately covered with fat ... <i>(1972,Sheepfarming Annual 1972,71</i>)

### gar
 
 <i>n.</i> gar n -s [short for garfish] : any of various fishes that have an elongate body resembling that of a pike and long and narrow jaws: as b : any of various halfbeaks of Australia and New Zealand

>  gar n -s [short for garfish] : any of various fishes that have an elongate body resembling that of a pike and long and narrow jaws: as b : any of various halfbeaks of Australia and New Zealand <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,935</i>)

### garage
 
 <i>n.</i> an area where a continuous miner is operating

>  In an area where a continuous miner was operating - called a "window" in North Island mining parlance but, curiously, a "garage" by South Island miners - Pastar pointed to an indentation in the wall the size of a breadboard. <i>(Jul 2004,NZ Geographic,No. 68/43</i>)

### garage bar
 
 <i>n.</i> an unlicensed liquor bar (set up in a garage)

>  Garage-bar charge laid ... Garage bars have been springing up around the country since legislation introduced last year outlawed smoking in licensed bars and clubs. <i>(Oct 28, 2005,NZ Herald,A5</i>)

### garbo
 
 <i>n.</i> A rubbishman. [[AND 1. Garbage-man 1953; 2. rubbish 1970.] [??rare OMIT]]

>  Radical girls like to have a tame Polynesian boyfriend ..., preferably a Varsity drop-out, but never an ordinary garbo or busdriver. <i>(Jun 7, 1972,Salient,16</i>)

>  They were usually a woman d'un certain age, with the exception of a garbo (as in collector, not Greta) and the hilarious thing was that when Eddie asked the caller on the line if they remembered Bergo or Turpso or whatever those former TV cheesies were, the old ducks nearly all said, "Who?" <i>(Aug 23, 2002,Dominion,B9</i>)

### Garden City
 
 <i>n.</i> a nickname for Christchurch.

>  <b>PICARD</b> Got loose in the garden city of Maoriland ... Here's Cathedral Square, the centre of the City of the Plains ... <i>(1906,Some Ups and Downs in NZ&A,34</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The city of Christchurch with its parks and avenues and the quiet Avon piddling through the town. The Garden City they call it. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,24</i>)

>  <b>HASCOMBE</b> Was this the Garden City? Was this the place so very much like Home? <i>(1969,Down and Almost Under,26</i>)

>  [Advt Caption] Christchurch, The Garden City <i>(Oct 31, 1972,NZ Truth,36 3/4</i>)

>  <b>TUCKER</b> ... my parents were informed that the Wellington Cricket Association would provide the necessary funds to get me to Christchurch with the team but that I had to be responsible for my hotel accommodation whilst in the garden city. <i>(1978,Thoroughbreds Are My Life,8</i>)

>  Christchurch United was founded in 1970, the result of an amalgamation of several senior [soccer] clubs in the Garden City. ... <i>(Apr 27, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  Mr Taylor said schoolgirls made sexual overtures to the garden city's bus drivers, but he didn't believe such behaviour was any more common than in the past. <i>(May 31, 1987,Sunday News,3</i>)

>  It has become fashionable to refer to Christchurch as the City Beautiful and the Garden City, without doing very much to preserve or increase the boasted beauties of street and square and park. <i>(Dec 27, 1924,Christchurch Star,6</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [huckleberry](hom-hyp.md#huckleberry))

>  <i>garden huckleberry [s] Solanum nigrum</i> L. var. <i>guineese</i> L. <i>Ibid.</i> 39 <i>huckleberry, garden [s] Solanum nigrum</i> L. var. <i>guineense</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,28</i>)

### garden nasturtium
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Tropaeolum majus</i>

>  <i>garden nasturtium [s] Tropaeolum majus</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,28</i>)

### garden of New Zealand
 
 <i>phr.</i> (historical) early occasional name for Taranaki

>  I said [to Mr Ironside, Port Underwood Missionary], `Do you know anything about Taranaki, New Plymouth Settlement?' `Yes, well; I have travelled over it, and found it the garden of New Zealand.' <i>(1841,Letter</i>) [NOTES: >1841 Letter from emigrant `Amelia Thompson' in 1940 Establishment of New Plymouth 148]

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> Notwithstanding all the deficiencies of the harbour, the settlers at New Plymouth are quite satisfied with their locale, and the circumstance of their possessing a country, which by every one is allowed to be the `Garden of New Zealand,' has prompted them to exertion and enterprise. <i>(1842,New Zealand (Heaphy),99</i>)

>  <b>MAJORIBANKS</b> This district [Taranaki], from being the finest in the country, has been called the `Garden of New Zealand'. <i>(1845,Travels in NZ,67</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The Taranaki country ... is commonly described by all who have seen it as `the Garden of New Zealand'. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,273</i>)

>  <b>EARP</b> The latter [Taranaki], or New Plymouth district has, from its great productiveness, the beauty of its climate, and the facility which it presents for easy cultivation, been termed `the garden of New Zealand'. <i>Ibid.</i> 167 The district of Taranaki, which comprises the country around Mount Egmont, has with justice been termed `the garden of New Zealand', ... <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),38</i>)

>  <b>SEWELL</b> The Taranaki country is the favorite [sic] district of the Maoris. It is called the garden of New Zealand, and very beatiful it is, as well as very fertile. <i>(1855,Journal of Henry Sewell,2/159,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FITTON</b> The settlement of New Plymouth, or Taranaki, so called from the native name of Mount Egmont, ... has been frequently spoken of as `The Garden of New Zealand'. <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),145</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> Taranaki used to be called by its inhabitants `The Garden of New Zealand.' <i>(1861,New Zealand, As It Was and As It Is,34</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Small steamers ... will carry away the produce of the [Taranaki] district which may with justice be called the garden of New Zealand. <i>(1868,Past and Present of NZ,208</i>)

>  `We never used to have these heavy frosts before the emigrants came in,' said the old Taranaki settlers some twenty-five years ago, as they thought of the golden days, when the `garden of New Zealand' was fenced off from the rest of the world by forty miles of standing bush. <i>(1911,TrNZI,43/439</i>)

>  <b>MANNERING</b> Taranaki had been justly called `The Garden of New Zealand' from the early times of settlement. <i>(1943,Eighty Years,82</i>)

### gardening
 
 <i>n.</i> digging or cutting footholds in ice. [(mountaineers')]

>  Due to the gardening we had done on the upward journey the return was much quicker ... <i>(1948,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,IV/18/258</i>)

### garfish
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Reporhamphus ihi</i> or (? earlier) <i>Hemirhamphus intermedius, H. australis</i>, =half-beak, =ihe, =piper, =takeke. [1970 Pictured and named on the definitive issue, of the NZ 7 1/2 cent stamp]

>  He sent.. sixty dozen garfish to Wellington, and got only 6s 6d for them. <i>(Sep 10, 1910,NZ Free Lance,13</i>)

>  <b>COLENSO</b> A small but graceful species of garfish ... [55] flow, or rather sprung into my boat. ... Here [near Bay of Islands, the natives] call them Taheke [check ?Takeke]; but among the southern tribes, Ihe. <i>(1844,Excursion in the Northern Island of NZ</i>) [NOTES: in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 54-55]

>  <b>SORENSON</b> HALFBEAK (a) Scientific name: <i>Reporhamphus ihi</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Garfish; Piper. (c) Other common names: None known. (d) Principal localities where taken: North Auckland, Auckland and Cook Strait. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,29</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Ihe</i>, n.<i> Hemirhamphus intermedius, the garfish. </i> <i>(1971,Dictionary of the Maori Language,74,1975 ed.</i>)

>  The garfish, or piper as it is called in Auckland, is a common fish along our coasts. sense same as OED (<i>Belore vulgaris</i>) =piper. <i>(Oct 21, 1972,Press, The,12/3</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> GARFISH Often called Piper, these slender fish with their needle-point noses (or rather lower jaws) are common inhabitants of our shallow seashores. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,115</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> PIPER (Garfish, ihi) <i>Hyporhamphus ihi</i> ... The piper is an elongate fish with a long beak-like lower jaw. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,171,1984 ed.</i>)

>  [species name] garfish .. [scientific name] <i>Hyporhamphus ihi</i> .. [other name(s)] piper, halfbeak, needlefish <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Garfish Hyporhamphus ihi </i>... Often called piper. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,66</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] garfish 956-68 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <b>[WICKHAM]</b> As we nosed out of the bay, the needle-nosed garfish - "piper" were darting along the surface of the water, like a shoal of silver snakes. <i>(1897,Casual Ramblings,15</i>)

>  It was our privilege to witness an immense shoal of garfish pursued by mutton birds in Waikawa Bay. <i>(Jan 30, 1890,Otago Witness,18</i>)

>  In New Zealand, garfish is also know [sic] as piper or half beak. <i>(Dec 5, 2003,Dominion Post,E5</i>)

>  Many ... varieties of sea-fish are caught round the coast, such as barracouta, groper, rock-cod, ling, flounders, soles, gar-fish, and others, all which are plentiful; ... <i>(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The,32</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> This name is applied both to the SKIPPER (<i>Scombresox Forsteri</i>) and to the Ihi or Half Beak (<i>Hemirhamphus intermedius</i>) which are allied fishes representing those of the same names in the British seas. <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,118</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 86. HEMIRHAMPHUS INTERMEDIUS. Cant. Gar Fish. <i>(1872,Fishes,53</i>)

>  [Native or Common Name] Ihi, Garfish .. [Scientific Name] <i>Hemirhamphus intermedius</i> <i>(1877,TrNZI,9/485</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  That no flounders should be sold under nine inches long; no red cod under ten inches; no mullet under nine inches; and no garfish under fourteen inches -... <i>(1879,TrNZI,11/381</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>SENIOR</b> Amongst the sea-fishes fit for food there are ... a number of periodical fishes ... and these include frost-fish. barracuda, horse-mackerel, king-fish, dory, warehou, mackerel, and garfish. <i>(1880,Travel & Trout,288</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> GARFISH (<i>Hemirhamphus intermedius</i>). ... [34] This name is applied both to the skipper (<i>Scombresox Forsteri</i>) and to the ihi or half-beak, which are allied fishes representing those of the same names in the British seas. The representatives of the former genus, however, though common in England, appear to be rare in these seas, while the half-beak, which is of rare occurrence in the British seas, is common all round New Zealand, and is the ordinary gar-fish that is so highly appreciated, especially in Auckland and Dunedin. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,33</i>)

>  Of the edible [pelagic] fishes ... such as the frostfish, barracouta, horse-mackerel, kingfish, dory, warehou, mackerel, and garfish; while only the ling, hake and haddock ... are among those of more southern types ... <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,167</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMNS</b> <i>takeke, Hemiramphus intermedius, garfish. </i> <i>(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,172</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> In England the name is applied to any fish of the family <i>Belone Vulgaris</i>. In Melbourne the <i>Garfish</i> is a true one, <i>Belone ferox</i>, Gunth., called in Sydney `Long Tom.' In Sydney, Tasmania, and New Zealand it is <i>Hemirhamphus intermedius</i>, Cantor.; and in New South Wales, generally `Guard-fish,' and it is the river-fish <i>H. regularis</i>, Gunth., family <i>Sombresocida</i>. Some say that the name was originally `Guard-fish,' and it is still sometimes so spelt. But the word is derived from <i>Gar</i>, in Anglo-Saxon, which meant spear, dart, javelin, and the allusion is to the long spear-like projection of the fish's jaws. Called by the <i>Sydney fishermen</i> Ballahoo, <i>and in Auckland</i> the Piper (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,158</i>)

>  <i>Hemirhamphus intermedius</i> Cantor. (<i>Hemirhamphus intermedius</i> of former list.) Garfish occur commonly in Otago Harbour during the summer moths. <i>(1913,TrNZI,45/234</i>) [NOTES: Thomson, Otago]

>  Garfish. <i>Hemirhamphus intermedius</i> ... <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/271</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps]

>  HEMIRAMPHUS INTERMEDIUS Cantor. <i>Garfish</i>, or <i>Piper; Takeke</i>. Occurs in abundance around all the northern coasts. Common, Otago, in spring and early summer. ... Distribution: New Zealand coasts. <i>(1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,IV/120</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Hemirhamphus intermedius</i> Cantor. GARFISH or PIPER; TAKEKE. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,21,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> The well-known garfish or piper (<i>Hemirhamphus intermedius</i>) has the lower jaw protruding far beyond the upper, and uses it for grubbing among the seagrass in shallow bays. <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,198</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 198]

>  <i>Hemirhamphus intermedius</i>: Garfish or piper. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,71</i>) [NOTES: 1936 Handbook for NZ (ANZAAS Conf. Jan. 1937) 71]

>  Hemirhamphus intermedius Cantor. Garfish (piper). <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/404</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Garfish or Piper <i>Reporhamphus ihi</i> (Phillipps) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,403</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 403]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> GARFISH or PIPER (TAKEKE) <i>Reporhamphus ihi</i> . This fish is known in Auckland as Piper, although in Otago it is called Garfish or Guard Fish. ... It is on account of this peculiarity [a long bottom jaw] that they are sometimes called Halfbeaks. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,157,2nd ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> THE GARFISH <i>Reporhamphus ihi</i> (Phillipps) ... Maori name: TAKEKE or IHI. <i>Ibid.</i> 169 Garfish <i>Reporhamphus ihi </i>Piper; Guard-fish; Half-beak <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,33</i>)

>  garfish A fish with a long spear-like snout; the name is given to several different fishes in Australia and New Zealand. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),178,8th ed. ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Garfish (piper) - ihe, takeke, <i>Reporhamphus ihi</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-12</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> GARFISH ... <i>Other names: Reporhamphus ihi</i>; piper, half-beak; ihe, takeke (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,206</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> GARFISH [<i>Reporhamphus ihi</i>] ... Also called piper, halfbeak, ihi, and takake. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,16</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Longsnouted Garfish <i>Euleptorhamphus longirostris</i> (Cuvier) <i>(1955,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,403</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 403]

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] garfish, longsnouted garfish <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> needlefish.

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> NEEDLEFISH ... <i> Other names: Scomberesox forsteri</i>; saury, skipper, double beak, ocean garfish, ocean piper. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,205</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] garfish, ocean 975 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [[see quot Convolvulus and Allium triquetrum]]

>  <i>garlic, three-cornered [s] Allium triquetrum</i> L. ... <i>Ibid.</i> <i>three-cornered garlic [s] Allium triquetrum</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,28</i>)

>  (?Allium triquetrum) A species of wild flowers which is a troublesome noxious weed. Often called wild onion by mistake. <i>(Dec 10, 1973,Dominion,18</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Allium triquetrum</i> THREE-CORNERED GARLIC ... Onion weed ...Native to: Europe and North Africa. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,294</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> WILD ONION (<i>Allium vineale</i>), is also called wild garlic and crow garlic ... [It is a bulbous perennial with tall cylindrical stem, bearing at its apex a cluster of white to reddish flowers ... ] <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,55,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>garlic, wild [=] three-cornered garlic: wild onion [s] </i> <i>Ibid.,</i> 86 wild <i>garlic [=] three-cornered garlic: wild onion [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,28</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a longdrop lavatory [ORIGIN: rhyming slang]

### gas gun
 
 <i>n.</i> a gas-stove lighter, sparked by a flint device.

I kept pulling the trigger and all the sparks shot out and scared the daylights out of the poor cows; ... <i>(1964,Any Old Dollars Mister?,49</i>)

### gas man
 
 <i>n.</i> a player with speed

>  "We couldn't get our gas men running into gaps," he said. <i>(Nov 5, 1999,Evening Post,28</i>)

### gasper
 
 <i>n.</i> a sheep gasping for breath while being shorn (usu. caused by grass coming up into the sheep's throat). [(shearers') -->1979 Oct 12 L. Bauer]

>  <b>BOWEN</b> GASPER. A sheep gasping for breath while being shorn. The trouble is usually caused by grass coming up into the sheep's throat. <i>(1956,Wool Away!,156</i>) [NOTES: glossary: Shearing terms]

>  <b>TURNER</b> There are names for types of sheep, the cot with matted wool; the cobbler, a difficult sheep; the flyer, a fast one; the kicker; the sandyback and the gasper, which gasps apparently because grass comes into its throat. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,147</i>)

>  Gasper: Sheep gasping for breath while being shorn. Usually caused by grass coming up into the sheep's throat. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,55</i>)

### gate
 
 <i>n.</i> rugby term for imaginary gate behind ruck

>  "We're allowing people to come through a very extended gate," he said, referring to the imaginary gate behind a ruck that is meant to ensure players come in square. <i>(Sep 2, 2005,Dominion Post,C10</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the mouth

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Gate. 4. The mouth: New Zealanders' (from ca. 1910), esp. soldiers' in G.W. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,318</i>)

>  [In local criminal slang] ... `moosh' or `gate' is mouth, ... <i>(Apr 4, 1938,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <b>PARK</b> `Oh, shut yer big gate!' said his father shrilly, ... <i>(1951,Witch's Thorn, The,113</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> `Not with a snuff-stick hanging out of their gates. ... ' <i>(1978,Glory and the Dream, The,76</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> gate mouth; WWI soldiers; eg `Shut your gate, mate, or I'll shut it for you.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,48</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> give (a word) the suffix -gate indicating a scandal, deriving from 'Watergate'

>  More so if it hangs around long enough to be "gated" - as in Corngate and Paintergate. <i>(Jul 16, 2005,NZ Herald,B6</i>)

### GATE
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Gifted and Talented Education [ORIGIN: acronym]

>  "Here we refer to a high achieving children's initiative as Gate - gifted and talented education..." <i>(May 11, 2005,Capital Times,7</i>)

### gate fever
 
 <i>n.</i> a form of cabin fever

>  Others [on home detention] developed "gate fever" frustrated by seeing the freedom they took for granted just outside their property. <i>(Sep 13, 2003,Dominion Post,A14</i>)

### gate sale
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the sale of farm, orchard or market garden produce at the roadside [ i.e. at the farm gate.]

>  Perfect location for gate sales. <i>(Sep 22, 2002,Hawke's Bay Today,Property Guide 9</i>)

>  .. offsetting the increased price obtained by producers when making gate sales. <i>(Dec 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,584</i>)

>  Splendour. Plantings seem now to be restricted to gate sales, for which this variety is ideal. <i>(Nov 1974,Orchardist NZ,338</i>)

>  This block is just waiting for the right person to plant and set up a winery or gate sales orchard. <i>(Feb 19, 2002,Realenz.co.nz</i>)

### gate speed
 
 <i>n.</i> the speed at which a racehorse leaves the starting gate

>  Our horse has good gate speed and should be able to get up near the lead without too much trouble. <i>(Jan 30, 2004,Dominion Post  Sport & RaceForm,20</i>)

### gate sweep
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>EVANS</b> The hay was brought in by the rope or by a horse-operated sweep. This comprised a vertical framework something like [a] wooden farm gate, but with two wings of similar construction at the ends. <i>(1956,History of Farm Implements,62</i>)

>  <b>HAWKE & SCOTT</b> These sweeps were rather like a gate with hinged wings and teeth protruding on both sides, about ten feet long... Other gate sweeps, which had no teeth, worked in the same way, the driver standing on the bottom rail or perhaps lying on the top of the load. <i>(1985,Early Farm Machinery in NZ,89</i>)

### gate-leaner
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a garrulous farmer who is not very active.

>  <b>NELSON</b> gate-leaner: a sociable farmer who would rather talk than work. <i>(1999,Eugene Nelson Glossary,7</i>)

>  ineffective farmer: a gate-leaner. Station cadet from England: a gate-leaner. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### gate-opener
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the lowest status employee on a sheep station; the person employed to open gates for the driver in the days when roads were not fenced.

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> I probably played a more important part as gate-opener for her, as there were about five gates to be opened across the road in those days. <i>(1994,I Was So Lucky,37</i>)

>  general farm helper: gate-opener. A cadet from England - a gateopener. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### gate-rafters
 
 <i>n.</i> The rafters on either side of the gate of the logging-dam in a driving creek [(kauri bushmen's)]

>  <b>REED</b> The two rafters on either side of the sill were known as gate-rafters, and the distance between them - perhaps twenty feet - would be the width of the gate. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,201</i>)

### gate-shy
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of stock] hesitant at an open gateway and that have to be driven through with effort.

>  <b>MCNAUGHTON</b> The leaders lined up and looked through the gateway and turned away. This made the sheep directly behind suspicious and they also became gate-shy. <i>(1970,Tat,21</i>)

### gatehanger
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a ram of doubtful efficiency.

>  a ram of doubtful efficiency a gatehanger. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### gatekeeper
 
 <i>n.</i> Committee of senior Ministers with responsibility for overseeing governent financial expenditure; euphemism for older "razor gang" (see Australian National Dictionary - 1981); a political overseer

>  [Hdg] Shipley calls in razor gang... Prime Minister Jenny Shipley said a razor gang of ministers would scrutinise spending. <i>(Jun 30, 1998,Dominion,1</i>)

>  The memo.. said that Plunket administered public money and "cannot go to the public and request their financial support for funds if we are not seen to be responsible gate keepers". <i>(Nov 28, 2002,BLANK ENTRY,A5</i>)

>  A couple of close relatives casually used the word gatekeeper to refer to interfering pests who delete information from a data base... Today's gatekeeper is a person who controls the flow of information in a large organisation, deciding what is passed upwards to the executive levels or to a parent company, and downward to subordinate staff or subsidiary companies... I can see the gatekeeper now, grey-suited, in a windowless office, cackling softly, drunk with power and righteousness, accessing databases and sorting out the bits to be sent on their way up or down. <i>(Oct 6, 2001,Dominion,12</i>)

>  [Hdg] Clark keen for gatekeeper to take over Hobbs' office. Prime Minister Helen Clark wants first-time minister Marian Hobbs, who is rapidly earning a name for dithering, to hire a policy gatekeeper along the lines of her own adviser Heather Simpson. <i>(Apr 19, 2000,Dominion,2</i>)

>  \{He\} said Miss Clark was right to suggest Ms Hobbs should hire a policy gatekeeper if she was concerned about the first-time minister's performance. <i>(Apr 21, 2000,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  Jenny Chisholm notes [that gatekeeper] crops up in churches that draw a firm line between those who are saved and the also-runs doomed to perdition. The gatekeepers are the ones making it clear when you are in or out. Brian Roberts says it occurs in many role-playing computer games... <i>(Oct 13, 2001,Dominion,14</i>)

>  Ms Wilson is said to be feeling as bruised as Broadcasting Minister Marian Hobbs, another of Miss Clark's Cabinet protegees, was recently over the prime minister's desire for her to hire a policy gatekeeper. <i>(May 29, 2000,Dominion,2</i>)

>  New Zealand needed to ensure that people could make decisions rather than have them made by the "gatekeepers in Wellington." <i>(Aug 3, 2001,Dominion,9</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  And they are unhappy at the Fisheries Ministry's refusal to make the tribe the gatekeeper for voluntary fisheries officers nominations. <i>(Jan 26, 2002,Dominion,13</i>)

>  "The gatekeepers to the sickness benefits," Sowry says, grimacing, "are the GPs. They're also the gatekeepers to the ACC system." <i>(Mar 8, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i>

>  "I'm proud of the song and you just have to be a gatekeeper and make sure people do not try and do a tacky job with it." <i>(Dec 1, 2002,Sunday Star Times,A3</i>)

### Gateway Antarctica
 
 <i>n.</i> the Centre for Antarctic studies and research at the University of Canterbury

### gateway effect
 
 <i>n.</i>

This leads them to smoke more and eventually leads them to the point where marijuana just doesn't do anything for them anymore, so they have to move through the gateway onto hard drugs...To apply the "logic" of the "gateway effect" one would have to concede that if you have a cup of coffee then you will inevitably go on to drink alcohol, smoke cannabis and end up on the P. <i>(2004,Salient,8/33</i>)

 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to muster.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> straggle: to cover country to gather in stock that have been missed. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,80</i>)

### gathering dog
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a heading dog of gentle nature.

### gauge box
 
 <i>n.</i> A goldfields device for measuring the amounts of water discharged. [goldmining]

>  <b>EVANS</b> `When other and more accurate means of measuring water are not conveniently available, it may be done by using gauge boxes of the dimensions ... `The gauge box shall be 12 feet long, 20 inches or 40 inches wide, inside measurements ... in accordance with the undermentioned table ... In other words, if the depth of the pressure above the board, above the top of the aperture, is five inches, the aperture is twenty inches wide, and two inches deep, the number of sluice heads discharged is one.' <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,8</i>)

### Gay Blacks
 
 <i>n.</i> NZ gay sports representatives

>  In response to 2006 being a huge year for the Gay, Bi, Lesbian and Transgender ("GBLT") calendar, a group has been formed in Wellington & Christchurch named the Gay Blacks. This group consists of those intending to travel to the Gay Games, Chicago and Outgames, Montreal in July/August 2006. The Gay Blacks will aim to assist those who wish to compete at either one or both of the games by providing: - A co-ordinated approach to a team uniform - Access to information on travel and accomodation opportunities - Be a reference point for those people intending to participate both before and during the games <i>(2006,Internet - see Notes,-</i>) [NOTES: www.gayblacks.co.nz]

### gelly
 
 <i>n.</i> gelignite

### gelly head
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The miners [at Martha Mine c1907] often suffered from severe headaches, known as [5] `gelly heads', caused by the fumes from blasting in confined spaces and from handling the `gelly'. ... If [the nitro-glycerine] came into contact with the miners' hands, and they then wiped the sweat from their foreheads, they would get a terrible headache. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,4</i>)

### gem
 
 <i>n.</i> Prob. a short form of gem scone q.v., a sweet mixture baked usu. in a gem-iron, or a patty-tin

>  WHITE FLOUR GEMS ... Flour ... Milk ... 2 Eggs (whites only) ... make a batter ... Bake in hot gem pans in a quick oven. <i>(1914,Colonial Everyday Cookery,277</i>)

>  <b>BEATON</b> GEMS self raising flour ... eggs ... sugar ... warm milk ... butter ... salt ... Have the gem iron at boiling heat ... a tablespoon of the mixture ... put into each ... hot oven. <i>(1910,Universal Cookery Book,29</i>)

>  GEMS ... butter ... sugar ... egg ... milk ... flour ... baking powder. Cook in Gem irons. <i>(1915,Bungalow Recipe Book, The,75</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> GEMS ... Have the gem irons as hot as possible ... half fill with mixture, put on top tray of oven. <i>(1917,Economic Technical Cookery Book,272</i>)

>  GOLDEN MUFFINS or GEMS ... butter ... castor sugar ... eggs ... marmalade ... flour ... soda ... <i>(1930,Electric Cookery - Hints Instruction and Recipes,43</i>)

>  GEMS ... flour ... sugar ... milk ... butter ... B.P. ... 1 egg ... bake in hot gem irons in a hot oven. <i>(1934,Marigold Book of Recipes, The,34</i>)

>  GEMS ... selfrising [sic] flour ... butter ... sugar ... 1 egg. Milk to mix a batter thicker than pikelets ... <i>(1936,Merry Meal Maker Cookery Book,32</i>)

>  GEMS 1 egg ... butter ... baking powder ... flour ... sugar ... Irons must be hot ... <i>(1938,Milkmade Cook Book,41</i>)

>  <b>PEARSON</b> ... Mr O'Reilly having to take out his dentures to remove them from a treacle gem. <i>(1963,Coal Flat,50</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Both gems and the gem irons they are made in are New Zealand inventions. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,157</i>)

>  Another kitchen, this time with the smell of fresh-baked gems to greet me. <i>(2001,Huia 4: Contemp. Maori Fiction,26</i>)

>  The outstanding favourite is the gem, ginger-flavoured; as for its specialised container, the gem-iron (unknown to any dictionary I have consulted, including my own production), I can now report that there are sufficient gem-irons in our kitchens to provide the navy with the makings of the hull of the frigate they are looking for... These gem-irons are not for scrap. <i>(Jun 4, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),55</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> A straw-boater hat [? trade name]. - [(obs.) ca1920 Auckland, R. Mason]

### gem iron
 
 <i>n.</i> sc. for making gem cakes. Earlier gem pan

### gemfish
 
 <i>n.</i> A food-fish similar in appearance to the barracouta, [Also called southern kingfish]

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>GEMFISH Rexea solandri</i> (Silver kingfish, southern kingfish, hake, king barracouta) <i>(1982,Collins Guide,287,1984 ed.</i>)

>  [species name] gemfish .. [scientific name] <i>Rexea solandri</i> .. [other name(s)] southern kingfish, silver kingfish, kingfish silver <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> Gemfish <i>Rexea solandri</i> ... Also occurs off southern Australia, as well as Japan. The name gemfish, conveying family relationship as well as irridescence, is more appropriate. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,121</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [mountaineers' and trampers']
