>  They [Pete and Sam] got up the low scree, sidled across the first face ... but they were getting bombed so they cramponed up to just below a gendarme ... <i>(Apr 19, 1971,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

### Gene Tunney
 
 <i>n.</i> money [prisoners'slang]

>  I was caster for Gene Tunney, so I took a slapsie maxie to the course. Gene Tunney ... money <i>(May 21, 1963,NZ Truth,19</i>)

### General Assembly
 
 <i>n.</i> the NZ parliament (see also 1982 and 1987 cits)

>  VI. ... it shall be lawful for Her Majesty ... to constitute ... a General Assembly in and for the Islands of New Zealand, to be called the General Assembly of New Zealand, which said General Assembly shall consist of and be holden by the Governor ..., and a Legislative Council, and a House of Representatives; ... <i>(Aug 28, 1846,NZ Statutes</i>) [NOTES: 1846 Aug 28 An Act to make further provision for the government of the New Zealand Islands (9 and 10 Vict. c. 103) in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 20]

>  The `progress' party appeal to the General Assembly. <i>(Jul 29, 1851,Lyttelton Times,4</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> For the government of the Colony as a whole there were constituted the two Houses which at present exist - the Legislative Council and the House of Representatives, together constituting the General Assembly. The councillors were nominated by the Governor, in this respect resembling the old nominee system. They held their seats for life. As showing the fear and detestation in which the word `nominee' was held, Mr. Fox and others who assisted in passing the measure viewed the relating clauses as the great blots of the Act. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,140</i>)

>  ... to the whole of the Canterbury Province only Five for the General Assembly. <i>(1853,Journal of Henry Sewell,1/202,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>RUTHERFORD</b> <i>(1949,Selected Documents,55</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Mar 9 Proclamation by Governor Sir George Grey providing for the constitution of the General Assembly, and the Superintendents and Provincial Councils of the Provinces. in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 55]

>  At the opening of the session of the General Assembly the Lower House with a single dissentient voice, voted it desirable ... to introduce the English practice of government by filling the chief offices of government with persons possessing the confidence of the Houses of Assembly. <i>(Aug 23, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/156</i>) [NOTES: C.W. Richmond]

>  <b>FITTON</b> There has as yet been only one full session of the General Assembly of New Zealand, held at Auckland since the passing of the late Bill for the Constitution of New Zealand; ... <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),20</i>)

>  `I had the honor of serving you in the first General Assembly, and have been a member of your Provincial Council since the extension of its numbers. <i>(Jul 1, 1857,Lyttelton Times,2</i>)

>  <b>EARP</b> Under the new Constitution, the government of New Zealand is vested in a Governor appointed by the Crown, and a General Assembly, consisting of two houses, one elected by the people, the other appointed by the Crown for life. <i>(1858,Handbook Wellington,4</i>)

>  1 During the session of the General Assembly, which has just closed, the following measures, on subjects specially affecting the aboriginal natives of New Zealand, have passed both Houses:- ... <i>(Sep 29, 1858,Memorandum by Native Secretary</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Sep 29 Memorandum by Responsible Advisers on Native Affairs in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 104.]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> ... the Secretary of State intimated [in 1856] that the Speakers of both Houses of the General Assembly were entitled to be styled `Honourable'. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,2/248</i>)

>  <b>BUDDLE</b> The hon. gentleman said, `Hitherto there has been no attempt to meet the special and local wants of the Aborigines ... Between the provincial Councils and the General Assembly the wants of the Natives in [17] these respects have ... been wholly neglected.' <i>(1860,Maori King Movement,16</i>)

>  I was in Auckland attending the session of the Genl. Assembly. I went as representative of Omata, a district possessing now, 5 houses and a stockade. To add to the pleasures of the war we had a desperate political conflict. The two parties which divide us are the Ultra provincial and the General Govt. parties called opprobriously the Centralisers. The difference ... is no doubt the old difference under a new face, England or America? <i>(Dec 9, 1860,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/666,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: J.C. Richmond]

>  The localisation of the land fund ... was the inevitable result of the Ultra-Provincialism which [Grey] did his best to introduce. Unconstitutionally he started the provinces before the General Assembly - thus launching the constitution [441] `bottom upwards' as old Charleton used to say. <i>(Dec 27, 1877,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/440</i>) [NOTES: C.W. Richmond]

>  There has lately been presented to the Houses of the General Assembly a State paper dealing with the census of the Maori population. <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/150</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> <i>(1930,Early Canterbury Runs,160</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> It took Otago members over two months by sea to reach Auckland for the first session of the General Assembly. There was good reason, then, for decentralized institutions; ... <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,87</i>)

>  <b>DALZIEL</b> General Assembly: Parliament. <i>(1968,Sir Julius Vogel,32</i>)

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> ... the authority of the executive derives from the supremacy of Parliament, or, to apply the correct terminology for New Zealand, the General Assembly. That is the `... bipartite body consisting of the House of Representatives and the Governor-General', which exercises legislative function. <i>(1987,Politicians, Public Servants & Public Enterprise,12</i>)

>  General Assembly ... 2. N.Z. an older name for parliament. Now in occas. elliptical use for General Assembly Library, the library (now part of the National Library) attached to Parliament and housed as an adjunct to parliament Buildings. also G.A. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,463</i>) [NOTES: nf. 1986 NZPOD; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 206]

>  Provincial Councils Bill II Jurisdiction of the Council 24. No such Law or Ordinances shall be repugnant to the Law of England or to any ordinance to be made and enacted by the Governor-in-Chief, with the advice and consent of the Legislative Council of New Zealand, or by any General Assembly thereof. <i>(Jan 25, 1851,Lyttelton Times,3</i>)

### general electorate
 
 <i>n.</i> a parliamentary electorate other than a Maori electorate

### General Government
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) a 19th c. term for central government as opposed to any of the provincial governments

>  We do not expect that the General Government will move in the affair. <i>(Aug 23, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/156,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: C.W. Richmond]

>  [from Taranaki settlers] `That one of the two parties into which the natives are divided (commonly styled the friendly party) claims a right to the support of the British Government. <i>Ibid.</i>164 `That the General Government of New Zealand has been kept constantly informed on the subject of this memorial ... <i>(Aug 18, 1858,Australian & NZ Sheep Farming</i>) [NOTES: 1855 Aug 18 Australian and NZ Gazette in 1856 Fitton NZ 161]

>  ... the administration of our affairs [in the Province of Canterbury] is embarrassed by the great distance of the General Government from us. <i>(Jul 1, 1857,Lyttelton Times,2</i>)

>  The policy of self-reliance is being steadily pursued by the General Government. This policy was initiated by the Weld Ministry ... <i>(Jan 14, 1869,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

### General Grant
 
 <i>n.</i> a ship wrecked off the Auckland Islands in 1866

>  So there's a ship, the <i>General Grant</i>, which sank off the Auckland Islands in 1866, and has thus far eluded 29 different search teams. <i>(Jan 31, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

### general hand
 
 <i>n.</i> [internet search Jan 2006: Australianism; also used NZ]

>  [heading] <B>GENERAL HAND WANTED</B> <i>(Sep 8, 2004,Hawke's Bay Today,C4</i>)

>  WHAKATU COLDSTORES LTD <B>GENERAL HAND VACANCIES</B> <i>(Mar 3, 2005,Hawke's Bay Today,29</i>)

### General McArthur
 
 <i>n.</i> Occasionally used for a return at meals, especially pudding: a play on McArthur's `I shall return'. [(seamen's obs.) ca1982 p.c. Bill Martin, NZ Seamen's Union.]

### general roll
 
 <i>n.</i> the roll of a general electorate

### general store
 
 <i>n.</i> a store esp. rural selling general merchandise for all home, farm and work needs: by the 1980s confined to country use. (See [store](spa-sys.md#store))

>  Wholesale & Retail General Store, ... <i>(Apr 1843,NZ Gazette</i>) [NOTES: 1843 Apr `NZ Gazette' in 1956 Gore Levins 9]

>  <b>GODLEY</b> There are two `general stores', three butchers, two bakers, etc. Meat is 5d. per lb. milk 3d. per quart. <i>(Mar 17, 1851,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1851 Feb 27 Godley Letter in 1950 Carrington Goldey 125]

>  He has opened a General Store. <i>(Mar 17, 1851,Lyttelton Times,1</i>)

>  <b>MACKIE</b> They, like the other colonists have had many difficulties and alarms to go through, but now they are thriving and keep a general store. <i>(Feb 5, 1853,Traveller under Concern,76,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAITT</b> There is ... a branch Post Office and about a dozen `General Stores.' <i>(1856,Progress of Canterbury, NZ, The,8</i>)

>  <b>KENNEDY</b> Two jolly old fellows ... roamed into the `general store' [at Temuka] ... asking for a `pund o' her best watter buskits,' ... <i>(1876,Colonial Travel,196</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> ... one [building in the goldfields township] being the `General Store', where every description of merchandise is sold; the other a public house, ... `The Maori Hen Hotel', where alcoholic liquors ... are vended. <i>(1881,White Hood and Blue Cap,5</i>)

>  <b>HART</b> There were but three general stores [in Chch in 1852], but at these one could get anything from a needle to an anchor. <i>(1886,Stray Leaves,29</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> It boasts of ... three general stores (or shops, as they would be called in England) ... <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,97</i>)

>  <b>GRIEVE</b> By far the most important building in the Pipiwai borough was the general store. <i>(1939,Sketches from Maoriland,32,1961 ed.</i>)

>  A general store, privately owned and complete with electric light and telephone, is still operated on the ground floor. <i>(1948,Our Own Country,7</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> ... pub, post office, butcher ... and general store ... <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,204,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Waters of the Moon)]

>  Those old-time general stores were usually little more than cheaply constructed sheds with the longest side facing a main road. The primary needs, food, clothing and builders' equipment, were all catered for; ... <i>(1970,Way Up North,73</i>)

>  <b>DAVIES</b> A small general store provided most of their immediate needs. <i>(1975,Outback,9</i>)

>  There was a petrol station on one side of the Main ... Road, two hotels ... and between them the Post Office - a newish building dating from the severance of this service from the General Store across the way and itself replaced by a trading chain. <i>(1985,Long Lives the King,24</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> I approached Lees' General Store. The kuia no longer sit against the corrugated iron fence alongside, ... dribbling on their moko. <i>Ibid.</i> 98 Tahora had a standard four railway houses in a row, a general store selling petrol and oil. <i>(1985,G'day Country,62</i>)

### general storekeeper
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also storekeeper](spa-sys.md#storekeeper))

>  In consequence of the undersigned having to compete with so many General Storekeepers which are daily presenting themselves before the public, feels it is his duty to offer to the public a choice selection of Groceries ... <i>(Apr 1983,NZ Gazette</i>) [NOTES: 1843 Apr `NZ Gazette' in 1956 Gore Levins 9]

### general strike
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> One of the incidents which stands out very clearly in my childhood was the 1913 General Strike. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,95</i>)

### Generation Debt
 
 <i>n.</i> students caught by user-pays philosophy

>  The student loan scheme with its high interest rates has made it difficult for "Generation Debt" to see how it can possibly get ahead by staying home. <i>(Nov 1999,North & South,46</i>)

### generator
 
 <i>n.</i> an electricity company producing electricity

>  Generators have been allowed to vertically integrate and pucharse retail capacity, to an extent that they have become largely integrated vertically and internally hedged. <i>(May 7, 2003,Dominion Post,C9</i>)

### genetic bottleneck
 
 <i>n.</i> restriction in genetic diversity

>  Ms Collen said bringing kokako from Mapara to Pukaha Mount Bruce will achieve a greater genetic diversity, avoiding a potential "genetic bottleneck" and ensuring a healthy new population. <i>(Sep 7, 2005,Wairarapa News,14</i>)

>  Department of Conservation kokako recovery group leader Rose Collen believes they will bring a greater generic diversity to the forest, avoiding a potential 'genetic bottleneck' and ensuring a healthy new population. <i>(Sep 21, 2005,Wairarapa News,8</i>)

### genetic bottlenecking
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [genetic bottleneck](g-go.md#genetic-bottleneck))

>  Genetic bottlenecking occurs when a population is reduced to very few individuals with similar genetic code, and can result in fertility and health problems in future generations. <i>(Sep 7, 2005,Dominion Post,14</i>)

### genetic gain
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the improvement in one's livestock, measured by the value 'breeding worth'.

>  Rates of genetic gain have increased in Landcorp's terminal sire breeding programmes ... <i>(Oct 22, 1998,NZ Farmer,22</i>)

>  We use E/T and AI extensively to maximise genetic gain. <i>(Apr 26, 2002,Dominion,26</i>)

>  Mating yearling heifers to superior bulls is becoming more common. It is a way to speed the rate of genetic gain. <i>(Jun 1982,NZ Dairy Exporter,3</i>)

>  Genetic gain is measured in breeding worth, and higher BW means, quite simply, more dollars in the bank. Because genetic gain is so important, we are committed to making sure farmers get it; as far as Livestock Improvement is concerned, we have a commitment to increase genetic gain by five BW units/year. <i>(Sep 22, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,Farming 2000 Supp. 10</i>)

>  Expert Livestock Androvax has minimal impact on the rate of genetic gain in commercial ewe flocks. <i>(Feb 10, 2002,Fencepost.com</i>)

### genetic recovery
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a stock breeding programme where five generations of a breed are traced for excellence.

>  They now run two separate units with 490 genetic recovery Jerseys with a BW 140. <i>(May 31, 2002,Dominion,28</i>)

>  These genetic recovery animals are also fetching premiums in the market place. <i>(May 31, 2002,Dominion,29</i>)

### genista
 
 <i>n.</i> a flowering plant

>  I rounded Golden Point to find it ablaze with golden genista flowers and waves of their sweet scent washed across the bay. <i>(Feb 28, 2004,Dominion Post,E14</i>)

### gennaker
 
 <i>n.</i> a yacht sail

>  The gennaker, which looks like an enormous black garbage bag attached to the bow, has a malevolent life of its own. <i>(Feb 16, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

### gentian
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>gentian [s] Gentiana </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,28</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The chief plants I noticed [on Ruapehu] were a beautiful everlasting, the harebell, 2 kinds of gentian, a pretty speedwell, an epacris ... a wild carrot also abounded. <i>(Jan 30, 1853,Journal,8/70</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Jan 30 Taylor Journal [TS:ATB] 8/70. Also quoted in 1966 Mead Richard Taylor 192]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> We have one very stupid white gentian; but it is, to say the least of it, uninteresting [131] to the casual observer. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,130</i>)

>  <i>Gentian</i>. Gentiana corymbifera. This is another of the few plants most hill shepherds know. It is a great joke to persuade a new chum to chew the root of it. ([1898 Morris Austral-English]) <i>(Oct 21, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> The clumps of tussock ... sheltered a scattered growth of English grasses, of dainty wild flowers ..., a delicate little blue-bell, almesia, gentian, and ... the bidibid which hooked itself onto the sheep's wool to form great tangles. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,37</i>)

>  [Waimakariri, Canterbury High Country] Scabweeds cover the rocks ... The downy seeds of the scabweeds fills [sic] the hollows like swansdown ... and dotted in profusion amidst the tussock are magnificent Mt Cook lillies [sic], Eyebright and gentian all the way to the hut ... <i>(1978,Manawatu Tramping Club Jubilee,89</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> All this alpine meadow was beautiful with flowers: giant celmisias with satiny-white petals like enormous daisies, mingled with snow-white gentians, and the wonder of the Alps - the mountain lily. <i>(1911,Through South Westland,6</i>)

### Gentle Annie
 
 <i>n.</i> A name given to various hills or slopes (some, ironically, steep) on bridle-tracks and coach-roads and remaining as local names (occas. and locally, a creek): origin possibly in a popular song, transferred thence to slopes.

>  "Boy racers," explains a Taradale local. "They like the corners." She's talking about the Gentle Annie, which snakes up from the bustle of the coast to the solitude of the vast Kaweka Forest Park and beyond that to the Central Plateau. <i>(Sep 2002,AA Directions,25</i>)

>  Annie was quiet and pleasant and hence the nickname to [sic] her licensed premises, the Gentle Annie [sc. in Kawerau Gorge]. <i>(1957,Tavern in the Town,238</i>) [NOTES: 1968 edition]

>  <b>LORETZ</b> I suggested that she climb 'gentle annie', the highest hill around Raetihi. <i>(1976,Moments of Life,23</i>)

>  It has to be remembered that below the dam the Shotover River and the streams, "Roaring Meg" and "Gentle Annie" empty into the Kawerau River. <i>(Sep 8, 1926,NZ Observer,2</i>) [NOTES: 9]

>  At the turnoff marked Gentle Annie, the climb begins ever so gradually up through the forest to Rocky Lookout. <i>(Feb 12, 2000,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> There was naturally a desire to find a somewhat easier path [over hills in Otago] and so what was known as the `Gentle Annie' track came into use and fashion. <i>(1874,George Washington Pratt,15</i>)

>  Afterwards found good crossing and carried the road from the township to the top of `Gentle Annie'. <i>(Jul 20, 1866,Taranaki Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1866 July 20 S. Percy Smith Taranaki Journal in 1922 Cowan NZ Wars 2/529]

>  `Ascending the long hill known as Gentle Annie, we wondered why such an application had been given to it. Having reached the foot of the Gentle Annie and crossed the creek, we cantered gaily along for several miles ...' <i>(1959,Taranaki Journal,43</i>) [NOTES: c1880s an anonymous tourist in 1959 L. Masters Tales of the Mails 43]

>  <b>KELLY</b> Our attention was concentrated in breasting those `Gentle Annies' as the steep ridges are called [? that] we are constantly ascending and descending on the greater portion of our route. <i>(1892,Journey Through Waitara,3</i>)

>  <b>WIN</b> We have Gentle Annies in every district, a legacy of either incompetence or laziness [of surveyors]. <i>(1924,Win Family, The,6</i>)

>  <b>LETHBRIDGE</b> There are a number of so-called `Gentle Annies' in New Zealand which I have negotiated from time to time, but this [at Ngaruroro] is one of the worst. <i>(1971,Sunrise on the Hills,144</i>)

>  <b>BARNS-GRAHAM</b> .. a long hill which bore the rather cynical name of `Gentle Annie.' <i>(1950,Sheep Station,24</i>)

>  Now you look out for this 'ere hill, it's called Gentle Annie, and lots o' blokes has come to grief on it. <i>(1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,X/100</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Two hours climb to get to Gentle Annie (the point where the power lines now cross the track), ... (iv) Auckland, and Thames goldfields. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,201</i>)

>  <b>LUSH</b> Lucy told us that this morning all the upper part of `Gentle Annie' (the hill at the foot of which their house is built) was covered with snow! <i>(Jul 21, 1874,Thames Journals,150,1975 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MULGAN</b> `Gentle Annie' was a hill they had to climb up to the bush uplands. <i>(1934,Spur of Morning,163</i>)

>  On the summmit of Gentle Annie [Murimotu highlands, Ruapehu] ... I met with what appeared to be a ... variety of Yellow Admiral butterfly ... <i>(1895,TrNZI,27/119</i>) [NOTES: (Buller)]

>  <b>CONDON</b> If they put a creamery at the top of Gentle Annie Hill, I venture to say that there would be from 500 to 700 cows milk sent to it. <i>(1992,Hurleyville,71</i>) [NOTES: 1902 quote]

>  <b>St JOHN</b> It may be laid down as an axiom that whenever the tourist in New Zealand arrives at a hill christened `Gentle Annie' he may prepare himself for a breather; there was no exception to the rule as far as concerned the `Gentle Annie' of Waikarimoana [sic], and it was uncommonly hot work getting to the top. <i>(1873,Pakeha Rambles Through Maori Lands</i>) [NOTES: 1873 St John Pakeha Rambles in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 573]

>  <b>MILLAR</b> He lived in a tiny hut [in Westland] ... at the top of Gentle Annie. (One wonders how many Gentle Annies there must be in New Zealand!) <i>(1953,High Noon,146,1965 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Gentle Annie an easy slope or a difficult slope; originally a coachman's term. - a barmaid, specifically Annie Bowling in Queenstown area; ref. James McNeish's Tavern in the Town; also the name of a song and a Turkish gun at the Dardanelles, 1915. [Check McNeish] <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,48</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> The waggon road at that time had reached [50] Kuripapanga, and big teams of navvies were engaged in forming it up over the Gentle Annie. On coming to a lookout place on the eastern side of the Annie George pulled his horse up and gazed up. <i>Ibid.</i> 60 On one occasion, ... , he did a complete turnabout with his coach and team on the narrow precipitous road about half way up the Annie, without undoing or breaking a single strap. <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,49</i>)

>  <i>Roaring Meg</i>, usually a creek and far more rough and tumble, is apt to be in the neighbourhood of <i>Gentle Annie... Roaring Meg</i> may come from the name of the famous gun of Londonderry. <i>(Sep 1959,Tararua,13/49</i>)

>  <b>CONDON</b> If they put a creamery at the top of Gentle Annie Hill, I venture to say that there would be from 500 to 700 cows milk sent to it. <i>(1992,Hurleyville,71</i>) [NOTES: 1902 quote]

>  <b>CONDON</b> A delivery cart belonging to Mr Ridge, Hurleyville storekeeper, came to grief on Friday on the top of the Gentle Annie hill through the breaching strap breaking. <i>(1992,Hurleyville,79</i>) [NOTES: 1913 quote]

>  <b>condon</b> One [delivery) was around the roads close to the township, the other went up the Gentle Annie and beyond. <i>(1992,Hurleyville,80</i>)

>  The mere mention of "Gentle Annie," "the Big Pump," "Block Nine," the "Waitohi" and so on, is sufficient occasion for a launch by any old Thames hand into stories of the past. <i>(Jan 25, 1913,NZ Observer,2</i>)

>  They are now on their way to the tunnel, one of them [sc. a steam engine] having successfully been piloted over "Gentle Annie". <i>(Sep 17, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,21</i>)

>  Mr Grenfell describes passing Roaring Meg and Gentle Annie as "child's play:" <i>(Jan 22, 1924,Otago Daily Times,44</i>)

>  And hardly any moving parts, just like a Gentle Annie. <i>(Jul 21, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),65</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>A TRAMP</b> A wondrous change all this since the days we `swagged it', trudging on ... up by the Molyneux, blessing `Roaring Meg' and sweating over the `Gentle Annie.' <i>(1891,Casual Ramblings,108</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> [?Otago /Canterbury] Not to worry that they were short of two fleeco's, that a truck had broken down right on a bad bend of the Gentle Annie ... - <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,4</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the name of a (sluggish) creek, specif. a tributary of the Kawarau River, Otago (also called `Annie's Creek').

>  <b>PYKE</b> There are three creeks in succession on this line of road - tributaries of the Kawarau River. The first is very small, and in a dry season merely drips from the rocks, and is called `Crying Jenny.' The second, which tumbles down from the mountains impetuously and with great noise, is `Roaring Meg.' The third, being a sluggish stream, has received, by popular assent, the designation of `Gentle Annie.' <i>(1874,George Washington Pratt,3</i>)

>  <b>HOLLOWAY</b> [Coach en route for Clyde] crossed Kawarau River - passed Gibbs Town - Drove over Annie's Creek. And on, till we came to within half a mile of Roaring Mag [sic, but check]. <i>(Mar 21, 1874,Journal of a Visit</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Amongst others who left [the Dunstan rush for Kawarau, c1862] were the members of a theatrical or concert party, and among them was a girl who sang a song called `Gentle Annie' and this name was sometimes jokingly applied to her. Going over the hills some of the rest were grouching, but she tramped on lugging her swag ... singing to cheer them on. The creek was called after her because of her brave spirit. That was said to be the reason of the name Gentle Annie, but I never heard how Roaring Meg got its name. There is a yarn nowadays that both were called after barmaids ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,161</i>)

>  <b>HISLOP</b> There are now several bonnie burns coming down the mountain sides to the river, the largest of which are the famous Roaring Meg and Gentle Annie; and a visitor ... may wash his colours of gold from almost anywhere while his wee wife boils the billy. <i>Ibid.,</i> 53 ... Roxburgh and Cromwell are well behind, and I slow down ... to enjoy the sight of the water gushing from the spillway of the newly-erected power station at the Roaring Meg corner of the [Kawarau] Gorge. This heavy stream, which breaks so suddenly on a stranger from its very rough surroundings, got its name (according to early-day tradition) from the fact that Meg, one of the two girls on their way to a dancing saloon at Arrow, created such a fuss at crossing this turbulent water that vibrations of her voice loosened rocks on the mountain side to add still further to the roaring of the stream and of Meg herself. There are different versions concerning the naming of this stream, and maybe some of this one is my own. ... A few miles further on the next stream to be crossed is the Gentle Annie, called after Meg's pal, who no doubt was a much quieter type of tourist than Meg herself. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,20</i>)

>  THE NAME Gentle Annie evokes many things - rivers, mountain roads, hills - in all parts of the country. Gentle Annie is an elusive figure in our folklore; sometimes an object of tender love songs, she is also known, in the company of Roaring Meg, as a colourful figure in the bars of goldrush Queenstown. <i>(Nov 3, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),133</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a long, slow hill, difficult to traverse.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Among other things Mr Brewer recalled having been on drives of 5,000 wethers and more from Mangaohane over Gentle Annie and right out to Napier, a distance of over seventy miles. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,143</i>)

>  <b>MCGILL</b> .. Other Gentle Annies crop up in the Nelson area, the Tararuas, on the West Coast, near Gisborne and on the Napier/Taupo road. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,34</i>)

>  I was going down the Gentle Annie and the brakes bust. <i>(Apr 2, 1956,NZ Dairy Exporter,116</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. topping a saddle - Wanganui's Gentle Annie - drops down into the Wanganui Valley itself. <i>(1972,In the Wake of the Axe,82</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> The Gentle Annie was a pretty rough road with a lot of hills and it was tough going. <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,55</i>)

>  <b>AXBEY</b> We hunted the area for about three weeks, then packed up and walked over the Gentle Annie to Te Mahunga Station ... <i>(1991,Along the Track: a collection of NZ hunting yarns,12</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> As we were going up the Gentle Annie [near Rai Valley] an old bull knocked up. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,43</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [Perhaps from a Victorian song (R. Mason's mother remembered a well-known song of the name): see discussion of n1. Perhaps the origin of n1, but perhaps derived from it. The only citation is from Otago goldfields. (Obs.)]

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> [Mother] was no Gentle Annie but gallant and mettlesome. <i>(1950,Otago Interval,8</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> [WWI] (See [Anafarta Annie](all-ari.md#anafarta-annie))

>  GENTLE ANNIE: A nickname for a certain Turkish gun at the Dardanelles. <i>(1925,Soldier and Sailor Words,104</i>)

### gentleman
 
 <i>n.</i> gentleman of the road, gentleman of the swag, in ironic use =swagger.

>  <b>STRONACH</b> The homestead was full of people : two parties of deerstalkers, one party of trampers - the word `hiker' had not then arrived - and a gentleman of the road. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,55</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> There were not the same opportunities here, so the class of wayfarers so frequently known as sundowners in Australia were always called swaggers in Otago. These `gentlemen of the swag' were not in evidence until after the gold diggings broke out, and after that time they seemed to call more at farmhouses than at the big stations, which were more out of the way. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,12</i>)

### gentleman colonist
 
 <i>n.</i> Ironically applied to one who was not seriously engaged in the pursuit of `colonising', `farming', etc. - with perhaps a connotation of `more money' than `experience'. [cf also gentleman farmer]

>  <b>ADAMS</b> It could not be expected that a vast and uncultivated plain should present an inviting prospect to the unpractised eye of a `gentleman colonist.' <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,19</i>)

>  <b>LADY BARKER</b> [get quot] <i>(1874,Station Life in NZ,74</i>)

### gentleman's club
 
 <i>n.</i> Commonly applied, usu. ironically, to a private club thought (often in its own estimation) to be socially exclusive.

>  I drank tea and spent the evening with them at Laday's Club, a very nice house indeed. There is also a gentleman's club here [at Christchurch]. <i>(Apr 3, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,30,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> ... on the third corner was what was known to the citizens by the choice name of the Gentlemen's Club. <i>(1935,Little Country, The,410,1937 ed.</i>)

### gentleperson
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Hdg] Gentlepersons' club. One of New Zealand's oldest gentlemen's clubs has opened its doors to women as full members. <i>(Jun 25, 1998,Dominion,3</i>)

### geo-gypsy
 
 <i>adj.</i> (Geology)

>  Leonore will stage in her capacity as geo-gypsy helium woman, commenting on the benefits of using helium-isotopes emitted in geothermal springs as a tool to understanding processes in the mantle and to constrain the lithospheric structure beneath the Central Andes. <i>(Jul 1996,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,110/47</i>)

### geoduck
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Geoduck (pronounced gooey duck).. is an unusual shellfish much venerated by the Japanese. <i>(Apr 1990,Cuisine,108</i>)

>  Smith played himself and was hired to front and direct a documentary about the fictional town of Ureroa which came under national spotlight because of its notorious residents - The Gooey Duck. The Gooey Duck, a shellfish with a long phallic-like siphon, was hailed as an alternative to Viagra and road-tested by Smith. Seated in a country tavern, Smith selected a battered love mussel from a pile of greasies, ingested it, clapped eyes on Jenny on screen, became viciously aroused, clutched his privates and rushed to the men's to relieve himself. <i>(Jul 28, 2001,Dominion,2</i>)

>  The geoduck (pronounced gooey duck) is the newest addition to our native aphrodisiacs - it certainly looks the part, but as to its performance, who knows? <i>(Mar 25, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),37</i>)

>  Well, we did at least manage two days [of birthday party], a follow-up dinner being necessary to finish up the leftover seafood, including a quantity of geoduck from Golden Bay. Pronounced "gooey duck", it is admittedly not the sexiest name for a little-known shellfish which, in its natural state, borders upon the obscene; it comprises a shell with a long fat siphon tube and really, ought not to appear in public without a pair of underpants. <i>(Dec 24, 2005,Dominion Post Indulgence,12</i>)

>  The town's monument to a local delicacy, the "geoduck" shellfish, was destroyed... The "gooey duck" (as it is pronounced) is discovered to be a ferocious aphrodisiac. <i>(Aug 4, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),77</i>)

>  Stephen started with the Golden Bay geoduck ("You know the duck isn't real duck," our waitress informed us sweetly), kumara and tua tua cake with yoghurt and garlic. <i>(Jan 1992,Metro,140</i>)

>  New Zealand has two native species of the peculiar bi-valve, named after a North Indian word for "to dig deep". The geoduck lives about half a metre beneath the seabed, it extends two long siphons up to the sea floor to act as feeding and water tubes. It is these tubes that are highly sought after in the gourmet world of seafood. <i>(Dec 2, 2001,Sunday Star Times,E3</i>)

### geognome
 
 <i>n.</i> geologist

>  Most of the geognomes spent the redundancy money on flights back to Gondwana, which was a short trip in those days. That is why you seldom see a geognome today except in the basements of universities. <i>(May 1984,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,64/19</i>)

### geographical
 
 <i>n.</i> see cit.

>  The homeless man cuts himself off from friends and family in the very early stages of the disease [of alcoholism], unlike his more settled counterparts. Often he does this by taking what is known in the treatment centres as a `geographical'. In their terms [sc. alcoholic vagrants'] it means continually making a fresh start in another town. ... Some reasons for taking a geographical are quite practical. ... Often those who haven't done many geographicals, or who haven't slept out much, don't think they are true alcoholics ... <i>(Feb 14, 1988,Dominion Sunday Times,19</i>)

### Geomusic
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Pacific Geomusic [title of review of ] The Ring of Fire by John Rimmer, first performed by the London Sinfonietta, October 12, 1976, Wellington Town Hall. <i>(Apr 1977,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,43/24</i>)

### Geordie
 
 <i>n.</i> [Extended, poss. confused, use of Geordie a coastal collier (see OED2 a.) and Tynesider (OED2 2 d).]

>  <b>WALKER</b> He was a regular `shell-back' skipper. He was a first-class navigator and a regular `Geordie' - coaster skipper - all knocked into one. <i>(1902,Zealandia's Guerdon,127</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a Scotsman [Bennett below is mistaken? (elsewhere a Geordie is a Tynesider)]

>  <b>CHRISTIAN SHIRRES</b> The sensation of alighting there [at Kurow, from train] was, to me, very strange: a great black mountain seemed to rise up at my very feet, and a few country `Geordies' were gaping around. <i>(Jun 1888,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1888 June Christian Shirres Diary in 1980 Neave The Land of Munros 53]

>  <b>BENNETT</b> A Scotsman is a Geordie ... also current in Australia. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,89</i>)

### George
 
 <i>n.</i> An early coll or slang name for a Maori chief, from George III, phs occas. applied ironically against the British king, as well as the Maori, by American whalers. [hori In early 19th and early 20th c. NZ, from George II, IV, V, VI a common name applied to non-European `natives', or adopted by them as names.] (See [hori](hom-hyp.md#hori))

>  <b>CRUISE</b> He was accompanied by a person to whom the whalers had given the name of King George [Te Uru-ti or Kingi Hori]. ... As he and the celebrated George of Wangarooa will be frequently mentioned ..., [one] may caution the reader not to mistake the one for the other. <i>(Apr 9, 1823,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1823 (1820 Apr 9) Cruise Journal (1957) 77]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>JACKSON</b> George, the driver (all natives being christened George), pulled on the reins ... <i>(1943,Passage to Tobruk,95</i>)

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> `Igri, George' - for every `Wog' is George or Abdul, just as the private soldier is either George or Johnny to the `Wogs.' <i>(1944,Troop Target,100</i>)

>  <b>MacDONALD</b> [The ghari] carries eight Kiwis, one of whom dethrones `George'. ... [These] chariot races ... [are] highly dangerous to the `georges.' <i>(1945,Away from Home,13</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> Tony hailed [the taxi]. `How much to Cairo, George?' `Forty piastres, George.' `Too much, George. Twenty.' <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,30</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Everyone, soldier and Egyptian alike, is known as `George', probably because our emperor is King George the Fifth or Sixth. He hasn't made much of an impact anywhere. `How did that Wog know my name?' each perplexed quota asks in every new reinforcement. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,260</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>REID</b> Couldn't you see, George? [to a strange digger] [fn] All natives were addressed as `George', and the name soon became the universal one for all strangers as well. <i>(1944,Turning Point, The,46</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [in post-war NZ the term had some currency until c1960 in address to a stranger or in rebuking a person (who has made a mistake, an exaggerated claim, etc.): e.g. Come off it, George!]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [WWII]

>  The copper or the soldier, / The schoolboy or the guide, / The driver of the gharry, In which you take a ride.. / No matter who, or what he is, / To us, he's just plain, George. <i>(May 24, 1943,NZEF Times,II/100 : 5</i>)

>  Talking about George (and one does a lot of it out here), it would be interesting to know why every New Zealander, irrespective of the wishes of his godfathers ... should be honoured by his sovereign's patronymic. The NZEF, not to be out-done, describes every Abdul and Hamid as `George', so that between the two the slogan `let George do it', once applied to a well-known New Zealand politician [sc. George Forbes] and to an even better known Australian comedian [George ?], is eminently applicable. <i>(Aug 18, 1941,NZEF Times,8/4</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a copper coin (esp. a penny). [ca1930 Blenheim p.c. Miss Gertrude O'Connor) Esp. in `I'm going to spend a George'; hence, `visit George', for a visit to the lavatory. obs.]

### George Speight
 
 <i>n.</i> [figurative]

>  Surprisingly coherent, it's the George Speight of wines; there's a bit of a wait before widespread tannin unrest lets you know how spurious was the initial sweet-talk. <i>(Jul 12, 2000,Evening Post,19</i>)

### geranium
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FEATON</b> Matuakumara [Maori name] .. Geranium [Settlers' name] .. Geranium dissectum .. Herb. ... Pinakitere [Maori name] .. Geranium [Settlers' name] ..Geranium dissectum .. Herb <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,178</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Gentiana saxosa. </i>

>  <i> Gentiana saxosa. </i> <i>(1888,NZ Country Journal</i>) [NOTES: (Canterbury A & P Assoc.) (And)]

>  Presently we may emerge from the forest upon some moor-like tableland in the heart of the ranges, where we again meet with a characteristic flora composed largely of heath-like Gaultherias, white gentians, blue ground-lilies, yellow forget-me-nots [fn <i>Myosotis australis</i>.], native daisies [fn <i>Brachycome Sinclairii.</i>], creeping veronicas, and white-flowered Pimeleas, with magnificent clumps of the larger Spaniard. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,188</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Geranium ... Traversii</i> ... Chatham Island Geranium. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,32</i>)

>  Chatham Island Geranium, Geranium traversii. Perennial herb with short silvery hairs densely covering the leaves and stems. Solitary flowers are produced in abundance ... <i>(1971,New Zealand's Heritage,1/267</i>)

>  ... for example, the Chathams geranium is Geranium traversii Hook. f., the abbreviation indicating that it was named by Sir Joseph Hooker. `Hook. f.' stands for Hooker filius, the son ... <i>(Oct 9, 1982,Press, The,15</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Gentian Griesbachii. </i>

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Gentian Griesbachii. </i> <i>(1908,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Report on a Botanical Survey of the Tongariro National Park (And)]

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>Gentian Griesbachii. </i> <i>(1909,AJHR,C-11</i>) [NOTES: Botanical Examination of the Higher Waimarino District]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> cut-leaved cranes-bill, jagged-leaved-cranes-bill, wild geranium.

>  <b>113</b> CUT-LEAVED GERANIUM (<i>Geranium dissectum</i>) is also called cut leaved cranes bill, jagged-leaved-cranes bill and wild geranium. It is found in waste places from Auckland to the Bluff, but is commonest in the north. It is a perennial, [A hairy annual] with branching, trailing, or semi-erect stems 1 to 2 ft. long. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,HILGENDORF,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>cut-leaved geranium [s] Geranium dissectum</i> L. <i>Ibid.</i> 28 <i>geranium, cut-leaved [s] Geranium dissectum</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,19</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Geranium dissectum var. australe </i> <i>(1906,Plants of NZ</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  Amongst the host of introduced weeds it is difficult for anybody but a botanist to distinguish the smaller and less peculiar native plants, such as the tiny yellow-flowered oxalis, the white-flowered native geranium [fn <i>Geranium microphyllum</i>.], the small white native heath [fn <i>Leucopogon frazeri.</i>], and others too numerous to mention. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,183</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Geranium microphyllum. </i>

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>(1908,AJHR,C-14</i>) [NOTES: Report on a Botanical Survey of Waipoua Kauri Forest (And)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>geranium, small-flowered [s] Geranium pusillum</i> L. <i>Ibid.</i> 70 ;<i>small-flowered geranium [s] Geranium pusillum</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,28</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> dove's foot.

>  <b>HILGENRDORF</b> DOVESFOOT (<i>Geranium molle</i>), or soft geranium or wild geranium or soft cranes bill, is common in all soils and situations, and has been considered a native. It is a diffuse much-branched annual or perennial, softly hairy in all its parts. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,113,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>geranium, soft [=] dove's foot [s]</i> <i>Ibid.</i> 72 <i>soft geranium [=] dove's foot [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,28</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>geranium, turnip-rooted [s] Geranium retrorsum</i> L'Her. <i>Geranium solanderi</i> Carolin

>  turnip-rooted geranium <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,28</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> CUT-LEAVED GERANIUM (<i>Geranium dissectum</i>) is also called cut leaved cranes bill, jagged-leaved-cranes bill and wild geranium. It is found in waste places from Auckland to the Bluff, but is commonest in the north. It is a perennial, [A hairy annual] with branching, trailing, or semi-erect stems 1 to 2 ft. long. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,113</i>)

>  <i>geranium, wild [=] storksbill [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,28</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

It is a diffuse much-branched annual or perennial, softly hairy in all its parts. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,113,1967 ed.</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [gerry](g-go.md#gerry))

### geriatric generals
 
 <i>phr.</i>

 <i>n.</i> Contemptible person; ingrate

### German biscuit
 
 <i>n.</i> Called Belgian biscuits after WW1

>  GERMAN BISCUITS ... Eggs ... Sugar ... Butter ... Spice ... Flour ... Rice [ground] ... Powder ... Vanilla Essence ... Salt ... Mix with ... milk ... cut into rounds ... While warm, place jam in between each two and ice the top one with white icing. Sprinkle pink sugar ... <i>(1910,Colonial Everyday Cookery,254</i>)

>  <b>TENDALL</b> GERMAN BISCUITS put two together ... jam between. Ice either with pink or white icing. <i>(1911,Kirkcaldie & Stains Cookery Book,15</i>)

### German cake
 
 <i>n.</i> G.C.s became known as either English or more popularly Belgian biscuits. [nf dicts]
