### German collie
 
 <i>n.</i> See cit. 1933.

>  German Collie: a variety of sheep dog which is blue (black and white spots). He usually has one or both wall eyes. (C.) <i>(Oct 21, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss]

>  <b>LILICO</b> She was what is known in New Zealand as a German collie but on the Borders they were called `Bilton Blues'. <i>(1934,Sheep Dog Memoirs,3</i>)

### German doggy
 
 <i>n.</i> See cit 1875. Compare Nelson huntaway.

>  <b>PEACHE</b> Towards the plain the sides of the mountain are steep and we sent some `German doggies' down to hurry the sheep. `German doggies' are what the shepherds call the stones and rocks they roll down. <i>(Dec 26, 1875,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1875 Dec 26 Peache Jnl in 1970 Gray Quiet with the Hills 39]

>  <b>PICKERING</b> There is immense satisfaction in seeing the progress of a great boulder smashing its way downhill. In the high country such rock tumblings were referred to as `german doggies', a cheap way, apparently, of persuading the sheep to head downhill. <i>(1988,Hills, The,48</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a rock sent downhill to move sheep while mustering in high country. (See [Maori huntaway](m-mar.md#maori-huntaway),[Nelson huntaway](n-new.md#nelson-huntaway),[Taranaki huntaway](t-ten.md#taranaki-huntaway))

>  <b>PEACHE</b> Towards the plain the sides of the mountains were steep and we sent some 'German doggies' down to hurry the sheep. 'German doggies' are what the shepherds call the stones and rocks they roll down. <i>(1975,Journal,39</i>) [NOTES: in GRAY (1970) Quiet With the Hills]

>  <b>PICKERING</b> In the high country such rock tumblings were referred to as 'german doggies' ... <i>(1988,Hills, The,48</i>)

### German ivy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Ibid.</i> 61 GERMAN IVY (Senecio mikanioides) Perennial climber with thin<i>German ivy [s] Senecio mikanioides Otto </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 40 <i> ivy, German [s] Senecio mikanioides </i> Otto <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,28</i>)

>  Khandallah resident Angela Potts says it is not right that the regional council doesn't have a campaign to eradicate the pest plant German ivy. <i>(Nov 11, 2003,Independent Herald (Wellington),3</i>)

### German macaroons
 
 <i>n.</i> [Were these abbreviated to macaroons after WW1? Cf German cake.]

>  GERMAN MACAROONS ... castor sugar ... ground almonds ... whites of 3 eggs ... juice ... lemon ... wafer paper ... Bake them in a very slow oven ... <i>(1906,Tetaka Kai: A Book of Well Tried Recipes,81</i>)

### German owl
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also little owl](lay-liv.md#little-owl))

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> LITTLE OWL<i> Athene noctua Local name</i>: German Owl. ... Between 1906 and 1910 several shipments were liberated in Otago whence other districts were stocked. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,185</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> The trees heaved and rocked in the rising wind; moreporks and the little `German' owls called from the macrocarpa. <i>(1985,Envoy from Mirror City,170</i>)

>  The introduction in 1906, of the European Little Owl, frequently called the "German Owl," and its subsequent establishment, chiefly in parts of the South Island, hardly compensates us for the loss of the Laughing Owl. <i>(1944,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),2(21) : 24</i>)

>  I dissected the body of a Little Owl, or German Owl. <i>(Mar 30, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,60</i>)

>  <i>Little Owl Athene noctua</i> (SCOPOLI, 1769) The little owl was introduced into New Zealand between 1906 and 1910; the first shipment was delivered to Otago from Germany. ... OTHER NAMES: <i>German owl, brown owl. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,258</i>)

>  The German owl, or little owl, was introduced into New Zealand to check the small birds pest. <i>(Aug 29, 1939,Taranaki Herald,6</i>)

>  When I was trapping I caught a little grey German owl. <i>(May 13, 1924,Otago Witness,73</i>)

>  The sinister note of the German owl heard recently near Christchurch reminds one that yet another blow has been struck at our native birds. <i>(Jul 18, 1927,Press, The,11</i>)

### German sausage
 
 <i>n.</i> [In general use from 1837 (OED2 4), NZ 1870, and known to Katherine Mansfield and Robin Hyde by that name:]

>  "I suppose you do not make German sausage now?" asked the chairman... "We call it 'luncheon', sir," was the reply. <i>(May 21, 1917,Wanganui Herald,4</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> LUNCHEON MEAT, sort of a low grade liverwurst. This is a South Island term only. It is the interesting to note that this stuff was called German sausage prior to World War I.[roman] <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,13</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> Sometimes, to make it more real, we had lunch together ... and sharing the slice of German sausage and a bath bun with loaf sugar in it. <i>(1899,Stories,7,1984 Alpers ed.</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> And in all the butcher-shops, pink-rinded German sausage, with the delicious little triangles of bacon fat, had become `Belgian sausage'. Belgium is brave little Belgium, one of our [WW1] Allies. It suffered a name change during WW1 to Belgian or Dominion sausage. q.v. -->(Cf. A.A. Roback Dictionary of International Slurs 35 German measles: ... during the Nazi War, the malady was popularly called `Victory measles' as if the original label were a compliment to the Nazis. <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,65,1970 ed.</i>)

>  The haggis buyer has placed it on the table raw... He had looked upon it as another species of black pudding or German sausage. <i>(Aug 25, 1916,Press, The,6</i>)

>  [Advt] May be German sausage is the titbit you most prize / Or else you on polonies dote. or count your earthly joys / By occasional refreshment in the way of saveloys. <i>(Sep 10, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,1</i>)

>  While at Wellington the police administered nothing but dry bread to their guests, Auckland came to light with German sausage! <i>(May 3, 1913,NZ Observer,17</i>)

>  Many fatal results have occurred from eating German sausage, which, having been cooked, has remained for a long time thus protected before being eaten. <i>(May 7, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,19</i>)

>  <b>BATISTICH</b> German sausage was still patriotically called Belgian sausage, but why it was thought better to eat sausage named after your friends I couldn't make out. <i>(1987,Sing Vila in the Mountain,71</i>)

>  He's been living on bread and jam, a pound of German sausage, and some eggs. <i>(Feb 26, 1910,NZ Truth,6</i>)

### germo
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] germo, germon 2094 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### germon
 
 <i>n.</i> albacore

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> ALBACORE ... <i>Other names: Thunnus alalunga</i>; germon. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,272</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> ALBACORE [<i>Thunnus alalunga</i>] ... Germon and longfinned albacore are alternative names. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,50</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] germo, germon 2094 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### gerry
 
 <i>n.</i> short for geriatric (person) [1984 14 F E122Wgton Girls C. 31 GERI [a sick person] -->1984 14 F E122Wgton Girls C. 2 GERI [a mad person]]

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> Lester Dipport despised the very old people of the party. He called them gerries. <i>(1982,Master of Big Jingles,143</i>)

### get a person wet
 
 <i>phr.</i> to have a person beaten, where you want them

>  He skites about in-fighting/ Stick to him, Mick, you've got him wet. <i>(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,29</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> <i>(1945,When the Wind Blows,40</i>)

### get a rattle on
 
 <i>phr.</i> hurry up; get a move on, (said to a person who is lagging in some way) (cf rattle one's dags) [ca1945 (Wellington) -->ca1950 V.O'Sullivan (Auckland Schools) cf OED 4c. dial and slang To make haste, to hurry off, to work briskly (1700-1883) from the general sense `To move, fall, etc, rapidly ...'; EDD [check] -->1960 17C M B6 St Bede's, Chch 6 RATTLE, GET A RATTLE ON]

### get a shine on
 
 <i>phr.</i> to get a move on, to hurry up (chiefly imperative) [ca1920 W.H.B. Orsman]

### get a spark up
 
 <i>phr.</i> to become excited, merry esp. with liquor) (See [cf. spark up](spa-sys.md#spark-up))

>  <b>BELTON</b> Today young men who intend going to a dance drink until closing time ... just to get a spark up, they say. <i>(1939,Outside the Law,50</i>)

>  <b>COLE</b> Can't get a spark up on beer tonight. <i>(1949,It Was So Late,15</i>)

### get a wriggle on
 
 <i>phr.</i> hurry up [very common in 1940s 1953 16 M A34 Thames DHS 6 GET A WRIGGLE ON -->1953 17 M A39 Thames DHS 6 GET A WRIGGLE ON -->1984 14 F E127Wgton Girls C. 6 GET A WRIGGLE ON]

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> Get a wriggle on, Rangi. <i>(1944,Troop Target,60</i>)

### get all over
 
 <i>phr.</i> to get/run all over (a person), to defeat, to best (a person in fight, argument, etc). [In this sense: poss. an alteration of knock a person all over the shop P8 pugilistic slang 1874.]

>  <b>WALKER</b> Being a new-comer he was at this station expected to box, and got `all over' his first opponent. <i>((9/0/1902),Zealandia's Guerdon,175</i>)

### get away
 
 <i>v.</i> Of usu. noxious vegetation, to spread unhindered.

>  <b>SARGESON</b> getting away: fern and manuka getting away, spreading unhindered. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,300</i>) [NOTES: Pearson Glossary]



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [land or pasture] to become run out.

>  They [runholders] are compelled to buy high-price stock, rather than allow their country to get away. <i>(Jun 1910,NZ Farmer,556</i>)

>  Baleage or haylage is made from a crop which has got away and is more mature (40-50% seed heads.) <i>(2002,Lifestyle Farmer,Oct/Nov 45</i>)

>  In seasons when feed is abundant and there is really no need for any special supplementary feed, the paspalum-paddock is likely to get away. <i>(Feb 1918,NZ Journal of Agriculture,90</i>)

>  Then, during the period between 1914 and 1919, certain areas on the station 'got away.' <i>(Jan 21, 1929,NZ Journal of Agriculture,16</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to draft and send stock for sale or slaughter at freezing works.

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> There .. sale stock to get away. [get away fat] <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,76</i>)

>  .. the last remaining lambs will be weaned after the last draft of milk lambs has been got away. <i>(Dec 20, 1928,NZ Journal of Agriculture,413</i>)

>  .. if the lambs are to be got away fat 'off their mother' they should be in abundant milk ... <i>(Sep 1, 1937,Weekly News,81</i>)

>  After the lambs are got away in December the ewes are held in a few hard-grazed paddocks to prevent them becoming over-fat. <i>(May 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,501</i>)

>  Calving begins in September, going on until mid-November, and the young stock is put on to paddocks that become available as the lambs get away fat. <i>(May 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,503</i>)

### get down
 
 <i>v.</i> To fall (bush).

>  `... You're getting some more bush down on that section of yours in the Arataki block ...?' `I have just let fifty acres to some chaps, and hope to get down another fifty or hundred acres next season if all's well. ... Those bushfallers ... were asking where they could get a regular supply of good butter, ...' <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,48</i>)

### get down on
 
 <i>v.</i> to appropriate; to take; to steal

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> get down on. To appropriate illicitly: New Zealanders': 20C. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang,324</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> Get Down On: does not mean `to go down on', although there are similarities. `I'm getting down on that tin of biscuits at the rate of knots? I'm EATING that jar of cookies very quickly.' <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,44</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> get down on to steal; eg `Let's get down on some of these stereos, eh?' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,48</i>)

### get fast/make fast
 
 <i>phr.</i> to make a successful harpoon strike; to harpoon [(whaling)In NZ records 1836; 1845 EJW; fasten to 1864] (See [also fasten](f69-fin.md#fasten))

>  2 mate got fast but was obliged to cut in consequence of receiving a blow from the whales flukes; lost an Iron, and about 10 fathoms of line. <i>(May 14, 1836,Piraki Log,35</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> Here the Crescent made fast and killed but the whale sank and the gunsman was forced to cut. <i>(1968,Turn of the Tide, The,23</i>)

### get hitched up
 
 <i>v.</i> to get married (elaboration of general English get hitched)

>  Elsie and I got `hitched up' <i>(1911,Kiwi on the Swag,14</i>)

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> get hitched up <i>(1944,Troop Target,24</i>)

### get hold
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>BAINES</b> When we got `up' the first pig was keeping the dogs at bay, and as yet they had failed to `get hold', and no wonder, for ... he had lost his ears; ... <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,46</i>)

### get home on
 
 <i>phr.</i> land a blow on; get at successfully, score off. [?f.hide and seek: to reach home and outdo `he'; cf. P8 home 7. get home (to), to land a telling blow: orig boxing. But not get home on.]

>  Some months ago I wrote the `Lone Hand' I would not renew my subscription if they published fraud advertisements, and specified Kugalman's soap and a hair restorer. They replied, in the `Bulletin', that both articles were good, and said I would not use them because I was `incurably bald and hopelessly unclean.' Now see me get home on them. <i>(Oct 15, 1910,Maoriland Worker,2/3/2</i>)

>  Queen Mary objects to cigarette smoking by women, and, whilst a recent quest in a country house, got home nicely on two up-to-date dames who had smoked cigarettes in her presence without asking her permission ... On this occasion, after dinner, Mary stood up and knitted for a solid hour, until the cigarette smokers, pleading fatigue retired to bed. <i>(Jun 17, 1911,NZ Truth,312/1/3</i>)

### get hopping
 
 <i>phr.</i> get going, get cracking [1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 6 GET HOPPIN -->1953 14 M A4 Thames DHS 6 GET HOPPING]

### get in behind
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural, humorous) to concede, fall into line, or to respond to command.

>  The hats have the Lifestyleblock.co.nz logo on the front - plus Get in behind Lifestyleblock.co.nz on the back .. Love the 'get in behind' caption. <i>(May 3, 2002,Lifestyleblock.co.nz</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to heel [esp. as a command to sheepdogs].

>  <b>DAVIS</b> ".. Hey, you there, Darkie, you leathery lump of lightning. Get in behind." <i>(1962,Mutton on the Menu,77</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> Every now and then a man will shout 'get in behind, Dark', 'come out o' that, Ben' or 'Wareleggo, Maid.' <i>(1951,NZ High Country,20</i>)

>  <b>DAVIS</b> "Get in behind there." Uncle Walter's dogs were showing a marked desire to chase a startled ewe. <i>(1962,Mutton on the Menu,22</i>)

>  <b>DICK</b> Life had been tame for the dogs for many miles, so, heedless of their masters' cries to 'Wayleggo there, Tip', 'Get in behind, Jock ...' they rushed madly hither and thither. <i>(1964,High Country Family,64</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> The only speech would be a sharp command to dogs to 'get in behind' or some muttered oath as a foot slipped in mud or tripped on rock. <i>(1974,Kingdom in the Hills,86</i>)

>  [Hdg] Get in behind!... Australian triallists don't have huntaways (those loud barking dogs) and, while in New Zealand the trials use a combination of human and dog, the Aussies have the dog do all the work. <i>(Sep 9, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

### get in on
 
 <i>v.</i> ?Do a person out of; con a person out of. [nf dicts: phs] a var. of OED2 61 b. get in with become familiar with, etc [??OMIT]

>  'I've no doubt the old josser who got in on me for my `print' makes a practice of getting his paper that way every day - it looked like it from what the other fellow in the carriage told me. <i>(Jan 21, 1911,NZ Truth,291/6/5</i>)

### get in on a sparrows' ticket
 
 <i>phr.</i> gain free admission

>  The Brothers Fuller entered the hall.. one paying his cash at the door and the others getting in on "sparrows' tickets." <i>(Oct 13, 1917,NZ Truth,5</i>)

### get in tow
 
 <i>phr.</i> [get involved with]

>  He had a real good old bender, and, naturally, got in tow with a tart. <i>(Sep 18, 1909,NZ Truth,7</i>)

>  Next he got in tow with a Petone barmaid and used to be away from home for a week on end. <i>(May 30, 1914,NZ Truth,14</i>)

### get intercoursed
 
 <i>exclam.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> get intercoursed! euphemism for `get fucked!'; eg `Can't you see I'm busy. Why don't you go and get intercoursed!' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,49</i>)

### get into
 
 <i>v.</i> to wade into, attack (food, work, a problem, etc); to get `stuck in'

>  Watty's quick at the start - gets into his log quicker 'n Dave. <i>(1916,Wowser, The,47</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> We got into a feed at a boarding house <i>(1939,Camps, Tramps and Trenches,11</i>) [NOTES: 1917 Feb 1 in 1939 Miller Camps Tracks and Trenches 11]

### get into gear
 
 <i>phr.</i> get moving (See [get one's arse into gear](g-go.md#get-ones-arse-into-gear))

>  Doom reckoned I should go because I could run the fastest. Before any of us could get into gear, Mrs Douglas came into the woolshed ... <i>(Dec 1986,Marist Messenger,36</i>) [NOTES: Joy Cowley]

### get it on with
 
 <i>v.</i> to have a (gay) affair with [?women prisoners']

>  <b>BEATON</b> HELEN: ... You know, I'm too scared to talk to half the women in here just in case I get mugged for gettin' it on with someone's girl. <i>Ibid </i>86 SANDY: ... Although, last year was grouse when the Virgin Mary got stuck into Joseph for tryin' to get it on with one of them Wise Kings. <i>Ibid </i> Glossary 109 Get it on with: to have an affair with; fuck. <i>(1984,Outside In,50</i>)

### get jinx
 
 <i>exclam.</i> [(in 45s)]

>  <b>McGILL</b> Old Calamity was an early one-man band of fiddle, horn, cymbals and kettledrums. Gambling was poker of 45s, a diggers' card game from California involving legs, queer suits and Maggie, cries of `Get Jinx!' and `Go for the Doctor!' <i>(1980,Ghost Towns of NZ,127</i>)

### get Karitanied
 
 <i>phr.</i> become pregnant

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Plunk a baby; get plunked; get trubied; get karitanied</i>. To go into a maternity home and have one's baby there hence, to become pregnant: New Zealand: C. 20. B., 1941. The first (<i>plunk a baby</i>) refers to the Plunket Society, as obviously does the derivative second; the third refers to Sir Truby King, noted for sage advice upon, and sustenance for, the feeding of infants; the fourth comes ex Karitane Home. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang,1139</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>get Karitanied</i> get pregnant; after Karitane hospitals of the Plunket Society, where new Mums are looked after. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,49</i>)

### get mobile
 
 <i>phr.</i> to get going, hurry up (also as exclamation) [Jim Cameron writes : Applied to people and animals. I can find no dictionary entry for this expression. It was common circa 1970s, perhaps mainly among children, but may now be obsolete. 1951 14 M 10 Wellington H.S. 6 GET MOBILE -->1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 6 GET MOBILE -->1953 16 M A34 Thames DHS 6 GET MOBILE]

>  Many of the new expressions [from the 2NZEF] are self explanatory. `Get mobile', for instance, needs no interpreter to explain that the Sergeant means by it that he would like a speedy move. <i>(Jan 28, 1944,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>) [NOTES: L.R.Hobbs]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> `Speak for yourself,' he says, loking impatiently at the watch he took from a prisoner at Gambbettolo. `Wish they'd get mobile.' <i>(1970,On the Ball,134</i>)

### get off
 
 <i>v.</i> (See [bike](../B#bike),[grass](go-gra.md#grass),[horse](hom-hyp.md#horse))

### get off one's bike
 
 <i>phr.</i> to become excited or angry; hence don't get off your bike! - don't get excited or angry, do your scone, go to town, [also 1953 15 M A26 Thames DHS 15 DON'T GET OFF YOUR BIKE [don't wait, expressing a short time] ??] (See [cf. get off one's horse](g-go.md#get-off-ones-horse))

>  <b>BENNETT</b> `Don't get off your bike' (from `don't get off your horse', via a music-hall song?) means `don't get rattled, excited' <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ</i>)

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> Don't get off your bike, Jock. <i>(1944,Troop Target,75</i>)

>  <i>bike ... 3. get off one's bike. Austral.</I> <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,104</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 68; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 47]]

### get off one's horse
 
 <i>phr.</i> become angry; do one's block, get off one's bike.

>  <b>JACKSON</b> We duly told [the drunken, irritable soldier] not to get off his horse. <i>(1943,Passage to Tobruk,68</i>)

### get off the grass
 
 <i>exclam.</i> an exclamation of disbelief, disapproval, or dismissal; equivalent to "stop pulling my leg" and "no way" [1984 16-17 F E3 Pakuranga Coll. 35B GET OFF THE GRASS [F4M4]]

>  Press for Service `I got you to write it in the best traditions of journalism ...' `Aw, get off the grass, Alan.' <i>(May 24, 1986,TV1</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> get off the grass! scornful reaction; eg `You're putting me on. You reckon he'll be All Black fullback before he leaves school? With all the jokers they have for the position? Get off the grass!' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,49</i>)

>  Come on Gents, Get off the Grass [headline] John Stewart usually has talked so much sense ... that it is hard to believe he uttered utter nonsense at the New Zealand Rugby Union council meeting ... <i>(Aug 14, 1988,Sunday Star (Auckland),C1</i>)

### get on
 
 <i>v.</i> to instigate a drinking session; often get on it.

>  He saw some of the barmaids at the Cri. `get on' occasionally. On one occasion a bottle of cham was brought to his room, but the barmaid who called in was only there a couple of minutes. <i>(Sep 21, 1907,NZ Truth,118/5/3</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> I had shaken him off as soon as we arrived in London on the U.K. leave scheme from Italy because, when he gets on it, he is likely to put a man in crook with the provosts. <i>(1970,On the Ball,134</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> get on it dedicated drinking of alcohol; eg `Is that you Colin? Hey, what d'ya say we get on it tonight?' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,49</i>)

### get on like a bush fire
 
 <i>phr.</i> var. of <i>get on like a house on fire</i>, of people, to be on very good terms

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> But what about Sally Smith? She likes dancing with me. We get on like a bush fire. <i>(1964,Walk on the Beach,162</i>) [NOTES: (Not for a Seagull)]

### get on one's bike
 
 <i>phr.</i> to make a move

>  <b>Slatter</b> Never leaving a bloke alone. So I got on my bike and went down the road. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,21</i>)

### get one snouted
 
 <i>phr.</i> to bear a grudge against, have it in for

>  Seems to me [the boss] has got me `snouted' / What it's for, hell only knows. / For I'm never off the stringer - / Spare me days and stone the crows. <i>(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,30</i>)

### get one's a into g
 
 <i>phr.</i> (informal) get one's arse into gear, get going

>  <b>MITCALFE</b> Come on, come on, get your A into G. <i>(1983,Pighunter,14</i>)

>  GET YA `A' INTO `G' <i>(Mar 16, 1984,Ephemera,6</i>) [NOTES: 16 F E13 Pakuranga Coll.]

### get one's arse into gear
 
 <i>phr.</i> get going [[nf P8: ?arse = insolense; nonsense.]-->ca1940s Wellington 9. [nf P8 arse gear get.] get one's arse into gear (often imp.), (to) get moving. (Cf. a into g).]

>  As it has been noted by the Publications Board, that Cappicade did not make a large profit. Gyles get your arse into gear and produce a mag that everybody will want to buy, do not drag up yesterday's sick jokes. <i>(Sep 19, 1973,Unknown,18</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> Get your arse into gear and do a bit of work. <i>(1985,Secret Diary of a Telephonist,82</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> arse into gear getting going or organised, usually as rude advice; eg `If you don't get your arse into gear, we'll never make it on time.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,10</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> `C'mon, fella - get your arse into gear.' <i>(1989,Frigate Bird,70</i>)

### get one's bush legs
 
 <i>phr.</i> adaptation of get one's sea-legs

>  <b>COWAN</b> The recruits from Wellington know nothing of the bush ...They were falling over logs and vines; a man needs to get his bush-legs just as a sea passenger needs to get [211] his sea-legs; he must learn where to place his feet andhow to get through the bush with the greatest speed and the least trouble. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/210,1983 ed.</i>)

### get one's hair off
 
 <i>phr.</i> [inversion of 'keep one's hair on'?]

>  The Borough Council naturally got its hair off and started talking to everyone about it, but they failed to get the City Council and the St Clair Borough Council to join the St. Kilda body in buying the sections, and it therefore gave the thing best.(?) <i>(Apr 2, 1910,NZ Truth,249/4/7</i>)

### get one's kite out
 
 <i>phr.</i> to become angry, infuriated (cf. get one's shirt out, become shirty) [ca 1935 (and earlier) Jane Morison. 3. Airs, side, esp. to put on kite. ca 1940 W.H.B. Orsman `used about WW1 times'] (See [cf. kitey](kee-kiw.md#kitey))

### get one's mocker up
 
 <i>phr.</i> to become angry, to get one's temper up. b. to put up one's fists preparatory to fighting. (This may be a variant of get one's monkey up)

### get one's rag out
 
 <i>phr.</i> to lose one's rag/temper

>  He refused to hand it over to Clara, and that lady got her rag out and said `If you don't get out of the house by 4 o'clock I'll pitch your things into the gutter.' <i>(Sep 5, 1908,NZ Truth,168/4/7</i>)

>  <b>PICARD</b> The big burly elephant got his rag out here, and threatened to do things. <i>(1906,Some Ups and Downs in NZ&A,9</i>)

### get one's shirt out
 
 <i>phr.</i> to become angry

>  <b>PICARD</b> Jumbo (the bloke that got his shirt out with the Yank [earlier rag] ...) now does his turn. <i>Ibid.</i> 29... he got his shirt out, did Bill, and threw a cold, damp, .. <i>(1906,Some Ups and Downs in NZ&A,13</i>)

### get one's tits in a tangle
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>BEATON</b> DI: Don't get your tits in a tangle, Ma, No harm meant and none taken, ... <i>(1984,Outside In,36</i>)

### get plunked
 
 <i>phr.</i> get pregnant

>  <i>get plunked</i> get pregnant; by association with the Plunket Society, which looks after pregnant mums if required. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,49</i>)

### get rooted
 
 <i>exclam.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> get rooted! vigorous objection; eg `Me play Aussie rules? Ahh. Get rooted!' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,49</i>)

### get stuck in
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  Knock it off, pack a sad, have a nice one Get stuck in, boots and all, have a coke, Half a shake, how ya doin', have a drop, of mother's ruin Hell's bells, blow that for a joke. <i>(Dec 2004,NZ Memories,No 51, p19</i>)

>  <b>MAHY</b> 'Get stuck in mate, and you can move the world,' said Selsey Firebone. <i>(1992,Underrunners,45</i>)

### get taped out
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>CHERRILL</b> You seem to have got this fool idea pretty well taped out. <i>(1950,Story of a NZ Sheepfarm,134</i>)

### get the crack
 
 <i>phr.</i> to be beaten up

>  You did see the odd person getting the crack [beaten up] here and there. <i>(Nov 2, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),38</i>)

### get the nips on to
 
 <i>phr.</i> to obtain, get hold of (?nips short form of nippers `fingers')

>  <b>DAVIN</b> We were all pretty plastered and we got the nips on to some more beer. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,5</i>)

### get the shove on
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  They didn't really meet us when we got the shove on them. <i>(May 30, 1993,Evening Star,40</i>)

### get the spike
 
 <i>phr.</i> to get piqued

>  The brigades men were not asked to come into the wet, and got the spike hot heavy and sudden. They said they reckoned they had a right to be present at the booze-oh and made dreadful threats about resigning. <i>(Jan 8, 1910,NZ Truth,237/4/6</i>)

>  Warriors, Wahines, Kuri, Poaka, Tamariki, Kumeras, Karos, Interpreters (1st, 2nd and 3rd grade), Poets Laureate, Poi Dancers, Guides, &c. on application to the Registrar. ...WAIPIRO - - An acursed drink ... TAIHOA - - The Police have the matter in hand POAKA - -a pig PUKEKO A bokakker out of season KORERO A long talk UTU - - The spike HAPU - - A Tribe KUMERA - - A sweet Spud <i>(1913,Auckland University Carnival Book,27</i>)

### get the strength of
 
 <i>phr.</i> understand

>  One would commonly hear it said among the boys that they couldn't get the strength of Spots [sc. a master]. <i>(1965,Memoirs of a Peon,164</i>)

### get the toot
 
 <i>phr.</i> of stock, to be poisoned by eating tutu (See [also tooted](ten-tot.md#tooted),[tutued](tot-tyr.md#tutued))

>  When we got back to camp we found 13 or 14 bullocks had got the `toot' - i.e. had eaten tutu and so poisoned themselves. <i>(Apr 19, 1864,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/107</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

### get to
 
 <i>v.</i> ?to attack; `get stuck into' (a person)

>  It was shown that another youth named William Thomas Turner followed the arrested one, yelling, `Get to the cops!' `Pull the coat off the cops!' etc., ... <i>(Aug 26, 1911,NZ Truth,322/5/8</i>)

### get trubied
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>BAKER</b> ...to plunk a baby, with further extensions in to get plunked, to get trubied... <i>((9/0/1941),NZ Slang,58</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Plunk a baby; get plunked; get trubied; get karitanied.</i> To go into a maternity home and have one's baby there hence, to become pregnant: New Zealand: C. 20. B., 1941. The first (<i>plunk a baby</i>) refers to the Plunket Society, as obviously does the derivative second; the third refers to Sir Truby King, noted for sage advice upon, and sustenance for, the feeding of infants; the fourth comes ex Karitane Home. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1139</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>get Trubied </i>get pregnant; after Sir Truby King, founder of the Plunket Society. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,49</i>)

### get under
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  Being early in the night many people were about, and the fire was got under. <i>(Jun 2, 1880,NZ Times,2g</i>)

### get up
 
 <i>v.</i> to prepare (wool) for sale.

>  <b>PAUL</b> The sorting and `getting up' of the wools have not hitherto been sufficiently attended to. <i>(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,40</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> For some time New Zealand wool was sent to this country very badly got up, partly from carelessness, partly from inexperience of the sheep-farmers ... <i>(1858,Notes on Sheepfarming in NZ,6</i>)

>  <b>SMITH</b> On all large stations ... a wool-classer is employed at shearing-time to `get-up' the clip. <i>(1927,Sheep & Wool Industry,80</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to prepare a law case; cf get up wool.

>  <b>MOSER</b> I hardly need say that the case was thoroughly `got up'. <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,52</i>)

### get with
 
 <i>v.</i> have a crush on, have lesbian relations with

>  When I was 15 girls didn't "get with" other girls. At my school if you were lesbian, you'd have tried to cover it up. But now, apparently, girls often get with girls at parties. It's a style thing. <i>(Jan 28, 2001,Sunday Star Times,D2</i>)

### get your hair cut
 
 <i>exclam.</i> [Jane Godfrey notes that Truth (1910 first half of year) tells about a kid in Hastings yelling out to mark Twain during his visit (and who had just died about the time of Truth's article) `Get your hair cut'. Twain immediately made for the nearest barber's and obliged. (Truth says the story is apocryphal.)]

>  <i>(Aug 20, 1977,Dominion,11/1-3</i>) [NOTES: ... one of the catchcries of the period. There were many such like `what-ho she bumps', `get your hair cut', `does you mother know you're out', `now we shan't be long' and the enigmatic `Pip Pip'.]

### get-out
 
 <i>n.</i> a set of clothes; a rig-out.

>  <b>McGILL</b> get-out set of clothes. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,49</i>)

### get-well, on the
 
 <i>n.</i> on the mend

>  He got a bit sick and lost a bit of weight, but he seems to be on the get-well. <i>(Aug 17, 2003,Sunday Star Times,A4</i>)

### get/come into holts
 
 <i>phr.</i> to get/come to grips or blows or into conflict (with) [ca1935 Havelock children : the common pronunciation or word for a `wrestling hold']

>  <b>McLean</b> When one got into holts with `Mickey' ... he would retreat .... <i>(1964,Willie Away,45</i>)

>  Then the Madigan Bros. came along and Lottie loosed her tongue and turned the vials of her wrath on Charlie Madigan, with the result that they got into `holts'. Though the constable separated them several times they fought like tigers, ... <i>(May 7, 1910,NZ Truth,254/7/5</i>)

>  Mr Lundon cross-examined witness at length and with determination, and as a result got into holts with Beak Kettle, more than once. <i>(Jul 3, 1909,NZ Truth,210 /8/1</i>)

>  <b>CHURCH</b> When we get to holts with the Germans it will be a close thing. <i>(1916,Tonks,267</i>)

>  Last week there came a new development, when the New Zealand Rugby League council came into holts with the corporation. <i>(Aug 1, 1964,Sports Post,6</i>)

### get/have (one's) weights up
 
 <i>phr.</i> to have a person's measure

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> `I'm always asking you something, and you always reply you don't know.' I laughed. `Well, Dicky, you have got my weights up, haven't you?' <i>(1978,Twilight Hour,38</i>) [NOTES: nf P]

### get/have one's wool combed
 
 <i>phr.</i> to get a scolding or thrashing [ca1938 Havelock I'll comb your wool for you if you're not careful]

>  <b>NESFIELD</b> I expect he got his `wool combed' that evening, when he summoned up pluck to go home. <i>(1881,Chequered Career,109</i>)

### getting up
 
 <i>n.</i> the preparing of wool for sale. (See [get up v](g-go.md#get-up))

>  <b>WELD</b> the `sorting' [3] and 'getting up' of the wools has not hitherto been sufficiently attended to. <i>(1851,Hints to Intending Sheep-Farmers in New Zealand,2</i>)

### gevuina nut
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Southland and Otago growers of gevuina nuts will meet this month to discuss forming a national association. <i>(Sep 16, 2004,Dominion Post,C7</i>)

>  [Hdg] Gevuina nut growers to gather. Southland and Otago growers of gevuina nut trees interested in forming a national association are invited to a meeting later this month. <i>(Sep 2, 2004,Southland Times</i>)

>  [Hdg] Gevuina nut - a cool climate macadamia. Gevuina trees have been growing and fruiting in New Zealand for close to 50 years which is evidence of its fit to New Zealand conditions. This member of the Macadamieae tribe of the Proteaceae produces top quality, high value nuts. <i>(May 2001,Crop & Food Research Broad Sheet,No. 42/1</i>)

### geyser
 
 <i>n.</i> a geological phenomenon

>  At Wairakei there us a veritable valley of wonders - geysers that throw up hot water at intervals, some regularly, others continuously, and some occasionally. <i>(May 28, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a hot-water system, a caliphont

>  Geysers appear to be very short-lived in geological time. Today, you can find in the scrub at Whakarewarewa about 65 geyser vents but in historical time there has never been more than about 16 playing at the same time... We have destroyed about 150 geysers in New Zealand in the last few decades and we were on the brink of losing them all. <i>(May 4, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

### geyser egg
 
 <i>n.</i> Geol. definition

>  An actively forming apron of siliceous sinter surrounds the geysers and upon it are found New Zealand's best examples of geyserite and "geyser eggs". <i>(Oct 1984,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,66/49</i>)

### geyser field
 
 <i>n.</i> an area where geysers are concentrated

>  The geyser field at Whakarewarewa, in the upflow portion of the field, is unique in the southern hemisphere and has probably the highest concentration of geysers in the world. <i>(Oct 1982,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,58/41</i>)

>  If destroyed New Zealand will no longer possess a major geyserfield; ironically this unique feature is presently at greatest risk. <i>(Oct 1982,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,58/41</i>)

>  The Rotorua geyser field is especially precious as it contains the last remaining geysers in New Zealand. Geyser fields at Wairakei and Orakeikorako were lost because of geothermal and hydro-electric deveopments in the 1950's and 1960's. <i>(Jul 1998,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,116/3</i>)

### Geyserland Express
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Other passenger services to end include the Silver Star, the Southerner, the Kaimai, Geyserland and Bay Express. <i>(Oct 30, 2004,Dominion Post,A1</i>)

### GHB
 
 <i>abbr.</i> the drug Fantasy or gammahydroxybutyrate (See [rinse](red-roa.md#rinse),[wash](../W#wash))

>  GBL breaks down in the body into its better-known relative gamma-hydroxybutyric acid (GHB), commonly called fantasy, wash or rinse. <i>(Jan 19, 2006,Dominion Post,A3</i>)

>  A police search.. found a bottle with traces of ghb, 2040 cannabis seeds, the remains of a hydroponic cannabis growing lab, anabolic steroids and more ghb in liquid form. <i>(Aug 6, 2003,Dominion Post,A6</i>)

>  The biggest worry for paramedics was ravers on Fantasy - gammahydroxybutyrate (GHB) - who went into deep states of unconsciousness after overdosing. <i>(Sep 24, 2002,Dominion Post,A3</i>)

### ghoa
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [kowhai](kiw-kwi.md#kowhai))

### ghoi
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) kowhai Sophora tetraptera and Sophora microphylla. [an early corruption of Ma. kowhai]

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> .. sledged posts from Tylee's spur and cut some Kohi posts for the horse paddock. <i>(1926,Tutira,122</i>)

>  I load wood from Ghoi Bush. <i>(Oct 14, 1860,St Leonard's Station Diary</i>)

>  I remember nothing but a rather curiously-shaped gowai-tree. <i>(1860,BLANK ENTRY,104</i>) [NOTES: in BUTLER First Year (1863)]

>  Gowai.- A common spelling and pronunciation of kowhai (Sophora). [gowai; kohi] <i>(Oct 21, 1933,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),15</i>)

### ghooparee
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [kuparu](kiw-kwi.md#kuparu))

### ghost shark
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  Black cardinalfish, frostfish, ghost shark, ribaldo, rubyfish, sea perch, trumpeter, white warehou and yellow-eyed mullet have now been included... Other reductions to catch limits are to specific stocks of grey mullet, barracouta and oreo dory. <i>(Sep 12, 1998,Dominion,2</i>)

>  ...though bottom trawlers were mainly fishing for orange roughy, they were also catching other species that live near the bottom, such as grenadiers, ghost sharks and kina. <i>(Jul 19, 2004,Dominion Post,A9</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> GHOST SHARK (<i>Chimaera novaezelandiae</i>). This is related to the Elephant-fish, but is not common. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,60</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Ghost Shark <i>Phasmichthys novaezelandiae</i> (Fowler) (Syn. <i>Chimaera monstrosa australis</i> Hector, preocc.) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,398</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 398]

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> <i>Chimaera novae-zelandiae</i>, the ghost shark, ... differs from the [elephant fish] in having a blunt snout with no proboscis ... <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,202</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>SHARK, GHOST</i> (a) Scientific name: <i>Hydrolagus novaezealandiae</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Pearl Flake (fillets). (c) Other common names: None recorded. (d) Principal localities where taken: Banks Peninsula, Timaru. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,42</i>)

>  [species name] ghost shark .. [scientific name] <i>Hydrolagus novaezelandiae </i>.. [other name(s)] dark ghost shark, pearl fillets, N.Z. ghost shark, chimaera <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  Five specimens of the "ghost shark," scientifically known as Chimaera Monstrosa, were also forwarded to the museum, the fish being about 2ft. 8in. long. An interesting feature of these fish is that they have a filament on the end of the tail like a whip. <i>(Jul 1, 1921,Ashburton Guardian,4</i>)

### gi-gi
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [kiekie](kee-kiw.md#kiekie))

>  <b>MAY</b> Fern-tree tops and 'gigi-gigi' served as a substitute for cabbage and to some extent helped prevent scurvy. <i>(1962,West Coast Gold Rushes,290</i>)

>  <b>HAWES</b> 'Unfortunately.. by taking away the heart of the tree you do just that - pluck out its heart.' 'Everyone knows that,' said Feefi's mother. 'It's called the gigi. Take out the gigi and you kill the tree.' <i>(2000,Dream of Nikau Jam, The,39</i>)

>  The gigi flowers looked very pretty, also the clematis. <i>(Nov 30, 1920,Otago Witness,57</i>)

>  Have you ever seen and tasted our native bush fruit, the gigi? <i>(Dec 28, 1920,Otago Witness,56</i>)

>  <b>HAWES</b> Now Feefi got all his tree knowledge from his mum, so he can't have had all that much concern for nikaus, could he? Cos taking the gigi out kills 'em, see? <i>(2000,Dream of Nikau Jam, The,143</i>)

 <i>adj.</i> As a distinguishing epithet in names of plants and animals see: bully, buttercup (white), cutting-sedge, fern, lycopodium; fern, maidenhair; fern, polypody; forget-me-not, gahnia grass, cutty grass; grass, fescue; grass, veld grass; groundsel, knotweed, mignonette, milkweed, moss, nelly, petrel, rata, reed, rimu, rush, spaniard, spurge, toetoe, weta, woodhen.

### giant cane rush
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) an endangered cane rush, Sporadanthus ferrugineus , endemic to Waikato wetlands.

 <i>n.</i>

### giant discing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  We do a lot of giant discing and seeding and the economy of doing it with new gear is doubtful. <i>(Jan 8, 2000,Dominion,17</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

"The giant dragonflies, which are capable of catching insects as large as cicadas, are not as common as they used to be." <i>(Feb 19, 2003,Capital Times,2</i>)

### giant eagle
 
 <i>n.</i> the extinct NZ eagle, Harpagornis moorei.

>  Sir George Grey thought [the Hokioi] might be the extinct New Zealand Eagle, <i>Harpagornis moorei</i>, excavated by von Haast at Glenmark in 1871... A giant eagle, <i>hokoi</i>, features in several Maori legends. One describes a cloaked Maori moving through the forest being mistaken for a moa and carried off by the eagle. <i>(Aug 2000,NZWords,4: 5</i>)

>  Sometimes [moas] would get stuck in that swamp over there, and giant eagles swooped down on them with their talons. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/92</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### giant kokopu
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The other fish whose young end up in our whitebait patties from time to time is New Zealand's largest native fish, the giant kokopu. <i>(Aug 26, 2006,Dominion Post,E7</i>)

>  <b>McDOWALL</b> I guessed then that the young of the giant kokopu (Galaxias argenteus) could probably also be identified as whitebait.Ibid. 50 The fifth whitebait species, the giant kokopu, does not climb like the previous three... With growth, giant kokopu become increasingly stocky, becoming the biggest and stoutest of the adult white whitebait, weaching 580 mm in length. <i>(1984,NZ Whitebait Book, The,10</i>)

>  On the West Coast, the season runs from September 1 till November 14 to improve the minor late-running species' chance of reaching coastal wetlands for giant kokopu, or forest-covered streams for short-jawed and banded kokopu, and koaro. <i>(Nov 11, 2003,Dominion Post,A18</i>)

>  The stream.. is home to at least eight species of fish including the threatened giant kokopu or native trout. <i>(Aug 31, 2004,Independent Herald (Wellington),5</i>)

>  Inanga are the most common species, but whitebaiters may also capture the young of giant kokopu, koaro and banded kokopu. "Shortjaw and giant kokopu are described as threatened species. ..." <i>(Aug 17, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,4</i>)

>  <b>DENNIS</b> The juveniles of five species of galaxias make up the whitebait catch: inanga (<i>Galaxias maculatus</i>), koaro (<i>G. brevipinnis</i>), banded kokopu (<i>G. fasciatus</i>), giant kokopu (<i>G. argenteus</i>) and short-jawed kokopu (<i>G. postvectis</i>). <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,187</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Giant kokopu Galaxias argenteus</i> Widely distributed but uncommon; ... <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,48</i>)

>  [Hdg] Kokopu currency... Giant kokopu will gain exposure on this year's annual commemotative coin issued by New Zealand Post. The rare native fish gleams in gold plating on a silver frosted five-dollar coin. Giant kokopu grow up to 55 cm and were an abundant species before predatory brown trout took their toll. <i>(Apr 23, 2003,Capital Times,3</i>)

### giant moss
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Dawsonia superba</i> giant moss <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Of all the ground dwellers in the forest none equals in stature the DAWSONIA or KING MOSS (<i>Dawsonia superba</i>), which though native also to Australia and Tasmania, nevertheless reaches its largest dimensions in the forests north of Westland, more particularly the [66] kauri forests. ... The normal height ranges from eight inches to a foot, but many specimens attain twice that height ... Truly it deserves the alternative name of GIANT MOSS. <i>(1961,Flora NZ,64, 66</i>)

### Giant Nozzle
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HEINZ</b> Before the advent of the Giant Nozzles, small hand directed nozzles were in use ... The Giant Nozzles were made of cast iron and being swivel jointed could be turned in any direction on the face. <i>(1952,Prospecting for Gold,32</i>)

### giant petrel
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A fine specimen of the giant Petrel (ossifragus giganteus), the largest of all the true Petrels- and whose proper haunts are these tempestuous seas that rage around Cape Horn - was caught the other day at Moeraki ... <i>(May 13, 1878,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  Several specimens of albatross are represented, and of petrels there is a capital collection, one especially fine bird being a giant petrel caught in 1881 on the passage from Wellington by the engineer of the Huia. <i>(Dec 29, 1884,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  One case contains birds only, including a lordly, quaint-pouched pelican, a furry baby royal penguin, two Nelly or giant petrels, a white swan, a flamingo and a specimen of the rare white crane which is the subject of a Maori saying (the white crane is seen once in a lifetime). <i>(May 24, 1906,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  GIFTS TO NEWTOWN MUSEUM .. Newtown Zoo, rhea, kaka, Japanese bantam, and giant petrel. <i>(Apr 22, 1910,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  [<i>27. Macronectes halli</i> Mathews 1912] <i>Northern Giant Petrel Nelly</i>) ...ranging widely throughout the southern hemisphere. <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,20,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Northern Giant Petrel Macronectes halli</i> MATHEWS, 1912 ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Nelly, stinker, Hall's giant petrel giant fulmar. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,69</i>)

>  <i>Northern Giant Petrel Macronectes giganteus</i> (GMELIN, 1789) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Nelly</i>, stinker, giant fulmar. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,68</i>)

>  [<i>27b. Macronectes giganteus </i>(Gmelin, 1789) <i>Southern Giant Petrel (Nelly) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,21,1980 ed.</i>)

>  We headed up a great herd of nellies (giant petrel or breakbones). <i>(Jan 30, 1890,Otago Witness,15</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> OSSIFRAGA GIGANTEA. (GIANT PETREL.) ... The Giant Petrel, or `Nelly', as it is called by sailors, is by no means uncommon in our seas, ... <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,297,1888 2nd edn 2/225 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> GIANT PETREL <i>Macronectes giganteus Other names</i>: Nelly, Stinker, Black Molly. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,35</i>)

>  As we came out [of the Snares Islands], flocks of sea-birds flew and swam around us, and we headed up a great herd of nellies (<i>Ossifraga gigantea</i>, giant petrel or breakbones), and chased them awhile. <i>(1891,TrNZI,23/492</i>)

>  The noddy, the albatross, the quaker bird, the Cape pigeon and giant petrel (<i>Procellaria gigantean</i>) are probably among the birds seen and described. <i>(Apr 29, 1863,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

### giant squid
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  What is thought to be a giant squid, the largest ever seen in New Zealand waters. was washed up on the isolated Pipikaretu Beach at the end of the Otago Peninsula early this week. <i>(Jan 30, 1948,NZ Herald,8</i>)

>  [species name] giant squid .. [scientific name] -- [other name(s)] <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  There is only one creature known to zoologists which might satisfy the descriptions of the sea serpent, and that is a giant squid or calamary [sic]. <i>(Aug 12, 1933,Evening Post,18</i>)

>  Although they normally inhabit deep waters, giant squids are often reported to have been seen in the vicinity of Cook Strait, particularly off Cape Campbell, during the months of June and July. <i>(Nov 5, 1945,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  [Hdg] Giant squid to get plastic surgery. New Zealand squid expert Steve O'Shea says his treasured specimens of giant squid can sometimes be a bit of a flop. Out of its natural habitat, the giant squid Architeuthis dux tends to be limp and smelly. <i>(Dec 4, 2004,Dominion Post,A16</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Lyall Bay casserole. In 1881 a giant 17-metre squid of the species Architeuthis longimanus, fearsome enough to inspire an H.G. Wells horror, was washed ashore at Lyall Bay. <i>(1982,200 Years of NZ Food & Cookery,69</i>)

>  Its Norse name, kraken, comes from the word for a tree stump with its roots still attached - a remarkably apt description of <i>Architeuthis</i> (ark-ee-TOOTH-iss) <i>dux</i>, the giant squid, largest invertebrate on Earth. <i>Ibid.</i> 35 Giant squid inhabit the sunless depths up to a kilometre below the surface. They are well adapted to the perpetual ocean night - their eyes, with a prominent dark iris, are the largest of any living creature, about the size of a human head. <i>(Jul 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 58/34</i>)

>  [Hdg] Te Papa displays colossus squid. Wellingtonians can now see one of the deep sea's most elusive and rare creatures, a colossus squid, at Te Papa. Regarded as the biggest and most aggressive of the giant squid species, it will be displayed for two weeks before being returned to the non-display collection.... Te Papa mollusc collection curator Bruce Marshall said many people would never have another chance to see the colossus. It has rows of swivelling hooks on the tips of its tentacles and reputedly has the biggest eyes in the animal kingdom. <i>(Apr 25, 2003,Dominion Post,A7</i>)

>  It's bizarre, but though humans have been to the moon, no one has ever seen a healthy giant squid, living as they do between 300 and 1000 metres down... In the last year 23 giant squid, up to 12 metres long, have become caught in the nets of Hoki trawlers off the coast of New Zealand. "This is the first time there has been any kind of activity that can be associated with a giant squid," he says. <i>(Feb 2, 2000,Capital Times,2</i>)

>  Steve O'Shea doesn't have any children and if he keeps working with giant squid he doesn't think he's going to. Let me tell you something: giant squid stink. <i>(Mar 2003,North & South,79</i>)

>  Up to 20 metres long, with eyes as big as saucers, tentacles as thick as tree trunks and covered in suction cups, a complex brain and a powerful parrot-like beak, the giant squid, or archteuthis [sic], is the largest "unknown" animal on Earth, says expedition leader Clive Roper, of the Smithsonian Institution. <i>(Dec 28, 1996,Dominion,2</i>)

>  [Hdg] Giant squid Blake's first mission... [His expedition] will search for giant squid off the Kaikoura coast... They are determined to find what they jokingly refer to as "the giant calamari". <i>(May 23, 2000,Dominion,3</i>)

>  What was once called the fabulous devil-fish is now known to every schoolboy as the giant squid. <i>(Apr 21, 1883,Otago Witness,27</i>)

>  <b>FORCH</b> Both Kirk (1880) and Dell (1952) recognised Cook Strait as a "hot spot" for giant squid. However, most of the present specimens have come from more recently developed major New Zealand fishing grounds. <i>(1998,Marine Fauna of NZ,82</i>) [NOTES: Kirk (1880)TNZI 12 : 310-313; Dell (1952) Bull. Dom. Mus. 16 : 1-157]

>  New Zealand could be the mating ground for the elusive giant squid... "Our belief [is] that Architeuthis [the giant squid] migrates here to breed and that the juveniles leave New Zealand shores for traditional foraging regions." <i>(Oct 6, 2000,Dominion,10</i>)

>  Those who like excitement will no doubt find the story of how Pablo killed the grizzly, and the description of a diver's encounter with a giant squid to their taste. <i>(Dec 18, 1902,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  Six giant squid were dropped off by fishermen at Niwa's offices last week, more than appears [sic] to have been caught in the rest of the world in a decade... About 60 giant squid had come into Niwa in the last six years. <i>(Aug 28, 2001,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  Even the mighty humpbacked whale sounds deep tonight / Away from the savage storm-tossed surface of cruel waves / And giant squid born in the dark fathomless depths cower... <i>(Jul 26, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),60</i>)

>  What is thought to be a giant squid - the largest ever seen in New Zealand waters - was washed up on isolated Pipikuretu beach at the end of Otago peninsula early this week. <i>(Jan 30, 1948,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  But that equally terrible and better authenticated monster, the giant squid, is also likely to be held responsible. <i>(Jun 5, 1919,Ashburton Guardian,3</i>)

>  A Wellington scientist has made history by capturing alive 14 giant squid - though they were juveniles and barely 10 millimetres long. <i>(Mar 1, 2002,Dominion,3</i>)

>  I had an unforgettable sighting in 1963 while we were living at Puyseger Point Lighthouse Station in Fiordland. Two or there hours before a major storm hit I watched with binoculars for about 10 minutes the amazing sight of a huge sperm whale (estimated length 60 ft) leaping, time and again, almost completely airborne with his body surrounded by the tentacles of a giant squid. The tentacles appered to me to be almost as long as the whale, though possibly my memory tricks me. <i>(Apr 1997,NZ Geographic,No. 34/4</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a native of the deinacrida species.

>  Imagine yourself in the dim, damp jungles of the deepest Wairarapa, among broods of marsupial bears, the colonies of people-eating giant wetas, and the nomadic herds of gorillas. <i>(Jun 5, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),64</i>)

>  Island such as Somes-Matiu played a crucial role in the survival of giant weta... <i>(May 5, 2004,Dominion Post,A10</i>)

>  While a wide range of invertebrates is found on the island, including stick insects and our largest species of giant weta, confined to Hauturu.. kiore, the Polynesian rat.. keep most forest creatures at low densities. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/28</i>)

>  Here, the Mana and Stephens Island giant wetas rub shoulders with half a dozen other endangered species, all of them thriving. <i>(Oct 14, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),71</i>)

>  Meanwhile, a plate showing New Zealand's giant weta, also the small big-headed weta Hemideina megacephala Buller, catches the eye. The giant, Deinacride heteracantha, "has a body 4 inches long and long spiny legs; it is practically extinct. <i>(Dec 24, 1926,Evening Post,17</i>)

>  They also found a giant weta, said to be the largest ever found. <i>(Dec 5, 1934,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  Pretty soon, we were all ripping crispy winglets off kiwi and spit-roasting giant wetas. <i>(Mar 24, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  On the morning set aside for the filming [he] told us that the Kaikoura wetas had just gone missing. <i>(Oct 14, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),71</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> GIANT WETA(<i>Deinacrida heteracantha</i>). A large, fearsome-looking insect with a body up to four inches in length. It is now very rare on the mainland.. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,47</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> <i>Giant weta</i>, Deinacrida rugosa. <i>Adult female from Stephen's Island </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/636</i>)

>  Giant wetas, of which there are three known species, are rare ... <i>(Feb 14, 1984,Dominion,4</i>)

>  By 1990, the island had been declared mouse-free, removing the main predatory threat to threatened species such as McGregor's skink, the giant weta and the goldstrip gecko. <i>(Oct 12, 2000,Dominion,9</i>)

>  <b>FORDYCE & MACLEHN</b> Mana remains under the protection of the Department of Conservation and is now home to many endangered birds, insects and small native animals such as the gold-striped gecko, McGregor's skink, takahe, tuatara and giant weta. <i>(2000,Bay, The : A History of Community at Titahi Bay,33</i>)

>  The initiative follows DoC's successful eradication of kiore - the indigenous rat - from Little Barrier Island, which has allowed tuatara, giant weta, skinks and cooks petrel to thrive again. <i>(Jun 12, 2006,NZ Herald,A12</i>)

### Gib
 
 <i>n.</i> Gib(raltar) board [ORIGIN: abbreviation]

>  You overrun the budget and, oh dear - bare gib. <i>(Aug 25, 2004,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

>  <b>IVORY</b> gib gibraltar fibrous plaster board panels (NZ) <i>(1982,Pacific Index of Abbreviations,134</i>)

>  [advt] GIB/ STOPPING/ SPECIALIST <i>(Mar 24, 1987,Karori and Western Suburbs News,18</i>)

>  Winstone Wallboards Limited is the owner of a number of registered trademarks including the word GIB(R), which is often incorrectly used as an alternative to the word plasterboard or drywall as a verb or proper noun. Examples of incorrect uses of the word GIB(R) would be, "gib stopper, gibbing, gib stopping, or gib plastering." In these examples the correct term would be "plasterboard stopper, plastering, plasterboard stopping or plasterboard plastering". <i>(Nov 2, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

### Gib board
 
 <i>n.</i> [Gibraltar Board and Gib are trademarked names to Winstons]

 <i>n.</i> [trademark.] In America and sometimes Australia called gypsum board. A tradename for a special kind of gypsum plasterboard.] CCR notes: My info is that G B started as a tradename. ### Gibson girl
 
 <i>n.</i> a small radio transmitter-receiver. [A portable radio shaped to be held betwen the knees in an inflatable multi-person rescue dinghy, thought to resemble the upper torso of a corsetted or bodiced Gibson Girl. Poss. infl. in choice of name by `Mae West' for the life jacket. Not used in the Pacific to his knowledge, but in Europe by bomber squadrons and coastal command. Thus not NZ. nf dicts. (?WW2)]

>  [The yacht's] emergency radio, a `Gibson Girl' set, was unheard <i>(Oct 7, 1963,Evening Post,14</i>)

### Gibson's (wandering) albatross
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) .

>  <b>Gibson's (wandering) albatross</b> <i>Diomedea gibsoni</i> A large (6.5 kg) species. About half the world population of "wanderers" breeds on New Zealand's subantarctic islands. As in all Albatrosses, elaborate courtship rituals occur when mates are chosen. <i>(2007,NZ Geographic Poster,No 84 Mar-Apr</i>)

### gidday/g'day
 
 <i>exclam.</i> elliptical and var. forms of good day [1951 14 M 10 Wellington H.S. 11 GID DAY -->1951 14 M 10 Wellington H.S. 11 GITTA -->1951 14 M 10 Wellington H.S. 11 GOOD'Y -->1960 17C M B8 Wanganui Girls C.I 11 G'AY MAN ->1981 Auckland Secondary Teachers College (Goldie Brown Collection) gidday -->1984 16 M E63 Pakuranga Coll. 11 G DAY -->1984 14 F E131Wgton Girls C. 11 G'DAY -->1984 15-16 M E62 Pakuranga Coll. 11 GIDAY [M3] -->1984 14 F E137Wgton Girls C. 11 GIDAY -->1984 16 M E73 Pakuranga Coll. 11 GIDDAH -->1953 14 M A8 Thames DHS 11 GIDDAY -->1984 15-17 F E2 Pakuranga Coll. 11 GIDDAY [F19M9] -->1984 14 F E122Wgton Girls C. 11 GIDDAY -->1984 14 F E124Wgton Girls C. 11 GIDDAY [F7] -->1984 16 M E77 Pakuranga Coll. 11 GIDDAY (GUDDAY) -->1984 14 F E138Wgton Girls C. 11 GIDDAY (GUDDAY) -->1984 16 F E14 Pakuranga Coll. 11 GDAY OR HI]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> `Gidday, Owen,' I say. `See you in a minute.' <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,201</i>)

>  <b>MUIR</b> `Up you for the winter, mate. I got some booze to get stuck into now. I'll be bloody ripe by the time the friggin' railcar goes. No bullshit. Gidday, sport. You bloody bastard.' <i>(1960,Word for Word,255</i>)

>  I suddenly hated England and longed to be "home" in New Zealand where there'd be no barriers and I'd be able to say "Gidday" to people who'd understand my language and way of doing things. <i>(Mar 24, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),57</i>)

>  <b>BRAMBLEY</b> ... my Bill called to them `G'day! What you got in there?' <i>(1966,Sea-cockies of the Manukau,75</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> `Gidday,' I say, annoyed that of all the troops in the park he has to pick on me. <i>(1970,On the Ball,134</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> ... Erina ... glided past him with ... `G'day, Boss.' <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,5</i>)

### Gimblett Gravels
 
 <i>n.</i> a wine-growing area in Hawke's Bay

>  Without Limmer, there would be no Gimblett Gravels, that shingly region of Hawke's Bay that has assumed legendary winegrowing properties since Alan almost single-handedly saved it from death by quarrying. <i>(Jan 24, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

>  Mr Yortt became involved in the industry back in the early 80s when he and former Wattie's chief executive David Irving established the original Irongate (as in Babich) vineyard on the Gimblett Gravels. <i>(Jul 19, 2003,Dominion Post,F7</i>)

>  Gimblett Gravels is an 800-hectare piece of rocky dirt in the Gimblett Road/Roy's Hill area of Hawke's Bay.. Does Gimblett Gravels wine justify its already exalted price?.. Gimblett Gravels.. are between 2C and 3C hotter than many other winegrowing districts of Hawke's Bay. As a result Gimblett Gravels grapes tend to ripen earlier. <i>(Feb 8, 2001,Independent Business Weekly,19</i>)

>  He told his readers "Gimblett Gravels does look like something special:". <i>(Mar 3, 2001,Dominion,25</i>)

>  The karts.. provided a well-earned diversion from a venture that had consumed his every spare minute for the first seven years, not to mention the battle he led to prevent land like his - about a third of the Gimblett Gravels - being mined for gravel. <i>(Feb 21, 2004,Dominion Post,E7</i>)

>  Most of our wines come from an 800-hectare old river bed in Hawke's Bay, called Gimblett Gravels. <i>(Oct 2005,Air New Zealand inflight magazine,81</i>) [NOTES: -]

>  Late last month, a group Hawke's Bay winemakers launched their new marketing association, Gimblett Gravels, based on the patch of deep shingle soils west of Hastings, where they all have vineyards... Why have the Gimblett Road Gang decided to define themselves purely on the basis of their soil, and then claim terroir status? <i>(Feb 24, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

>  A generous Gimblett Gravels Hawke's Bay red that's chokka with blackberries and plums and chocolate and spice... <i>(Nov 22, 2003,Dominion Post,E7</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [appellation] a deep river shingle flats viticulture locality in Hawke's Bay.

>  As land alone has no obvious taste characteristics, there is nothing about Gimblett Gravels that sets the wine apart from Tutaekuri Shingles or Pakowhai Silts. <i>(Feb 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

>  We were shown a stony hole in the ground, treated to mean temperature charts...left little doubt that the Gimblett Gravels district enjoys a raft of distinctive terroir factors not shared by the rest of Hawke's Bay .. They [the wines] were chosen because at least 95% of their grapes were grown in the Gimblett Gravels district... <i>(Feb 2001,Capital Times,Feb 7-13 17</i>)

>  Last month, a group of 34 wineries launched the Gimblett Gravels group, a marketing device and a way of defining wines from around 800 ha of ancient riverbed. <i>(Mar 24, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35</i>)

>  His Trinty Hill ripe, nutty syrah from the Gimblett Gravels region of Hawke's Bay sells half that. <i>(2001,Destinations,Autumn 145</i>)

>  Gimblett Gravels' 34 wineries and vineyard-owners believe the appellation (district title) is the first of its type in the new-world wines for a soil type consistently producing high quality red wines from Bordeaux varieties to Syrah. <i>(Mar 22, 2002,Hawke's Bay Today,9</i>)

### Girl Guide biscuit
 
 <i>n.</i>

In 1957 it was decided to try out the Canadian and U.S.A. schemes for raising money by the sale of special Guide Biscuits. Messrs Cadbury Fry & Hudson consented to make the biscuits and share with Dominion Headquarters the responsibility of such a venture. <i>(1976,65 Years of Guides,64</i>) [NOTES: In the Guide literature of this time they are just referred to as Biscuits - especially in the financial statements. Jammy Shrewsburys, with their glorious chewy texture, and Girl Guide biscuits, which one ate in the time-honoured way of nibbling around the rim first, then working methodically towards the central medallion. <i>(Jan 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 61/48</i>)

### girl out
 
 <i>v.</i> to use femininity as an excuse

>  Too exhausted to don sodden gear and face yet another four hours in ice-cold rain, I girl-out on a stored sail and skip a watch, leaving the fleet-footed and seemingly fearless sailors to steer us expertly through the worst of the night, and re-emerge as Auckland comes into view on Tuesday morning. <i>(Oct 23, 2004,Dominion Post,A9</i>)

### Girl Peace Scouts
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>Paul van Herpt</b> In 1908 [Cosgrove] was instrumental in setting up the Girl Peace Scouts, an equivalent to Scouts for girls... With this background, Cosgrove would appear to be arranging for a joint meeting between Woodend or Rangiora Girl Peace Scouts and the Peace Scouts of East Belt methodist, sometime during or after 1908. <i>(Oct 2001,Captain Cook,29(9), No. 613 : 102</i>)

>  In 1909 Colonel Cosgrove, a New Zealander who had fought in the Boer War and had there met Sir Baden-Powell, as he was then known, and had talked with him about his ideas on Scouting, thought that the girls of New Zealand should have some Movement of their own. Scouting had already found its way to New Zealand, but there was no talk of Guiding for girls. So Colonel Cosgrove called his Movement "The Girl Peace Scouts"... The girls wore khaki dresses, which were very long, but no one seemed to mind. They had hats which turned up at the sides, carried staves on all occasions, and they always had a bugler! Troop flags and haversacks were also part of the uniform. The girls did not believe in anything too tame, and had fearsome emblems such as curlews, moreporks, and peewits for their patrols. The knots were three pieces of ribbon tied in bows, to remind the girls of their threefold promise. The enrolment badge was very similar to the Girl Guide badge of to-day, with the letters P.S. (peace scout) on it... In October 1923 came official word that the Girl Guides' Association of New Zealand.. had been duly registered in London with Imperial headquarters. <i>(1953,Thirty Years of Guiding in NZ 1923-1953,7</i>)

### Girls Khaki Brigade
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  One of the most striking examples of the various fundraising methods was the formation of the 'Girls Khaki Brigade' in Wellington. ... The 'Girls Khaki Brigade' were to help promote and entrust contributions to the "More Men and Patriotic Funds." Properly known as the 'Young Ladies Contingent' the unit was known by a number of variations at the time. <i>(Feb 2005,NZ Memories,No. 52, p. 62</i>)

### Girls' War
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical)

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> `In 1830,' he [S. Percy Smith] states, `an occurrence took place at the Bay of Islands which is very illustrative of Maori customs and which led to further Nga-Puhi expeditions against the southern tribes. It has been called the `Girls' War' for this reason: <i>(1932,Letters & Journals of Samuel Marsden,451</i>) [NOTES: 1932 quoted in Marsden Letters and Journals 451-452]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Ururoa and the natives from Wangaroa, Matauri, &c., assembled at Rangihoua to come against Korararika. [ed. fn] This is the beginning of what became known as the `Girls' War'. <i>(1830,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,155,1961 ed.</i>)

### Gisborne cockroach
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Elsewhere in the region, the Gisborne cockroach is making its presence felt around Rotorua and Hawke's Bay - with ants becoming a year-round problem in all areas. <i>(Mar 21, 2003,Dominion Post,A4</i>)

>  The roach, originally from Western Australia, was thought to live only around the Gisborne and Auckland areas but some appear to have found their way south. The family which found them in Upper Hutt thought they were strange `beetles' but National Museum entomologist Ricardo Palma identified them as genus drymaplaneta, commonly known as the Gisborne cockroach. They are thought to have lived in Gisborne since the late 1960s. <i>(Feb 6, 1988,Dominion,3</i>)

>  The Gisborne cockroach, intoduced through Tauranga port from Western Australia in 1954, took hold in Gisborne, spread to Hawke's Bay in the mid-1970s and appears to be heading south. ... About 40 millimetres long, they are dark brown with a whitish margin on each side. <i>(Jun 25, 1989,Sunday Times (NZ),1</i>)

### give 'em a taste of Kiwi
 
 <i>exclam.</i>

>  They have gathered familiar junk and detected in it something about our much-debated identity, the id hiding behind the "give 'em a taste of Kiwi" ego. <i>(Mar 3, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),50</i>)

>  Thousands of Queenslanders and expat Kiwis, cans of the sponsor's product held high in the air, all screaming, "Give 'em a taste of ... " <i>(Jul 22, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27</i>)

>  <b>WOLFE</b> New Zealanders, therefore, faced the prospect of having a cheeky carnivorous parrot sporting a kotuku badge and urging its compatriots to 'give 'em a taste of Kiwi!' <i>(1991,Kiwi,73</i>)

>  The same people who get right behind "Give 'em a taste of Kiwi", and present us with an over-the-top jingoistic version of the Commonwealth Games have reacted with fear and horror at the prospect of devoting a piddling 20 percent of our radio airtime to music produced in this country by those Kiwis that they're otherwise so keen on getting behind. <i>(Apr 9, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),107</i>)

### give (it) best
 
 <i>phr.</i> (occas. give the game best) to give up, abandon an occupation or activity; to acknowledge defeat.

>  The Borough Council naturally got its hair off and started talking to everyone about it, but they failed to get the City Council and the St Clair Borough Council to join the St. Kilda body in buying the sections, and it therefore gave the thing best. <i>(Apr 2, 1910,NZ Truth,249/4/7</i>)

>  On again, then through Napier, the Patea country, in and out till I came to Maraekakaho station: when I took a pull I was by this time heartily sick of the game, and made up my mind to give it best, and came into Hastings. <i>(Jan 7, 1911,NZ Truth,289/4/5</i>)

>  give best v.i.-give best' sl. to acknowledge defeat; to give up. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,4</i>)

>  best, to give v. acknowledge defeat, cease pursuing an objective. <i>Ibid.</i> 9 give (it) best (Phr.) to give up hope, to cease trying. b. give the game best. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> He was twelve then and he mustered on that class of country - all walking country - until he finally gave the game best at Winterslow in 1958. <i>Ibid.</i> 168 (Glossary) Give the game best: To give up. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,87</i>)

### give a pay
 
 <i>phr.</i> to berate, criticise loudly

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> PAY (n) e.g. Give a -. Berate, criticise loudly. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,252</i>)

### give a rat's (arse)
 
 <i>phr.</i> care at all [Listed in Green as 20C.]

>  This brings us to the popular argument that [a new flag] will estrange us from Britain. Like they give a rat's. <i>(Jun 13, 2001,Capital Times,3</i>)

>  The few people who get paid to give a rat's about these things - journalists, politicians and consultants - are in a tizz, since the two television networks' polls, which normally register passingly similar trends, came out with totally different scenarios after polling at almost exactly the same time. <i>(Jul 3, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  Go and support and when you get the ball again, I don't give a rat's arse what you do. <i>(Jun 14, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

### give an order for the bank
 
 <i>phr.</i> to send (a player) off the field of play as a penalty, to send for an early shower (See [cf. give the order of the boot](../G#give-the-order-of-the-boot))

>  ... the referee was guilty of a grave dereliction of duty in not giving the suburbanite an order for the bank. <i>(Aug 19, 1906,NZ Truth,9/2/6</i>)

### give away
 
 <i>v.</i> to give up or abandon an activity.

>  <b>SYDER& HODGETTS</b> To stop' drinking' altogether. .in 4;`While you're having one or two (drinks) you haven't given her up. To give the grog away you mustn't have any.' - <i>(1962,Australian & NZ English [TS],410</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> If someone said, `I used to play the piano but I've given it away now', a New Zealander would join with other foreigners in thinking the speaker meant he had made a gift of his piano to some deserving person. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,126</i>) [NOTES: [Turner is mistaken here]]

>  give it away Austral. abandon or give up something <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,318</i>) [NOTES: nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng.Dict.; cf. 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 209]

>  give it away to abandon, often in exasperation; eg `Give it away, Sean, it's just not worth it.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,50</i>)

### give it a go
 
 <i>phr.</i> have a try (See [go](g-go.md#go))

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> You'll have plenty of time to learn to waltz.. if you start now. Shift the table back, Messenger, and give it a go. <i>(1981,Butcher Shop, The,71</i>)

### give it a pop
 
 <i>phr.</i> to make an attempt at it

>  [The traveller to New Zealand] might be ... told ... that he was `a melon' to have come out and `given it a pop'; ... <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The,18</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Pop, give a</i>. 2. <i>To give it a pop</i> is to make an attempt at it; to make a bet: New Zealand: since ca. 1919. R. G. C. McNab in <i>The Press</i> Christchurch, N.Z.), April 2, 1938. (In Austr: <i>have a pop at</i> = `Engage in a fight' B 1942) <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1140</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> pop: (an) attempt:<i> give it one last pop</i>, make a last venture. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,302</i>)

### give it lip
 
 <i>phr.</i> to call out

>  lip, give it v. call out. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),12</i>)

### give it the nod
 
 <i>phr.</i> plead guilty [criminals']

>  NOD (n) e.g., Give it the - . Plead guilty. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,251</i>) [NOTES: glossary]

### give it to the Belgians
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Belgians!, give it to the. C.p. advice to a man complaining about his food or clothing or inquiring what to do with some superfluity: New Zealand soldiers': 1916-18. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,45</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>give it to the Belgians</i> WWI advice to a comrade complaining of food or gear. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,50</i>)

### give one a pop
 
 <i>phr.</i> to engage in a fight, give (a person) a go; also WW1 fire at with a machine gun.

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> [The] following [was] employed by those who served in the [Great] War ... <i>give someone a pop</i>, to have a `go' at, especially in fisticuffs or in shooting with a rifle or machine-gun; <i>Ibid.</i> 287 He absolutely dropped his bundle, and, to make matters worse, I had started off with a duck's breakfast, but I saw a cookhouse and decided to give it a pop for a binder. <i>(1935,Slang Today,286</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Pop, give</i>. (a person)<i> a</i>. To engage in a fight (from ca. 1910); to fire at with machine-gun (G.W.): New Zealanders'. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,649</i>)

### give one a show
 
 <i>phr.</i> to give one a chance (to make good), give one a go

>  `I want to introduce myself,' I said [to the diggers]. `I'm going to have a service tomorrow .... I hope you will come.' There was a general response: `We'll be there and give you a show,' a bit of diggers' slang, which I found meant -' Here's your chance, can you use it.' <i>(Oct 6, 1866,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1866 Oct 6 in 1914 Harper Letts from Cby 102]

>  <b>CHAMIER</b> When these fellows started shingle-spliting, he asked them as a favour to give me a show. -->1905 Thomson Bush Boys 63 Give him a show [to tell his yarn] <i>(1891,Philosopher Dick,154</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> `Give the man a show, boss,' said Cranstown who occupied the next place on the board. <i>(1913,Sodger Sandy's Bairn,58</i>)

### give one arseholes
 
 <i>phr.</i> to revile, ill-use, utterly defeat. [This expression may be local or of high local frequency. ca1945 p.c. Wellington 1951 16+ M 32 St Mary's, Wgton 30A ARSEHOLES,GIVE HIM (BULLER)]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> And the only pep talk [the coach] ever gave them, all he ever said every Saturday, was go out there and give them arseholes. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,174</i>)

### give one dingbats
 
 <i>phr.</i> to drive one crazy

>  Lord! how the writer remembers that dreadful disaster when the Taranaki horse led the big field home and paid a big dividend, which gives me dingbats every time I remember it ! <i>(Nov 4, 1911,NZ Truth,332/6/4</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> The way she yelled gave me the dingbats. <i>Ibid.</i> 72 Being all on my pat up there ... somehow gave me the dingbats properly. <i>(1946,That Summer,56</i>)

### give one goss
 
 <i>phr.</i> to scold vigorously; to worry (as a dog does a sheep); to give pain. [ca1940 My crook back's giving me goss this morning]

>  <b>BAINES</b> We shall want his dog, for `Smut' here might come to grief if we fell in with an old boar, & no one `up' to give him `goss'. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,43</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> That's right, ole man, give `em goss. <i>(1914,Tale of Hown,173</i>)

### give one jaro
 
 <i>phr.</i> to abuse; to give (one) hell. [(obsolesc); ca 1972 W.H.B. Orsman had not heard it.]

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> Cookie'll give yer jaro 'f yur late fur supper. <i>(1904,Sons o' Men,42,1905 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Jaro, give. (a person). To scold, vituperate: New Zealand: C. 20. (O.E.D. Sup.) Prob. ex Maori iaua!, hold! or stay!; but cf. jyro (Addenda). <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,434</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> give jaro to scold (Maori `whauraura', to scold); eg `If that kid doesn't pipe down soon I'll give it jaro, by crikey.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,50</i>)

### give one Johnny up the orchard
 
 <i>phr.</i> to give someone what for. [ca 1939 Ruth Mason Wellington]

### give one rats
 
 <i>phr.</i> to give one a scolding, to become angry with

>  The daughter, Mrs Atholwood, who has been only a few weeks over from Sydney said that she called on her dear father and gave him rats for not providing her mother with money, even for trams to the hospital three times a week. <i>(Nov 3, 1906,NZ Truth,72/5/1</i>)

### give one that in
 
 <i>phr.</i> to admit in argument [Poss. a var. of OED2 give v. 59 a. give in ... that to admit ... that (under pressure of argument)] [ca1935 Otago p.c. R. Mason. I give you that in. `I admit that'.]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> As soon as it comes five o'clock I'm offski. They're hollering and swallering in the pubs right now. You gotta givit, those boys get away with murder really. <i>Ibid.</i> 162 He'll give it a burl, that boy, I'll give him that in - Bill's got two kids, I've got two kids. Level pegging, eh. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,85</i>)

### give one the evils
 
 <i>phr.</i> to give one the evil eye
