>  Often they will start a scrap for petty reasons: they don't like the way somebody looks at them. 'Are you giving me the evils?' <i>(Oct 31, 1998,Dominion,15</i>)

>  She believed people could read her mind, and described visions, including an armless man who floated up from the floor, giving her "the evils." <i>(Mar 11, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  Li Mei's giving me the evils. <i>(Sep 22, 2004,TV2</i>) [NOTES: Shortland Street]

### give one the go
 
 <i>phr.</i> to give someone the brush-off

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> Give yer the go, 'as she <i>(1905,Sons o' Men,231</i>)

### give one the order of the boot
 
 <i>phr.</i> dismiss, sack

### give one the ta-tas
 
 <i>phr.</i> to give one the fingers (as dismissive gesture) [spelt tat-tas in cit. (but from ta-ta = goodbye)]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> He gives me the tat-tas so I hoed into him. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,161</i>)

### give one the wheeze
 
 <i>phr.</i> to tip one off

>  <b>HAUGHEY</b> ... to use a colloquialism, `gives them the wheeze' as to who are on board. <i>(1946,Railway Reminiscences,27</i>)

### give one their lollies
 
 <i>phr.</i> to give someone their deserts

>  When spoken to by police Julian admitted injuring the boy, who, he said, had tried to `worm in' on Julian's girlfriend. So Julian `gave him his lollies' ... <i>(Mar 19, 1977,Evening Post,3/3</i>)

### give one their running shoes
 
 <i>phr.</i> to dismiss, sack a person

>  <b>BAKER</b> To give a person his running shoes, to dismiss a person from office (also a political use), which is an extension of the phrase to get the run. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,53</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> `To give a man his running shoes' [was] coined by a New Zealand Minister of the Crown [Hon. R. Semple] as a vivid substitute for the English `sack' or the American `fire'. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,92</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Running shoes, give (someone) his. To dismiss from office: New Zealand political: C. 20. B., 1941. Cf. run, get the. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1159</i>)

>  <b>PEARSON</b> <i>(1963,Coal Flat,111</i>)

>  running shoes, to give a person his (Phr.) to dismiss from political office. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),17</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> give somebody their running shoes dismiss from political office; what Governor General Kerr gave Prime Minister Gough Whitlam. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,50</i>)

### give something a run
 
 <i>n.</i> to give it a go

>  <b>I. DAVEY</b> RUN. Another colloquial meaning for `run' ... is to `give it a run and see how it goes' ... `it' being anything from giving a patient a new medicine to trying a shortcut in building, or an experiment in layout of a feature page in a paper. <i>(Jul 29, 1966,Letter</i>) [NOTES: (Whangarei)]

### give the chat/get the chat
 
 <i>phr.</i> to give/get firm advice or reproof (See [cf. chip v](chi-con.md#chip))

>  Skipper Roberts had better give the `chat' to play the game as it should be played. <i>(Apr 10, 1909,NZ Truth,199/3/1</i>)

>  The unspeakable dens kept by Marie Vernon and Cecilia Rea in College-street(?) naturally attracted the attention of the police. The Mt. Cook officers were always on the alert, and, though powerless of take any action, the women were given the `chat,' and their business was conducted as privately as possible. <i>(Sep 3, 1910,NZ Truth,271/3/2</i>)

>  <b>F.M. O'BRIEN:</b> Wellington Tramwaymen. The word, the news: e.g. Didn't you get the chat [in this case a special thumb-sign] that there was an inspector up the road. <i>(1949,Personal Communication</i>)

>  <b>R. MASON</b> (overheard in South Island) I gave her the chat about his latest doings. Spotted this car on the way back. ... One ten ack-emma.' <i>(1951,Personal Communication</i>)

### give the flick to
 
 <i>phr.</i> to give up, reject; abandon; sack

>  So knackered was I that the next day, I rarely made it to morning lectures, and I gave the flick to my Monday night waitressing job at the Chinese restaurant. <i>(Oct 7, 2002,Dominion Post,B11</i>)

>  [Hdg] Mayor gives big flick to smoking. Mayor Michael Bloomberg and the New York City Council reached agreement yesterday on a ban that would prohibit smoking in nearly every bar and restaurant and virtually all workplaces. <i>(Dec 13, 2002,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  <b>Ihimaera</b> Obviously, you've helped him to the point where he's been able to give me the flick. <i>(2000,Uncle's Story, The,277</i>)

>  Give him a flick and move on. <i>(Dec 10, 2002,TV2</i>) [NOTES: Shortland Street]

### give your left jandal for
 
 <i>phr.</i> desire greatly

>  Shorty Street also made its annual contribution to Kiwi colloquialisms: we learned how much value the average New Zealander places on simple rubber footwear with the phrase, "to give your left jandal for..." (something highly desirable, presumably). <i>(Jan 12, 2006,NZ Herald,E.G. p7</i>)

### giver
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [gyver](../G#gyver))

### gizzo
 
 <i>n.</i> A look, a squiz (See [Cf. geek](g-go.md#geek),[gink](g-go.md#gink),[gekko](g-go.md#gekko))

>  <b>VIRTUE</b> He'd go on up and have a good gizzo before Mum came inside. <i>(1987,Redemption of Elsdon Bird, The,6,1988 ed.</i>)

### Gizzy
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) Gisborne [ORIGIN: abbreviation]

>  .. we were glad to arrive at our backpackers in "Gizzy". <i>(May 1, 2005,Sunday Star Times,Escape p 11</i>)

>  <b>WINKS</b> New Zealanders show due respect for the names of their cities. Words like Paraparaumu ... do get horribly mangled, and dignified Christchurch sounds like a steam engine - CHCH - but one never hears of Auck, Gizzy, or Ham. <i>(1954,These New Zealanders,p52,1954 ed.</i>)

### GK
 
 <i>abbr.</i> goal keeper (from letters on player's back in netball?)

>  The imposing GK is certainly fast. <i>(Jul 11, 2004,Sunday Star Times,B10</i>)

### glad
 
 <i>n.</i> A `come-hither' look promising joy.

>  When I draw'd me wind, and wus walkin' aht/ In me slommicky 'orspital blue,/ I sights a tart, who `gets me cart'/ Wiv a `glad' wot bites me through./ ... We wus photograrfed, jest me an' 'er,/ 'Ere 'ave a cook at the proof.'/ Bill `cooked' and saw RED. <i>(Jul 10, 1919,Quick March,17</i>) [NOTES: by Harcus Plimmer]

### glad rags
 
 <i>n.</i> euphemism for sanitary pads [->ca1941 Havelock. -->ca1964 Auckland]

### glad-rag
 
 <i>v.</i> (informal) dress in glad rags

>  We have titified ourselves up - because this is the season to be glad-ragging - and set forth to a celebratory address. <i>(Dec 2005,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),42</i>)

### gladdie
 
 <i>n.</i> gladiolus

>  He'd ring me up to talk about glads, and we'd yak for a couple of hours... <i>(Feb 7, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  But over here it's all from the old place - kaka beak, gladdies, gerbera, hydrangea - it's my mother's flowers from the old place. <i>(1985,Landfall,156/454</i>) [NOTES: Grace (Waimarie)]

>  Oh, it would have been better in Kiwi suburbia, where Rach would have grown great gladdies and dug up new potatoes at Christmas. <i>(Mar 4, 2001,Sunday Star Times,A11</i>)

### gladdies
 
 <i>n.</i> A euphemistic familiar form of glad-rags, rather than (as Aust.) gladioli

### glads
 
 <i>n.</i> short for glad rags

>  A good night out would go something like this: spend hours putting on the glads, throw down a tab of acid or Ecstasy.. and hit the K Rd bars and clubs before winding up at Calibre around 3 am. <i>(Jun 1999,Metro,60</i>)

### Gladstone Scarecrow Festival
 
 <i>n.</i> an annual celebration of bird-scarers

>  Gladstone Scarecrow Festival .. Tiny Gladstone leaps to life for its annual celebration of the birdscarers that are so important to Wairarapa's farm crops. <i>(Nov 2005,North & South,32</i>)

### gladwrap
 
 <i>n.</i> (trademark) cling film

>  In his urban backpack, as well as items from the Super-Doop store, were the latest copy of <i>International Creative Scientist</i>, his plastic lunchbox with the gladwrap folded to re-use, and a piece of driftwood shaped rather less like a dolphin than he had first thought. <i>(Nov 6, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

>  <b>McGill</b> Bob tucked the note into the monogrammed pocket of a black silk shirt that clung to him like Gladwrap... <i>(1995,Gold in the Creek,161</i>)

### gladwrapped
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  I'm not only rapt but gladwrapped. <i>(Jan 3, 1998,Dominion,15</i>)

### Glam Slam
 
 <i>n.</i> a Wellington gay pride festival

>  It kicked off Wellington's week-long Glam Slam gay pride festival, which finishes on Saturday. <i>(Sep 12, 2005,Dominion Post,1</i>)

### Glammies
 
 <i>n.</i> gay equivalent of the Grammies awards

>  Camping it up at the Glammies award ceremony in Wellington are Tony al Dente, aka Cathie Sheat, left, and partner Pussy Grrl [sic], aka Andy Harness. <i>(Sep 12, 2005,Dominion Post,107</i>)

>  The Glammies ran for a number of years in the late 90's and always attracted large crowds from all walks of life. <i>(Jul 8, 2005,Scoop,-</i>) [NOTES: Scoop quoting Salient]

### glamming
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Downstairs, an entire courtyard of shops - one of which is a traditional barber - has similarly shunned the glamming of Ponsonby. <i>(Aug 11, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

### glamour boys
 
 <i>n.</i> [Orig. applied to RAF personnel: cf OED2 sb. 3.] A term applied to other units or arms of the Armed Services other than the speaker's, whose situation was supposedly less dangerous or more pleasant. Cf coconut bombers. Second Echelon NZEF members [WWII]

>  (Glossary) Glamour boys: Fair Causus Belli in 2NZEF. Title bestowed by the First and Third Contingents upon the Second Contingent after it got a blighty. <i>(1946,Johnny Enzed in the Middle East</i>)

>  <b>ELLIOTT & ADSHEAD</b> `Were you in the first Libyan campaign in 1940?' `No. I was one of the `G-lamour Boys'. We didn't come over until well after that. <i>(1967,Cowshed to Dog Collar,81</i>)

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> While the Fifth Brigade - the Glamour Boys - were still showing the flag in England ... <i>(1946,We Walked Alone,89</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> There can be no doubt that the Second Echelon - 'the Glamour Boys' - were infinitely more fortunate than any of their peers in this unexpected diversion [to England]. <i>(1999,There Was a Time,164</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Members of the Third New Zealand Division who were transferred to the Middle East after serving in the Islands.

>  Tales that would make the tales of the `glamour boys' or `coconut bombers' pale into comparative insignificance. <i>(Dec 1941,2NZEF Times,26/23</i>)

### Glasgow cocktail
 
 <i>n.</i> See cit. [nf dicts] [? prisoners']

>  <b>BAXTER</b> The girls in Arohata jail/ Are very rarely dressed in silk -/ Let us take a Glasgow cocktail/ Bubbling coal gas into milk,/ Drink up to Mary, Kate and Lou,/ <i>(1976,Collected Poems,266,1963 ed.</i>)

### Glasgow lease
 
 <i>n.</i> [1964 J. Winchester Check Paterson lands and deeds, Muir NZ Encyclopedia ofForms and Precedents vol. 7]

>  [Hdg] "Glasgow"leases ... The question at issue was whether the renewal of a "Glasgow lease" which provides terms for renewal, is in respect of that renewal exempt from the provisions of the Land Sales Act in that it is a renewal of an existing lease and not a fresh lease under the Act. <i>(May 26, 1944,Dominion,4</i>)

>  The board of the trust acknowledges Glasgow leases may no longer be a particularly satisfactory form of land tenure. <i>(Jun 1992,Metro,14</i>)

>  Perpetually renewable, 21-year ground leases are sometimes known as "Glasgow" leases. <i>(1978,Leases & Lands,6</i>)

### Glasgow leasing system
 
 <i>n.</i> perpetual leases with a 21-year renewable rent right

>  But since 1916, the land has been governed by rules known as the Glasgow Leasing System. This means they are leased in perpetuity with rents renewable every 21 years. <i>(Aug 26, 2004,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

### glass alley
 
 <i>n.</i> = bottlie

>  <b>HOWE</b> `Go on, Snowy! ...Two glass alleys from lemonade bottles if you do it.' [c1880s] <i>(1964,Stamper Battery,67</i>)

### glass ceiling
 
 <i>n.</i> the number of drinks before intoxication sets in

>  Redfrock and her friends are beginning to talk about the Glass Ceiling. This has nothing to do with work. This is about the number of drinks they can have. <i>(Nov 23, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

### glass walking
 
 <i>n.</i> an illegal activity, = ? (Orsman) [1984 16 F E26 Pakuranga Coll. 33 GLASS WALKING]

### glass-cutter
 
 <i>n.</i> A diamond.

>  Gifts had also been received in South Africa [by the rebel rugby players]. `But we didn't get the glass cutters (diamonds) like some [rugby union] councillors received a few years ago,' he said. <i>(Oct 19, 1986,Sunday News,5</i>)

### glassie
 
 <i>n.</i> bar glass-washer

>  During 1999, he was a barman and glassie at the Fat Lady's Arms. <i>(May 1, 2002,Capital Times,5</i>)

>  "It's all for the unsung heroes, the dish hands, glassies and rubbish removers and the customers who allow us to make it all possible." <i>(Aug 26, 2006,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  <b>Barback/Glassies</b> 1-2 nights per week. easy $$ for the most important job in the bar. <i>(Jun 8, 2005,Dominion Post,E16</i>)

>  GLASSIE - P NRTH - ONGOING 1-2 people are required to work in bar/nightclub on a weekly ongoing basis. Work involves collecting empty glasses and beer bottles. Could be very crowded, you will need to be hardworking and able to work late nites. Job could be up to at least 16-20 per week. You need black pants & shoes A shirt will be supplied. BRIGHT,BUBBLY PEOPLE PERSON:) <i>(2006,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.sjs.co.nz/JobProfile.aspx?JobID=31224 Accessed 10/04/06]



 
 <i>n.</i> A glass marble, usu. transparent; occas. a clear glass marble-stopper from early aerated-water bottles. Compare bottlie, glass alley. [ca1918-20 Auckland man 66 years old (p.c. Prof. Graeme Kennedy). Common in Auckland when he was a boy. -->1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 21 GLASSIES [games]]

>  The bigger or more skilful boys won most of my marbles off me, their glassies or steelies (ball-bearings) blasting my clay dakers ruthlessly out of the ring. <i>(Aug 8, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),64</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER in GIFKIN</b> I could.. shoot alleys, glassies and bottlies with the best of the boys. <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,7</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> `Reverend Mother talks of when she used to give you marbles for being a good little boy - pee-wees and agates, and stonies and glassies and a big tor. And once she tried to play with you, and you said she funked because her knuckle was inside the ring.' <i>(1933,Tides of Youth,153</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> He took a green bottle-o from the unresisting hand of an open-mouthed, dismayed child ... [177] whatcher let him take the glassy for? ... <i>(1951,Witch's Thorn, The,176</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Then there were the terms referring to particular kinds of marbles: for example, agates, aggies, aggotties, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cat's eyes, chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, molly-bars, mulley-bars [mullibars: large marbles], peelies, peewees, piss-wees, pretties, Scotties, Scottish alleys, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers, white alleys, and in more recent years ball-bearings, chippies, plastics, and woodies. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,174</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> There was no football [at Grovetown school c1870-1885]. ... We played marbles, Ring, and Eye-drop with taws, glassies, stonies, and agots. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,139</i>)

>  The bigger and more skilful boys won most of my marbles off me [at Auckland c1931], their glassies or steelies (ball-bearings) blasting my clay dakers ruthlessly out of the ring. <i>(Aug 8, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),64</i>) [NOTES: Sinclair `At the Point']

>  I could spin a top `overarm' and shoot alleys, glassies and bottlies with the best of the boys. <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,7</i>) [NOTES: (Dorothy Butler)]

>  <b>McGILL</b> glassy a glass marble. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,50</i>)

### glasswort
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Salicornia australis</i> [See Oxford Book of NZ Plants.]

>  Glasswort. Salicornia indica, Willd. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/47</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <i>glasswort [s] Salicornia australis</i> Sol. ex Benth. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,29</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>SALICORNIA AUSTRALIS, glasswort</i>. Also a maritime plant, growing in rock crevices <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,52</i>)

>  <b>LOCKLEY</b> Typically this salted margin is dominated along its landward edge by thick clumps of sea rush ... with broad patches of the low-growing succulent glasswort <i>Salicornia australis</i> <i>(1980,House Above the Sea,201</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Salicornia australis</i> GLASSWORT ... Succulent plant with tough prostrate stems ... <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,146</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> In the most exposed situations low growing succulent plants predominate, including the native ice plant <i>Disphyma australe</i>, the glasswort <i>Sarcocornia quinqueflora, Apium prostratum</i> and <i>Senecio lautus. </i> <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,155</i>)

>  A marker duly shows you the way down to a boardwalk on your right, which leads off to flats of sedges, glasswort and yellow button. <i>(Jan 1, 2004,Dominion Post,C2</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Salicornia australis</i>

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Salicornia australis</i> <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Salicornia australis</i>

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i>Salicornia australis</i> <i>(1907,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Report on a Botanical Survey of Kapiti Island]

### glassy-winged sharpshooter
 
 <i>n.</i> an insect

>  The recent scourge of introduced creatures that bite - such as black widow spiders, fire ants and dengue-carrying mosquitoes - suggests that it is just a matter of time before the arrival here of the principal cause of California's problems, the insect <i>homalodisca coagulata</i>, known as the glassy winged sharrpshooter. Pierce's Disease is caused by <i>Xylella fastidiosa</i>, a bacteria that lives in the xylem of plants, the thin layer beneath the surface that transports water and nutrients from the roots. <i>(Mar 31, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  Winston Peters.. is always followed by the Green's Ian Ewan-Street, insisting that all grape imports be inspected for an insect called the glassy-winged sharpshooter. <i>(Apr 7, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  MAF has established an analysis of the risks posed by Pierce's Disease and glassy-winged sharpshooters, and has consulted with Winegrowers New Zealand, table grape importers and government biosecurity agencies. <i>(Apr 28, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

>  Mr Ewen-Street has discipline himself to the one hobby-insect: the grassy-winged sharp-shooter which he says threatens our grape crop... Adopting a professorial tone, Mr Sutton said that, to the best of his knowledge, the grassy-winged sharp-shooter was not in the habit of laying eggs in bunches of Californian grapes in the way a black widow spider might. <i>(Sep 20, 2001,Dominion,2</i>)

### glax
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GRADY</b> The second digester has observation glasses which allow the operator to see when it is full of whale oil and glax. (Glax is the cooked meat and bones and the water.) When it gets to a certain level, the whale oil is blown into a pre-heat tank and the gluewater at the bottom of the digester is blown away. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,76</i>)

### Glaxo
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BRIDGES & DOWNS</b> Glaxo didn't take off straight away. <i>(2000,No 8 Wire: The Best of Kiwi Ingenuity,152</i>)

>  A brand new English Field Ambulance.. arrived at a N.Z. Rest Camp in France, to take over from the En Zedders, and.. were instructed by our boys in the use of "Glaxo", and told that a pound tin made three quarts of rich milk. <i>(Mar 28, 1917,Chronicles NZEF,II/15 : 55</i>)

### Glaxo baby
 
 <i>n.</i> Usu. pl., a nickname for young (? territorial) recruits at Tauherenikau (Wairarapa) Camp.

>  ... Glaxo babies ... <i>(1918,Book of the C1 Camp, Tauherenikau,7</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... some very big schoolboys ... I suppose we used to call them glaxo babies. <i>(Sep 25, 1882,TV1</i>) [NOTES: Commentator [on Schoolboy Rugby: Aust v NZ]]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HYDE</b> The woman turned to smile at her. `Sorry,' she said. `Did you see the Glaxo Babies? But the sailors were all right, weren't they? They'd have a job getting the sailors to fire on a crowd with women and children in it.' <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,303</i>)

### Glaxo camp
 
 <i>n.</i> nickname for the Tauherenikau Training Camp, situated near the Glaxo factory.

>  `What he saw in C1 Camp'/ Before I joined the `Glaxo' Camp/ My spirits soared .../ I felt my age - just twenty!/ <i>(Sep 2, 1918,Quick March,5</i>)

>  He was a `Glaxo' camp man who could not qualify in time to fight for his country. <i>(1919,Triad, The,27/3/41</i>)

>  The other [candidate] was a young graduate of Wellington who is just commencing practice here, but who was a `Glaxo' camp man who could not qualify in time to fight for his counntry. <i>(Jun 10, 1919,Triad, The,41</i>)

### Glaxo men
 
 <i>n.</i> the military trainees at Tauherenikau Camp.

>  To the Glaxo men of Tauherenikau [Military Camp] (if they remember the events of their camp at all) this wee verse will be a precis. <i>(Dec 10, 1921,Quick March,43</i>)

### gleichenia
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WHITE</b> The Gleichenias [51] are a very distinct kind, with their ladder-like fronds set in umbrella shape. Hymenophyllums, or filmy Ferns, are very curious. Adiantums, or Maiden-hairs, are in infinite variety. The Todeas are very beautiful, especially <i> Todea superba</i>, which is commonly called Prince of Wales' Feather. <i>(1905,My NZ Garden,50</i>)

### Glenavy bag
 
 <i>n.</i> [toponym. A rectangular leather bag (gladstone?),used prewar, by citizens of Oamaru (then dry) for their visits to the settlement of Glenavy. The bag was a convenient bottle size length.]

### Glennie kid
 
 <i>n.</i> a child at the Glendining complex 1954-62

>  During the 1954-1962 period, 50,609 children were in the Glendining complex. The 'Glennie Kids' were an identifiable group in the community and were vulnerable to comment and prejudice from their peers and the community. <i>(Jun 2005,NZ Memories,No 54, p. 53</i>)

### glide time
 
 <i>n.</i> originally, the ability with permission of one'ssuperiors to work one's own chosen hours within certain limits; later became synonym for taking life easy in the Public Service. Flexible working hours allowing for varied starting and finishing times introduced to the State Services [check Brit., Aust flexitime] (See [also gliding on](g-go.md#gliding-on))

>  <b>HALL</b> ... you look as if you spent the night there. ... Isn't that carrying glide-time to excess? <i>(1977,Glide Time,14</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> glide time an attempt by the Public Service to let staff work the 40-hour week that suited them became the title of Roger Hall's blockbuster play and ever since the concept has been informally viewed as an excuse for skiving or working for the government at a pace that would suit the mos t laid-back snail. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,50</i>)

>  Raine calls himself a flexitime editor. He means glidetime. <i>(May 31, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),37</i>)

>  In those days, she says, it was glide time in the public service. "You had permanent employment in the public service and everyone was just there and so if people didn't perform you just had to live with it." <i>(Nov 19, 2000,Sunday Star Times,A8</i>)

>  <b>HASMAN-JUDD</b> Monday mornings are always the worst, not because she minds having to go to work, but because of the inevitable dissection of the weekend. It seems to be a Public Service ritual, like the round of greetings as each person arrives in the office during the first half hour of glide time. <i>(2000,Woman Who Loved Plants & Other Stories, The,87</i>)

### Glide Time
 
 <i>n.</i> the Roger Hall play which gave rise to the TV series <i>Gliding On</i>. (See [gliding on](g-go.md#gliding-on))

>  It was a shock. It was so slack. Actually, it was like <i>Glide Time</i>. So I didn't stay very long. <i>(Aug 29, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)

>  "I've always looked on <i>Glide Time</i> as a watershed of New Zealand writing. It was the first time people all round the country queued to see a New Zealand play." <i>(Jun 28, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35</i>)

>  From the saggy cardie days of <i>Glide Time</i>, this species has evolved into a fashionable, affluent and educated ruling class. <i>(Aug 14, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

### gliding on
 
 <i>n.</i> coasting, taking things easy (from a television comedy series Gliding On)

>  Police were the last public servants still gliding on and they needed to be brought into the 21st century, Police Minister George Hawkins said yesterday. <i>(Mar 7, 2000,Dominion,2</i>)

>  Based on responses from more than 6500 public servants throughout the country, the survey's findings "explode the Gliding On myth". <i>(Apr 29, 2002,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  The old gliding on thing was obviously a caricature, but there's no doubt the 1990's cut that out. <i>(Sep 17, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

>  Survivors of the Gliding On era of State work institutions fondly remember the days of tea breaks. <i>(Jan 31, 2001,Evening Post,19</i>)

>  The SIS, being public servants themselves, were concerned about Hall's attitudes to the Public Service as displayed in Gliding On. <i>(Nov 29, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

>  The Public Service, however, was still in walk-shorts and Gliding-On mode. <i>(Jun 11, 1998,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  But with the <i>Gliding On</i>-type government jobs now gone for good, and a much leaner bureaucracy based in the Beehive, Wellington has been searching for new industries to feed its economy. <i>(Sep 26, 2004,Sunday Magazine (Auckland),8</i>)

>  <b>HUGHES</b> It was <i>Gliding On</i> time again but now I was actually in the building. <i>(1999,Shooting from the Lip,206</i>)

>  Can't be wowserism; no race tries harder to ape Auckland's chardonnay set than the giddy Gliding On rubberstamp-pushers who jostle into Wellington's bars each afternoon. <i>(Oct 21, 2000,Evening Post,26</i>)

>  The only series that ever came close to becoming a fond memory was <i>Gliding On</i>. <i>(Jun 25, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),63</i>)

### glimmer
 
 <i>n.</i> A marble name. Poss. a marble with glimmering specks in it when held to the light.

>  <b>HYDE</b> George Bennett's marbles were worth talking about, being composed in equal parts of alley tors and glimmers. [26] ... the beautiful little sparkly lights in the hearts of the glimmers when he held them up one by one to the gas-jet atoned for all else. <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,25</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Once Curly Adams' bag of marbles, glimmers and chalkies, came up [from the street-sewer, c1912] on the shovel, and the drain-men gave them to Eliza. <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,26</i>)

### glip
 
 <i>n.</i> [(whalers')]

>  <b>GRADY</b> Every time a whale sounds there is a slick, known to the old-time whalers as glip, left on the sea's surface. It is a smooth patch, left by a fast swimming whale, that alerts any gunner of [sic] any changes of course. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,14</i>)

### globefish
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 116. TETRODON RICHEI. Freminv. Globe Fish. <i>(1872,Fishes,72</i>)

>  Before proceeding with the descriptions of the two globe-fish, it may not be out of place to give a short epitome of the present varieties of the group Tetrodontina so far proved to inhabit the waters surrounding New Zealand ... <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/2443</i>)

>  <i>Spheroides richei</i> Fre[acute]minville. (<i>Amblyrhynchotus richei</i> re[acute]minville ...) The little globe-fish is occasionally met with in Otago Harbour. <i>(1913,TrNZI,45/234</i>) [NOTES: Thomson, Otago]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Sphoeroides richei </i>Fremville. GLOBE-FISH. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,56,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> The Globe fishes (<i>Spheroides</i>) are small with large heads and the body studded with tubercles,... <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,202</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 202]

>  <i>Sphaerodes richei</i> Freminville. Globe-fish (stink-fish). <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/418</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> 413 Globe Fish <i>Sphaeroides hamiltoni </i>(Gray and Richardson)... Globe Fish <i>Sphaeroides nitidus</i> Griffin... Globe Fish <i>Sphaeroides cheesemanii</i> (Clarke) ... Globe Fish <i>Contusus richei</i> (Freminville) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> GLOBE-FISH <i>Contusus richei </i>(Freminville) ... It has several other popular names such as Balloon-fish and Mouse-fish. In America it is known as Rabbit-fish and Stink-fish. The most humorous name is that of Publican-fish. The names Globe-fish and Balloon-fish were given because of the animal's habit of inflating itself with air to such an extent that the body was like a lamp globe or an inflated balloon. The Mouse-fish is the most popular among some fishermen and when the fish is distended it does present a striking resemblance to a mouse, with its cunning looking face. The name Rabbit-fish in America is so given because its teeth are like those of a rabbit. The most witty name, Publican-fish given to it by Australian fishermen, is said to have originated from the resemblance of an inflated Globe-fish to the rotundity of many successful publicans. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,378,2nd ed. ed.</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family TETRAODONTIDAE. Globe fishes (<i>Sphoeroides</i> spp, ...) can inflate themselves like blown-up balloons ... <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,233</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> GLOBEFISH [Spheroides richei] ... Puffer, toado, common toado, are alternative names sometimes heard here. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,24</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> GLOBEFISH ... Other names: Spheroides richei; puffer, common toado, sand toado. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,296</i>)

>  globe fish n. small fish called also toad-fish and porcupine-fish. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),9</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> GLOBE-FISH Small, up to 6 inches (15.24 cm) widespread inhabitant of near coastal waters the little Globefish is notable for his habit of puffing himself up into a balloon when disturbed. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,114</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>GLOBEFISH</i> (Pufferfish, prickly toadfish) <i>Contusus richei (Sphoeroides richei)</i> ... The globefish is a round bodied puffer ... [317] They prefer shallow coastal waters and are found in large numbers just off sandy beaches. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,316,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Pufferfish Contusus richei</i> Alternatively globefish, or toadfish. Also present in southern Australia. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,147</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] globefish 2355-83 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [?Source. <i>Tetradon Richei</i> Fremino Globe-fish.]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Sphoeroides gilbanksii</i> GILLBANK'S GLOBE-FISH. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,56,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Globe Fish <i>Boesemanichthys gillbanksii</i> (Clarke) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,413</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 413]

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] globefish, Gillbank's 2367 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Globe Fish <i>Takifugu oblongus</i> (Bloch) <i>(1955,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,413</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 413 This seems to belong under 1. simplex]

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] globefish, little 2373 3. <i>Takifugu oblongus. </i> <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### Globo
 
 <i>n.</i> Bank of New Zealand Estates Co. Ltd [also attrib. and l.c.]

>  Before rejoicing over the gobbling up of globo, I should like to know who constitute the Assets Company... The news that the globo assets are to be taken over by a syndicate of London capitalists.. has sent the colonial press into ecstasies of delight. <i>Ibid.</i> 7 The Globo Assets Account should be treated separately. <i>(Jul 12, 1890,NZ Observer,1</i>)

>  [Caption] Young N.Z. (blubberingly) - 'Boo-hoo! I've lost my beautiful globo balloon; it's gone sailing off to London. <i>(Jul 19, 1890,NZ Observer,13</i>)

>  Globo has been floated, and the Bank of New Zealand has received GBP1,125,000 for its mass of landed securities. <i>(Aug 2, 1890,NZ Observer,1</i>)

>  There is no doubt that the whole 'Globo' interest will be exerted to the utmost to prevent the return of land-taxers to our next Parliament. <i>Ibid.</i>. 9 'Globo assets' - 4,000,000 acres of New Zealand sold or pawned to London brokers who have been so very kind as to advance the bank enough money to keep the tottering concern afloat a little longer... Two of New Zealand's 'rulers and statesmen' are included in the 'globo'. <i>(Sep 20, 1890,North & South,6</i>)

>  [Hdg] The Globo Company. What English Financiers think of it. <i>(Sep 27, 1890,NZ Observer,9</i>)

>  It is no reply to say that the Auckland Tramways Company has ceased to exist and that its assets are merged in that all-gobbling Globo which has swallowed (but has not yet digested) all the B.N.Z. white elephants. <i>(Nov 1, 1890,NZ Observer,1</i>)

>  What price globo white elephant shares? <i>(Nov 22, 1890,NZ Observer,4</i>)

>  If all the New Zealand papers that are owned by a bank were to be sold up, in globo, it would be a good job for the colony. <i>(Dec 20, 1890,NZ Observer,1</i>)

>  To talk of the globo Press of New Zealand as being unmuzzled is rank humbug. <i>(Sep 13, 1890,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  The first symptom that the [Sailors'] Home was being worked on globo orders was seen in a notice that the charge for board was raised from 12s 6d to 15s per week. <i>(Sep 20, 1890,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  The B.N.Z. never tried to influence him.. or any other member of the Globo Ministry. <i>(Nov 29, 1890,NZ Observer,1</i>)

>  His presence there would be fatal to the chances of the Globo Ministry returning to power. <i>(Dec 20, 1890,NZ Observer,1</i>)

>  A company is being formed to take over the <i>globo</i> assets from the bank [of New Zealand]. <i>(Jul 10, 1890,Otago Witness,23</i>)

>  <b>DRUMMOND</b> The Estates Company had been formed five years previously to take over the bank's "globo assets," which consisted of real estate, trading concerns, and other properties that had fallen into the bank's hands <i>(1907,Life & Work of Seddon,216</i>)

>  This is a fine station and is, I believe, one of the Globo Estate's assets. <i>(Oct 28, 1899,NZ Times,3</i>)

### Globos
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Globos. Debenture shares in Bank of New Zealand Estates: Stock Exchange (-1895). A. J. Wilson Stock Exchange Glossary. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,333</i>)

### glory box
 
 <i>n.</i> A chest in which a woman stores linen, clothes, etc. against the time when she will be setting up her own household, or various treasured things. The contents of such a chest. Cf US hope chest. [ca1900 at least: common among women, and such chests advertised in furniture shops under the name glory boxes: (WHB Orsman - before WW1 at least check newspaper advts]]

>  A green wheelie bin with the number 8/39 on it was missing from the family home and .. a glory box about 1.5 m long was also missing. <i>(Sep 21, 1998,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> At home in the evenings, Carly sews for her glory box, ... Doilies, table-centres, nightgown-tops <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,114,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> Seeing the neatly folded linen there, the carefully worked doilies and table spreads, the unworn silk nightdress, she knew she had blundered on Moira's hoard, her glory-box. <i>(1949,Roads from Home,129</i>)

>  [A buyer in the Wanted to Buy column advertised for a glory box] <i>(Feb 7, 1951,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  <b>DARE</b> ... I was initiated into the wonders of her `glory box'. This box contained all her greatest treasures, mostly in the linen line. <i>(1956,Rouseabout Jane,125</i>)

>  <b>PEARSON</b> Myra only had her glory-box and thirty pounds ...; her parents said it was all they could do to pay for the wedding-breakfast. <i>(1963,Coal Flat,92</i>)

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> Rachel's wedding presents and the contents of her glory box were being sent ... from Invercargill. <i>(1969,Molesworth,143</i>)

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> glory box: Bottom drawer with lid on. View with alarm any girl who talks about hers. Some girls have glory boxes so big they live in them. <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,182</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> glory Box: Did you ladies have a HOPE CHEST as a girl? <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,46</i>)

>  glory box n. Austral. & N.Z. (esp. formerly) a box in which a young woman stores clothes, etc., in preparation for marriage. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,474</i>) [NOTES: 1986 NZPOD 320; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 211]]

>  <b>McCAULEY</b> When Alice was a girl she had a glory box. She started embroidering pillow cases to put away when she was in standard six. <i>(1986,Then Again,167</i>)

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> When I turned sixteen I bought a glory box. <i>(1987,Oracles and Miracles,167</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> glory box chest containing a young lady's manchester collection in anticipation of marriage; aka `bottom drawer' and `hope chest', latter still used in US. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,50</i>)

>  ... I gave that to my eldest daughters many months later for their glory boxes. <i>(Jan 13, 1989,Dominion,8</i>)

>  [Advt] Dorothy had seized upon 2 beautiful boudoir caps, declaring that they should never again find their way into Mollie's "Glory box". <i>(Sep 30, 1926,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,34</i>)

>  <b>Faith Oxenbridge</b> Shane is coming back to Christchurch in a month and Wendy and her mother have started shopping for her glory box. <i>(2007,Six Pack 2, The,34</i>)

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> She had quite a lot in her glory box, but they'd need some furniture. <i>(1984,Blackball 08,156</i>)

>  Most girls had a "glory box" - a large chest that you gradually filled with items for the linen cupboard when you got married. <i>(Feb 12, 2005,Dominion Post,E3</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> Raelene had this Karitane who went straight to town, bought a few knick knacks for her glory box. <i>(1980,Prisoner of Mother England,21</i>)

>  [Advt] Wallnut [sic] Glory Boxes. Sturdily built and available in several designs. <i>(Dec 7, 1939,Dominion,6</i>)

### glory flower
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>glory flower, glory pea n</i> see <i>Sturt's desert pea and kaka-bill</i>, N.Z. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),9</i>)

### glory hole
 
 <i>n.</i> the entrance to the disposal chute where oddments of meat, etc. are conveyed to a processor on a lower level. [freezing works]

>  <b>MORRIESON</b> ... he strode briskly, a quarter-filled sack of skids and gambols [94] over his shoulder, along the siding, stepping over the bright blotches of blood with the hovering blow-flies outside the `glory hole'....Chunks of wool-denuded meat [from the slipe master] were tossed into a barrow and, at intervals, wheeled to the `glory-hole'. <i>(1976,Pallet on the Floor,93</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HUNT</b> A Maori showed us where [the old flaxmill] was, and when we asked him if there were any signs he replied: `Te glory hole and te garden'. We found the old `glory hole' of the mill where once the catcher boy sat, covered with green pulp and flax gum as it dropped from the stripper rollers overhead. <i>(1951,Confessions,42</i>)

### glory of the west
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Leptopteris superba. </i>

>  <b>POTTS</b> (And) <i>Leptopteris superba. </i> <i>(1882,Out in the Open</i>)

### glory pea
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [glory flower](g-go.md#glory-flower))

>  (And) <i>Clianthus puniceus </i> <i>(1837,Botanist, The</i>) [NOTES: 1837 The Botanist (1837-1846) ed. B. Maund]

>  <b>FEATON</b> In the immediate centre the <i>Clianthus puniceus</i> (Kowhai- ngutu-kaka) stands out in all the glory of its crimson blossoms ... The name of `Glory Pea' is well fitted for so gorgeous a plant. <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,xi</i>)

>  <i>glory flower, glory pea n. </i>see<i> Sturt's desert pea and kaka-bill, N.Z.</i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),9</i>)

### glossy
 
 <i>adj.</i> As a distinguishing epithet in names of plants see ascarine, coprosma, karamu, palntain, daisy-tree

### glove soup
 
 <i>n.</i> Liquid generated inside surgical gloves. [(surgeons')]

>  <b>SMITH</b> We use ethereal soap for this [slipping on surgical gloves] - the gloves slide on much easier - but it leaves too much glove soup. <i>(1941,More Notes,19</i>)

### glow-worm
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Rongomaha may look like the epitome of English society, with its gardens, billiard room and bachelors' balcony, but the bush and the glow-worm caves are adjacent. <i>(Oct 16, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

>  As well as its campervan and bus businesses, Tourism Holdings has operated guided tours in the Waitomo "glowworm" cave and the adjacent Aranui cave since 1995, attracting about 400,000 visitors a year. <i>(Nov 2, 2003,Sunday Star Times,D1</i>)

>  The department acquired the glow worm caves from their Maori owners under the Scenery Preservation Act in 1908. <i>(Nov 19, 2001,Evening Post,19</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>The Habits and Life-history of the New Zealand Glowworm</i>. By G.V. Hudson <i>(1891,TrNZI,23/43</i>)

>  ... a young friend of mine ... informed me that he had found some pupae of the New Zealand glowworm (<i>Bolitophila luminosa</i>) ... <i>(1893,TrNZI,25/164</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> NEW ZEALAND GLOW WORM (<i>Arachnocampa luminosa</i>). <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,51</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> in NZ the insect <i>Bolitophila luminosa</i>

>  <b>GEE</b> 'There might be glow-worms.' The little colony shone privately. 'Quiet,' I whispered, 'or they'll go out.' <i>(1992,Going West,154</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> We noticed many glow-worms and from the entrance took several specimens of stalactites. <i>(Feb 28, 1848,Journal,V/187</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> The feeding of this bird [sc. Kiwi] at night with the large glow-worm (`toke-tipa' of the natives) is a very interesting sight. <i>(Oct 1866,Birds of New Zealand,360</i>)

>  <b>HUDSON</b> Family TIPULIDAE. The Glow-worm <i>Bolitophilia luminosa</i>, Skuse. <i>(1892,Elementary Manual of NZ Entomology,49</i>)

>  This insect (<i>B. luminosa</i>), found only in New Zealand, is much shorter than its name and not so long as a pin. <i>(1952,National Geographic Magazine,419</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Glow Worm - piritana, puratoke, <i>Arachnocampa luminosa </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-12</i>)

>  <b>DENNIS</b> ... the larvae of the fungus gnat (<i>Arachnocampa luminosa</i>) better known as the glow worm. <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,193</i>)

>  The light also caught a glow-worm slithering across its silky hammock to deal with its next meal. <i>(Feb 28, 2004,Dominion Post,E14</i>)

>  The common New Zealand glow-worm.. is a fly - a member of the Diptera Bolitophila luminosa. <i>(Mar 16, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  A second glow-worm walk will take place at 6.30pm on Saturday, May 18. <i>(Apr 10, 2002,Norwester,3</i>)

>  The little blue light of a single glow-worm burned in the darkened recess of the bank above me. <i>(Nov 17, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C9</i>)

>  A radiance became manifest that absorbed the whole faculty of observation - the radiance of such a massed body of glow-worms as cannot be found anywhere else in the world. <i>(Sep 30, 1924,Otago Witness,16</i>)

>  The glow-worm grotto in the Waitomo caves is more than worth any sacrifice of comfort or inconvenience made to see it. <i>(Feb 6, 1937,King Country Chronicle,4</i>)

### glue factory
 
 <i>n.</i> a figurative place of disposal

>  Although they [sc. unsuccessful Olympic athletes] have trained with extraordinary dedication they go straight to the glue factory. <i>(Sep 27, 2000,Evening Post,4</i>)

### glue pot
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep with sticky wool that is difficult to shear.

>  <b>SCURR</b> Glue Pot:- a sticky sheep; hard to shear. <i>(2000,Rabbiters' Glossary,2</i>)

>  sticky sheep: a glue pot. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

>  .. Canterbury duster, Waiouru blonde, cotty, decoy, glue pot and scrubdasher. <i>(Nov 22, 2002,Dominion,C6</i>)

### gluepot
 
 <i>n.</i> A mud hole or a bad place on a road or track where vehicles likely to get stuck. Also as a local name [?mainly coaching]

>  Poor Walker has lost five horses, one died ... one sunk in the Glue Pot and though there were seven men with ropes they could not move it, ... <i>(May 15, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,184,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Carefully straddling high ruts [in the badly constructed road] here and there, and crossing gently over soft and swampy places, he would dodge all the `crab-holes and glue-pots' with a dexterity born of long experience. <i>(1912,Reminiscences of Early Settlement Dunedin,240</i>)

>  gluepot n. a bad place in a road where vehicles are liable to get stuck; a cricket pitch after heavy rain. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,4</i>)

>  glue-pot n. stretch of road in which vehicles are apt to get bogged down. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),9</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Euphemism for pisspot. [nf dicts.]

>  A 1920s version of `Taffy was a Welshman' ended: `I went to Taffy's house/ Taffy was in bed;/ `Then I took the gluepot/ And stuck it on his head.' <i>(1920,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: ca1920s p.c. Dr C. Beeby]

### gluewater
 
 <i>n.</i> [(whalers')]

>  <b>GRADY</b> The second digester has observation glasses which allow the operator to see when it is full of whale oil and glax. (Glax is the cooked meat and bones and the water.) When it gets to a certain level, the whale oil is blown into a pre-heat tank and the gluewater at the bottom of the digester is blown away. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,76</i>)

### gluey
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep with sticky wool that is difficult to shear.

>  a sticky sheep - a gluey. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### glut
 
 <i>n.</i> A wooded wedge used to keep saw cuts open [kauri forestry]

>  Glut: Large wooden wedge (usually rata or miro), used to keep saw cuts open and to help topple trees from their stumps when cut through... <i>(1986,World of the Kauri, The,222</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> Huge "gluts" or wooden wedges, were at length used to raise the tree sufficiently to permit the insetion of a jack, against which the tree could no longer stand. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,157</i>)

>  Then they had to make large wooden wedges, "gluts", out of rata; and, only striking when the tree swayed and the cut widened, they succeeded in lifting eight or ten inches, when it crashed. <i>(1964,New Story of the Kauri, The,281</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> A lathe inserted between layers in stacks of sawn timber (to allow air to circulate or to make the stack more rigid); fillet, batten. [Southland sawmilling]

>  <b>NZ Forest Service</b> The rate of loading loose timber might be increased if greater use were made of cross and upright battens (referred to as gluts in Southland). <i>(Aug 9, 1948,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: TS memo from Conservator of Forests, Auckland]

### gnarly
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) unsettled.

>  The cows milk you wouldn't believe. We had a gnarly first spring and the herd still peaked at two kilograms of milk solids per cow. <i>(Mar 15, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

### gnawing
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) kissing

>  <b>McGILL</b> gnawing: kissing, street-kid style; e.g. `We did some gnawing, that was it. She didn't want to go any further.' [Alison Gray] <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,50</i>)

### GNS
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Institute of Geological and Nuclear Sciences, a Crown Research Institute formed from the DSIR's Geological Survey and Institute of Nuclear Sciences

>  The Institute of Geological and Nuclear Sciences (GNS) will receive $5 million per year over the next decade for its "Geonet" project, to be spend on modernising and expanding the array of instruments that, since the 70s, has monitored disturbances below the Taupo volcanic region and off the coast of the lower North Island. <i>(Mar 17, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

### go
 
 <i>v.</i> go for, attack

>  Grandma's rooster goes you. You'll have to be careful if you go outside. <i>(Nov 1, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,83</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> Of a route, to work, be accomplishable.

>  <b>GREEN</b> The route to the northern ridges of the peak ... would not `go'. <i>(1883,High Alps of NZ,232 [?222]</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> in phr. give (something) the go: give it a miss

>  <b>McGILL</b> give the go to reject a suitor or abandon a place or job; short for `give the go-by'; eg `Why dont' we give this game the go, eh?' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,50</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> GO (n) 1. Good idea. USA, mid 19th century. 2. Way of doing things, style. See also FORM (2). <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,248</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Something certain to succeed.

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Go. 11. An agreement, a settled thing; a certainty. Esp. in it's a go, occ. is it a go?: mostly Australia and New Zealand (- 1914). <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,334</i>)

>  go,in phrase it's a go,a fair change or deal; and it's no go, the deal is off, matter is ended. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),9</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> go, phr. it's a go the deal is on; eg G'morning, Cabinet. First, the bad news. Political union with the Aussies is on. It's a go. Now, the good news. It won't happen for years.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,50</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a contest; race; fight. [->1986 NZPOD 321 [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 176 under FAIR] go ... NZ & Austral. chance, deal (a fair go). -->1951 16+ M 32 St Mary's, Wgton 30B GO, (HAVE) A GO [a fight] -->1951 15 M 3 Marlborough C. 30B GO, HAVE A GO ? -->1951 14-15 M 33 Wellington H.S. 30B GO, HERE'S A GO]

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> There was that little go we had on the gumfield. <i>(1902,Land of the Lost,243</i>)

>  Why, our Sergeant said he would walk from [Palmerston North] to Ashurst ... to see a `dinkum go' through a crack [ie a good boxing match]. <i>(Oct 10, 1905,NZ Truth,17/3/2</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> ... in this `go' with a friend. <i>(1905,Bush Boys of NZ,16</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> To make, to commit

>  <b>GIFFORD</b> About an hour from home he leaps to the slidin' window [of the bus] and goes a technicolour yawn that MGM would have been proud to produce. <i>(1976,Loosehead Len's Bumper Thump Book,31</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> (transitive) to consume avidly, enjoy; (go a binder see binder).

>  <b>COOZE</b> How I could go a .... beer <i>(1944,Kiwis in the Pacific,12</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> I decided I'd go a bob dinner. [?= go to] <i>(1946,That Summer,124</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> I could go a good feed of eels just now. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,21</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> ... Those little freshwater crayfish up the Cobb tasted sweet. I could go one now. I would love one now. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,114</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> To find acceptable, to put up with; to stand

>  <b>SLATTER</b> `Can't go these [bar] stools. Sitting up here like Jacky. I like the old style.' <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,45</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i> To fight; to argue with; to attack verbally [ca1935 Havelock He got his rag out and went him.]

>  <b>GROVER</b> The nights he wasn't working she'd be going him for sitting around the house ... That was when he started hitting the booze. He'd go out with his mates and come home drunk ... <i>(1967,Another Man's Role,6</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a success

>  <b>JOSEPH</b> <i>(1962,Pound of Saffron, A,33</i>)

>  <b>CASEY</b> <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,217</i>)

### go a binder
 
 <i>phr.</i> to feel like, need (occas. eat) a meal (See [binder](b-bla.md#binder))

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Binder, go a</i>. To eat a meal: New Zealanders' (esp. tramps'): from before 1932. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,54</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> To eat a meal, esp. a satisfying one: New Zealand tramps' since ca1920; by 1930, low sl. Partridge, 1937. (Perhaps ex a meal consisting mainly of eggs.) <i>(1949,Dictionary of the Underworld,39</i>)

### go a roarer
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) (of a river) to be in high flood.

>  <b>MASTERS</b> Next morning the creek in front of the hut was going a roarer, making it useless my attempting getting the horses over. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,59</i>)

### go ahead
 
 <i>v.</i> .

>  <b>87</b> ... his opinion that Canterbury must `go ahead'. <i>(Dec 16, 1950,Journal of Edward Ward,1951</i>)

>  <b>REID</b> In front and upon the street line, if the township should `go ahead' and be surveyed, stands the store. <i>(1884,Rambles on the Golden Coast,135</i>)

>  ... open the proceedings by calling out `Go ahead!' <i>(1869,Punch or the Auckland Charivari,154</i>)

>  <b>LAPHAM</b> I signified my readiness to listen by the familiar if slangy, expression, `Now then, Pater, go ahead' <i>(1880,We Four,40</i>)

### go and fetch a bag of postholes
 
 <i>phr.</i> indicating an impossible task to a new chum or innocent, or to dismiss an importunate child. [ca1928 p.c. Auckland p.c. R. Mason `I heard of this as one of the standard jobs given to a new chum in some Auckland businesses' ca1935 p.c. Havelock (said to children)]

### go and have a roll
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [hill country farming] to relax, as saddle horses do on release of a saddle, roll on their backs.

>  pc with John Nation. <i>(1999,Personal Communication</i>)

>  <b>PETER</b> Go and have a roll: to unwind. <i>(2000,Peter Glossary,4</i>)



 
 <i>phr.</i> implying dismissal, disbelief, impatience; go away!; shut up; go to hell! [(obsolescent) ca1935 Havelock children]

### go around with
 
 <i>v.</i> go steady with

>  <b>McGILL</b> go around with keep steady company with a sexual partner, before or outside of marriage; eg `Tom's been going around with Jane for six years now. Still hasn't tied the knot.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,50</i>)

### go away/go away fat
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [of stock] be sent to freezing works as prime or fat. (See [go off fat](go-gra.md#go-off-fat))

>  My own bred cattle go away fat from March on. <i>(May 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,503</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> All wether lambs go away fat ... <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,7</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> The Romney wethers don't go away early as fat lambs but stay to be sold later. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,72</i>)

### go back
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [stock] to lose condition.

>  This contributor mustered the other day a number of yearlings and nearly all had a short, hacking cough such as lambs emit when "going back." <i>(Dec 13, 1918,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  When an animal shows signs of going back in condition owing to iron-hunger, give iron-ammonium citrate. neglected and gone back to fern and titree. <i>(Dec 20, 1928,NZ Journal of Agriculture,411</i>)

>  Ewe and cow condition good but some lambs starting to go back. <i>(Feb 23, 1978,NZ Farmer,9</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [land] to regress or revert to scrub.

>  .. about 2500 acres in grass, balance has been grass but neglected and gone back to fern and titree. <i>(Jul 15, 1940,NZ Journal of Agriculture,40</i>)

>  Only a short time ago the owners informed him that the places had gone back, that the pastures were suffering and the sheep were dying. <i>(Dec 1, 1928,NZ Farmer,1599</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> In 1945, another block of 365 acres of unimproved hilly land which had 'gone back' was purchased. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,69</i>)

### go bucket
 
 <i>phr.</i> travel economy class

>  Even when we're going bucket on Air Bechuanaland, we catch a sight of the koru tail at Heathrow and feel proud. <i>(Dec 7, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

### go bung
 
 <i>phr.</i> a) to become bankrupt, to fail in business. b) to fail, to become useless: `my car's gone bung', also noun gone-bung, a bankrupt. In NZ seems orig. applied to businesses or businessmen in the sense to go bankrupt, to fail; be ruined

>  <b>SCOTT</b> If Pirate goes under, Mills will go bung. <i>(1900,Tales of the Colonial Turf,14</i>)

>  <b>WICKHAM</b> The local Press is being run at a loss now ... and when the big dailies pour in - ... something must go `bung.' <i>(1888,Ramblings,21</i>)

>  <b>LLOYD</b> The contractor almost always goes `bung', as the Australian phrase has it. <i>(1900,Newest England,97</i>)

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> The merchant princes who have gone bung, and the geniuses who have gone bunger. <i>(1902,Land of the Lost,47</i>)

>  <i>bung, bong a. </i>sl. dead, bankrupt. -to go bung to die; to fail; to go bankrupt. [Abor.] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,2</i>)

>  <b>LAWSON</b> The Bank of New Zealand which someone had discovered was insolvent and would go bung unless the Government saved it. <i>(1951,Gold in Local Songs,169</i>)

>  <i>bung, to go (Phr.)</i> fail, become bankrupt [Poss. influenced by Abo. <i>bong</i>, dead]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),5</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> ... Dad had bought it cheap when Mr Brooks went bung, ... <i>(1978,Elver, The,27</i>)

>  If they don't, the company will go bung and the SPCA and Maf, among others, will be down on them like a pack of wolves, and they know it. <i>(May 18, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

>  Your Auckland men, look to mining as their only salvation from going "bung." <i>(Oct 22, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,21</i>)



 
 <i>phr.</i> in gen. use to become ruined, broken, worn out, etc. [ca1949 Wellington This torch battery's gone bung.]

>  `The state now guarantees the Bank,/ We guarantee the leather./ For honest boots that won't go bung,/ And stand all tear and weather,/ Go straight to D. Salmon.' <i>(1894,Waikato Times</i>) [NOTES: 1894 Advt in Waikato Times in 1964 Norris Settlers in Depression 227]

>  The man who has gone bankrupt for a large amount of splash ... all his schemes going bunger. <i>(Aug 12, 1905,NZ Truth,8/1/8</i>)

>  <b>LAWSON</b> Charleston might go bung some day, and then where would we be [of a mine, or mining town, =? `peter out'] <i>(1951,Gold in their Hearts,48</i>)

>  <b>HOGAN</b> In '69 the field went `bung',/ And the diggers moved on, broke./ <i>(1962,Billy-Can Ballads,31</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> `Crook valve. More expense.' `You mean it's gone bung?' <i>(1978,Glory and the Dream, The,8</i>)

>  I was hoping the springs would go bung. <i>(Mar 9, 1920,Hawke's Bay Herald,3</i>)

### go bush
 
 <i>phr.</i> drop out of society; become reclusive; spend time in the bush; disappear into the wilderness; go into hiding

>  Assuming he hasn't gone bush -- which was a roaring rumour in Christchurch on Saturday but curtly dismissed by Woodward on Sunday -- Henson will be in the test 22. <i>(Jun 28, 2005,Dominion Post,C16</i>)

>  After that Gordon "went bush". <i>(Sep 1998,North & South,60</i>)

>  Unfortunately the pig has a propensity for 'going bush' if left to his own resources and 'going bush' he has been ever since. <i>(Aug 15, 1960,NZ Journal of Agriculture,155</i>)

>  Ralph Te Kereama loved to go bush. For up to three months at a time. <i>(Apr 1998,Mana,No. 21 : 9</i>)

>  He went bush. Getting in touch with the rural life is essential for your health, he says. <i>(Oct 23, 2002,Capital Times,5</i>)

>  And for those who don't like the idea of donning tramping boots, there are other ways to "go bush". <i>(Nov 9, 2002,Dominion Post,F12</i>)

>  <b>David Geary</b> He'd gone bush by then - moving from hut to hut, living off tucker that trampers left behind. <i>(2008,Six Pack 3, The,116</i>)

>  One of this year's new All Blacks has "gone bush" - but not to dodge creditors or military service. <i>(Nov 23, 1955,NZ Truth,16</i>)

>  Kiwis are planning to go bush these summer holidays to make their anaemic New Zealand dollars go further, providing a boost for tent-makers and people who run camping grounds. <i>(Nov 20, 2000,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  [Hdg] Went Bush after Theft. <i>(Jun 20, 1934,NZ Truth,11</i>)

### go butcher's
 
 <i>phr.</i> (elliptical for go butchers' hook, rhyming slang for look, crook) to become angry (with), go off (at); to reprimand severely, scold

>  <b>CASEY</b> And that was either a nasty burst of static or old Ma Bower going butcher's. <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,212</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> I work flat to the boards, never even cough till ten, slogging me guts out to buy luxuries for the wife and she goes butchers because me home-brew stained her posh water set. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,150</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> You be late, Old Werry'll go butcher's. <i>(1969,Awatea,25, 98</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Gil went really butcher's at her to me for a minute. `Don't be a flaming galah, Ken,' he said. `For God's sake, listen. Do you want to get her up the flaming duff?' <i>(1973,NZ Jack,124</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>butchers</i> to be angry, often <i>go butchers</i>; short for `butcher's hook', rhyming slang for `go crook at' - English rhyming version a `look'; eg `That teacher's always going butchers.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,24</i>)

>  "I knew bloody well the old lady'd go butchers when she heard about me runnin' into naked sheilas up the bush." <i>(Mar 15, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),53</i>)

### go butcher's hook
 
 <i>phr.</i> (informal) to go crook (at); go off (at); become angry (with); tell off. [also go butcher's ca1910 at least W.H.B. Orsman] (See [also go butcher's](g-go.md#go-butchers))

>  <b>FRAME</b> I'll come back with a salmon or I'll go butcher's hook. <i>(1951,Lagoon, The,20</i>) [NOTES: (`Keel and Kool')]

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> `Mum'll go butchers' hooks at us if she finds out,' said Ted. <i>(1960,Ernie and the Rest of Us,119</i>)

>  <b>CASEY</b> The old man had come home full as a bull one night ... all his money gone. The old woman had gone butcher's hook and they'd got stuck into one another. <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,275</i>)

>  <i>butcher ... 3. go butcher's hook at</i> (someone) <i>NZ & Austral. sl.</i> go crook at, become angry with. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,96</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>CHERRILL</b> You should have heard him go off butcher's hook. <i>(1950,Sheep Farm,206</i>)

>  G.E.J.: Can any `Kia Ora Coo-ee-ite' dig up anything to beat this for the pure and unadulterated? A certain New Zealand Regiment, camped on the Jordan flats, recently came under the eagle eye of brother `Jacko', who immediately went `butcher's hook' or `ram's horn' and launched forth much frightfulness by lugging a 5.9 up on to one of the spare hills and chucking ironmongery promiscuously about the landscape. <i>(Aug 15, 1918,Kia-ora Coo-ee,Second Series 2/5,1981 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>Much New Zealand colloquialism is shared with Australia, e.g. just quietly</i> `between you and me', <i>the strength of</i> `the essence of', <i>come a stumer</i> `be disappointed, come a cropper', or <i>go butcher's hook(s)</i>, rhyming slang for `go crook, display anger' <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,177</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>To go crook</i> is to show anger or annoyance, to `sling off at'; and <i>to go butcher's hook</i> is presumably a development of this rhyming slang. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,90</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>phr.</i> to go bull-at-a-gate (at); bullock (at); work violently and unintelligently [ca1935 Havelock]

### go cronk
 
 <i>phr.</i> to go bad, go off; go off the rails, go crooked

>  ... and help save the country from going completely cronk. <i>(Aug 17, 1905,NZ Truth,9/6/3</i>)

>  There are plenty of girls named Selina about, which is not wonderful, but what is so is that so many of them go cronk. There are any number of prettily named petticoats that go wrong, but Selinas seem to take the bun. <i>(Nov 3, 1906,NZ Truth,72/5/8</i>)

### go crook (on)
 
 <i>phr.</i> to go bad (on); to become unpleasant (for); to play up (on).

>  <b>SMYTH</b> Got to have some cove massage me arms so's they won't go crook on me. <i>(1929,Girl from Mason Creek, The,149</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> `Pardon, It's gone crook on my digestion.' Berry doubled up. <i>(1937,Vintage Murder,165</i>)

>  On sick parade with a toe that looked as if it was going to go crook. <i>(Jul 19, 1943,2NZEF Times,5</i>)

### go crook on
 
 <i>phr.</i> ? to become angry with, to disapprove severely (the cit. may be <i>confusing</i> go crook on <i>with </i> go crook at).

>  <b>DRESCHFELT</b> It's a queer thing the way the law goes crook on a chap for a natural instinct like assault. <i>(1933,Broken Pattern,199</i>)

### go crook/go off crook (at)
 
 <i>phr.</i> to become angry (with)

>  <b>MILLER</b> ... a farmer arrived on the scene and went crook ... <i>(1939,Camps, Tramps and Trenches,143</i>) [NOTES: 1917 Sep 27 in 1939 Miller Camps, Tracks and Trenches 143]

>  Many of his friends went crook. <i>(Oct 3, 1917,NZEF Chronicles,86</i>)

>  ... the crowd merely took the man who went crook. <i>(1917,Kia Tupato Transport Magazine,15</i>)

>  ... the crowd merely took the man who went crook. <i>(1917,Kia Tupato Transport Magazine,15</i>)

>  GO CROOK. - To go on the crooked path.<i>Ibid.</i> 16 An' w'in yer finds that they's a war over 'ere yer goes crook about it. <i>Ibid.</i> 78 And many went exceeding <i>crook</i>, and cried amongst themselves, `Never come at that game, cobber.' <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,xiii</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> I sat up there for an hour before I though of cooeeing for Gus. I heard Gus cooee back ... He had been rambling about since dusk looking for me under the impression that I was bushed. His last `Cooee' brought him within a chain of me, and when he heard me answer he started to go crook. <i>(1927,Gus Tomlins,80,(ed Sturm) 1977 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Now don't say that about my tin plate or I'll go crook: He is out on his own and, in fact, he is shook on me. <i>(1935,Slang Today,287</i>) [NOTES: 1935 A.E. Strong WW1 scene in Partridge Slang Today 287]

>  <b>MARSH</b> ... see him when he goes crook ... <i>(1937,Vintage Murder,70</i>)

>  <b>McKINLAY</b> `I go crook?' Oh, that's simply New Zealandese for `Pourquoi s'en faire?' which is `Ich gebibble?' which is I should worry. <i>(1939,Ways & Byways,111</i>) [NOTES: 1939 McKinley Ways and Byways 111 [c1918, WW1]]

>  <b>BENNETT</b> <i>To go crook</i> is to show anger or annoyance, to `sling off at.' <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,90</i>)

>  <i>Crook, to go</i> - to give vent to anger. Also<i> fly off the handle, hit the roof. </i> <i>(1944,US War Dept. Short Guide to NZ,39</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> They'd all been going crook something awful about the cold and sitting still. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,116</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> ... Izzy went crook at her brother because her white frock was grass-stained. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,144,1976 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> Well, that was something to go crook about. <i>(1953,Till Human Voices Wake Us,9</i>)

>  ... said the Grim Dig ... All the best moaners work on the munga. Y' get three goes a day - and if you don't then you can really go crook. <i>(Jun 4, 1955,Upper Hutt Leader,5</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Annie was always going crook about it and finally she drove us out with shrill vehemence. <i>Ibid.</i> 80 The crowd went a bit crook about the penalties but nine-nil coulda been worse. <i>Ibid.</i> 129 `I shouldn't have been so spiteful. ... I'm sorry I went crook like that.' <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,14</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> `There wasn't no need for me to go crook like that. I'm just a bit jumpy about everything.' <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,42,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: `There wasn't no need for me to go crook like that. I'm just a bit jumpy about everything.']

>  <b>M,ASTERS</b> If you happened to whistle, sing or call out about the camp, old Harry would start cursing and going crook. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,165</i>)

>  `There wasn't any need for me to go crook like that.' <i>(1962,Landfall Country,148</i>) [NOTES: (Shadbolt ``After the Depression')]

>  <b>JOSEPH</b> `I've been relaxing and dad went crook about it.' <i>(1962,Pound of Saffron, A,34</i>)

>  <b>TEXIDOR</b> They have to send for the doctor when they're ill, and you'll see, if the fridge goes crook they'll send for me. <i>(1987,In Fifteen Minutes,152</i>) [NOTES: a1964 Texidor in 1987 In Fifteen Minutes 152]

>  <b>SARGESON</b> crook: <i>a crook business right through</i>, a thoroughly dishonest racket; <i>go crook,</i> (to) object or protest strongly; <i>looked pretty crook on it</i>, looked ill. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,298</i>)

>  <i>crook adj. (Sl.)</i> sick, bad, inferior;<i> (Phr.) to feel crook</i>, to feel ill; <i>go crook on</i>, become annoyed with, upbraid. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),6</i>) [NOTES: [Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 266; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 113]]

>  <b>DAVIN</b> The old man's always going crook because the thrushes and blackbirds roost there and come down and gobble up his blackcurrants. <i>(1975,Breathing Spaces,34</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> `Take the dog. ... The boss give it to her. Well, it goes crook and they get a vet and he reckons it's hopeless and it ought to be put out of its misery. So <i>she</i> goes crook. Screechin' and moanin', ...' <i>(1980,Photo-Finish,31</i>)

>  <i>Go crook:</i> complain or remonstrate <i>(1981,Auckland Savings Bank</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <i>crook ... 3 a. NZ & Austral</i>. ill, out of sorts (<i>John felt pretty crook</i>); (of thing) unsatisfactory, out of order <i>(things went crook from the beginning, the engine sounds crook to me). 4. go crook (at) NZ & Austral</i>. be angry (with), speak angrily (to). <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,176</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> ... the guards were very tolerant and only occasionally `went crook' when the van was cluttered with sleeping bags, packs and, sometimes, roaring primuses. <i>(1987,Rail,128</i>)

>  <b>DRESCHFELT</b> If Phoebe's gone crook at you she's had some reason for it. <i>(1933,Broken Pattern,197</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> ... they would go off crook at him. <i>(1950,Our Street,77</i>)

### go dog on
 
 <i>phr.</i> be keen on [1952 June 7 R. Mason Letter (Wellington) Dog: 1941 J.W. Butcher - Usually in negative. ca1941 Otago (usually in negative)]

### go double-fleecer
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to go missing, go bush, or leave one's familiar surroundings.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> To go double-fleecer - and the expression applies not just to sheep - means to go feral or go wild or go bush. Even the musterer's hacks go double-fleecer some mornings! A musterer himself, not sighted around the annual dog trials, can occasion the comment that he has gone double-fleecer. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,26</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> placer: a hermit sheep, one that has definitely gone double-fleecer. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,61</i>)

### go down
 
 <i>v.</i> (Of a gang-groupie), to submit to sexual intercourse (?or rape).

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> The first one [gang-groupie girl] went down OK and a few of the guys got stuck in; then someone tried to lay the other girl. <i>(1971,Bullshit and Jellybeans,34</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> It was a shock to find that my heifers commenced to go down in milk... <i>(1936,Follow the Call,35</i>)

### go fat
 
 <i>phr.</i> to become `fat lambs'

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Over the last few years the lambing has averaged over 120 per cent ... and half the surplus lambs go fat off the mothers at better than 30 pounds. See stick <i>(1973,Big Country,16,1977 ed.</i>)

### go flatting
 
 <i>phr.</i> (of young people) to go to live in shared accommodation (See [flat v](fin-fly.md#flat))

>  <b>McLauchlan ed.</b> We talked about varsity... Both of us, it turned out, had decided to go flatting for our second year. <i>(2000,Morrieson's Motel,143</i>)

### go for a skate
 
 <i>phr.</i> to slip or slide; fig. to go before a Court on a charge. [ca1930]

>  <b>CRUMP</b> You'd have gone for a skate if they'd caught you out. <i>(1962,One of Us,105</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> I don't want the cops here, yous guys'll go for a skate, for sure. Just blow <i>(1974,Maori Woman,99</i>)

>  Another apprentice went for a skate at Hawera when Ridgway got a month for crossing too soon. <i>(May 17, 1924,NZ Truth,10</i>)

>  In the second half of the big match, several Australian players illustrated perfectly the expression "To go for a skate." <i>(Sep 7, 1931,Evening Post,6</i>)

### go for one's/your life
 
 <i>phr.</i> to begin or do something vigorously; to run away quickly. (He was caught in the orchard and went for his life. [ca1938 Havelock Go on, run! Go for your life!]

>  <b>STRONACH</b> `Breakfast at half-past one, and as soon as we start to climb, go for your life.' <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,32</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> go for one's life give something your complete effort, or encouraged to do so ; eg `Look, she's alone now, you wimp. Here's your chance. Go for your life.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,51</i>)

### go for the catch
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [shearing] to attempt to take the last sheep in a catching pen before the bell. (See [catch](c-chi.md#catch))

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. the men would endeavour to select sheep which would enable them to just beat the gong and get an extra sheep. This was known as 'getting the catch'. <i>(1949,High Country Days,53</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Aiming to get a sheep before the bell is termed 'going for the catch.' [to get the catch] <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,95</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> .. in the last half of each year gangs are still hard at it in the Southern high country - chasing the ringer, ripping bootlaces, going for a catch and shoving an extra cobbler into the drummer's pen. <i>(1983,Other Side of Farming, The,ii 2</i>)

### go for the doctor
 
 <i>phr.</i> to go all out for the main chance; to make a strong attempt. ?also a call or card in the West Coast card game, forty-fives.

>  <b>McGILL</b> Old Calamity was an early one-man band of fiddle, horn, cymbals and kettledrums. Gambling was poker or 45s, a diggers' card game from California involving legs, queer suits and Maggie, cries of `Get Jinx!' and `Go for the Doctor!' <i>(1980,G'day Country,127</i>)

>  Once again Mullet `went for the doctor' in the ninth round and this time a right-cross put Hall down for another count of eight. <i>(Sep 24, 1952,Standard, The,4</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Anyway Jim knew what he had to beat and `he went for the doctor' and turned in a winning run. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,112</i>)

>  <b>McCARTHY</b> In a book I wrote after the [1945] tour I had this to say ...: `This day the Kiwi forwards went for the doctor'. In scrums and rucks they were supreme <i>(1973,Listen...!,10</i>)

>  <b>A.P. THOMSON</b> GO FOR THE DOCTOR - go all out, take every risk, ? go for the king hit, supreme effort. <i>(Apr 21, 1979,Personal Communication</i>)

>  [On Roger Douglas's budget] He's going for the doctor and apparently doing it quite well. <i>(Jun 19, 1987,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: Dick Griffin National Radio Morning Report]

>  <b>McGILL</b> go for the doctor again your supreme effort, or betting all your money on one race; the phrase comes from a card game called `forty-fives', popular on the West Coast from goldmining times, of Irish origin; author's Ghost Towns of New Zealand: `Gambling was poker or 45s, a diggers' game ... involving legs, queer suits and Maggie, cries of `Get jinx!' and `Go for the doctor!'' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,51</i>)

### go for the lick of one's life
 
 <i>phr.</i> depart at top speed; work at a forced pace

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> Go for the lick of your life down the lane <i>(1944,Troop Target,46</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> <i>(1946,That Summer,84</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> lick: going for the lick of our lives, working at a forced pace. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,301</i>) [NOTES: Pearson Glossary]

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,137</i>) [NOTES: quoting Sargeson That Summer]

### go girl
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  Have the All Blacks gone girl on us? <i>(Nov 10, 1999,Evening Post,6</i>)

### go halvers
 
 <i>phr.</i> [Cf. halfer.]

>  <b>CHRISTIAN SHIRRES</b> At Oamaru, I spoke to Florrie Sutherland, a typical colonial little girl - she had two pounds to spend and so had Ronald. They made it by skinning one thousand rabbits between them. They went `halvers'. They were down for their winter holidays. <i>(Jun 1888,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1888 June Diary in 1980 Neave The Land of Munros 53]

### go Home
 
 <i>phr.</i> to return to, or visit, England, the British Isles

>  <b>LUSH</b> Mr. Abraham said: `I suppose you know we are going almost immediately to England?' ... He told me his journey via Suez would cost him 400 [pounds] ... So `going home' is rather expensive. <i>(Jun 12, 1857,Auckland Journals,188,1971 ed.</i>)

>  I believe he and his wife will go home for a time <i>(1862,Letters,225</i>) [NOTES: Revans to Chapman]

>  <b>HINGSTON</b> ] .. colonists and emigrants are always going to go somewhere or other. We know several who have been `going home' as they call it, for ten years past. <i>(1879,Australian Abroad, The,295</i>)

>  I have arranged for wife and self to go home ... via Australia, ... New York, Liverpool. <i>(Jan 15, 1907,Chudleigh Diary,438,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> `I want to go Home, Mother. To London. To Paris. I must go Home, Mother. ...' <i>(1929,Riven,41</i>)

### go home
 
<b>2</b> <i>phr.</i> become worn

>  <b>BAKER</b> When an article of clothes, etc., becomes worn and ceases to be of service it is said colloquially to have gone home. This use is probably derived from the fact that many New Zealanders go Home - visit Britain - when they are old. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,43</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Home, have gone. `When an article of clothing, etc., causes to be of service it is said ... to have gone home ... from the fact that many New Zealanders go Home - visit Britain - when they are old,' B., 1941: New Zealand: C. 20. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang,1077</i>)
